Semiclassical analysis of a nonlocal boundary value problem related to magnitude
Heiko Gimperlein, Magnus Goffeng, Nikoletta Louca

Abstract
. We study a Dirichlet boundary problem related to the fractional Laplacian in a manifold. Its variational formulation arises in the study of magnitude, an invariant of compact metric spaces given by the reciprocal of the ground state energy. Using recent techniques developed for pseudodifferential boundary problems we discuss the structure of the solution operator and resulting properties of the magnitude. In a semiclassical limit we obtain an asymptotic expansion of the magnitude in terms of curvature invariants of the manifold and the boundary, similar to the invariants arising in short-time expansions for heat kernels.



1.
Introduction

The analysis of boundary problems for nonlocal operators has attracted much interest in recent years, including Dirichlet and Neumann problems for fractional Laplacians. In this article we initiate the semiclassical analysis of related boundary problems motivated by applications to the Leinster-Willerton conjecture for the magnitude invariant of compact metric spaces.

To be specific, we consider the integral equation with parameter
R>0

(1)

\int_{X}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}u(y)\mathrm{d}y=f(x).

Here
(X,\mathrm{d})
is a compact metric space, and we focus on when
X
is a manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
is a distance function satisfying additional regularity assumptions. Already when
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the unit disc, close to nothing was known for the solutions to (
1
). We shall prove in this paper that for
X
a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary, Equation (
1
) is well posed for
f
in the Sobolev space
\overline{H}^{(n+1)/2}(X)
and admits a unique solution
u_{R}\in\dot{H}^{-(n+1)/2}(X)
for sufficiently large
R\gg 0
(for notation, see page
4
). We relate the integral equation (
1
) to a pseudodifferential boundary value problem which is elliptic with parameter.

Our main results concern structural properties of solutions to Equation (
1
) such as asymptotic behavior as
R\to\infty
and meromorphic extensions in the parameter
R
to sectors
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
. The methods for pseudodifferential boundary value problems that we use date far back, see the work of Gregory Eskin
[
9
]
and Lars Hörmander
[
23
]
, but have in recent years seen much development in work of Gerd Grubb
[
16
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
.

The solution to (
1
) for the right hand side
f=1
enters in the so called magnitude function of
(X,\mathrm{d})
, studied extensively in for instance
[
2
,
11
,
12
,
27
,
28
,
30
,
33
,
34
,
44
,
45
]
. The empirical properties of the solution to (
1
) have recently found applications in data science, leading to precise conjectures for its structural properties
[
6
,
7
]
.

The case
f=1
can be considered as a minimizing problem relating to the ground state energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d}):=

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\int_{X}\int_{X}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(y):\;\mu\;\mbox{a signed Borel measure with $\mu(X)=1$}\right\}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\sum_{x,y\in X}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}c(x)c(y):\;\mbox{$c:X\to\mathbb{R}$ has finite support and $\sum_{x\in X}c(x)=1$}\right\}.

More precisely, if
R
is such that
(X,R\mathrm{d})
is positive definite (i.e. the matrix
(\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
is positive definite for any finite
F\subseteq X
), then by
[
34
]
a solution
u_{R}
to Equation (
1
) with
f=1
satisfies

\int_{X}u_{R}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})}.

Let us digest on the problem of finding
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
and studying its semiclassical limit, as it has been broadly studied in various mathematical communities. The ground state energy
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
is that of a signed distribution of finitely many particles on
X
where a particle in
x
interacts with a particle in
y
under the potential
\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}
. As such, the scaling parameter
R>0
should be thought of as an order parameter with
R\to\infty
corresponding to a semiclassical limit. The non-locality of Equation (
1
) and the ground state energy
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
makes the problem of explicit computation an impossibility, however in the semiclassical limit the problem localizes and is asymptotically described in terms of geometric invariants.
Related problems concerning ground state energies with nonlocal interaction potentials arise in the mean field description of interacting particle systems, such as
[
10
]
. Specifically for log gases the dependence of the ground state energy on the geometry has been of interest
[
47
]
. In complex geometry, Berman has studied similar minimization problems from which geometric structures emerged
[
3
,
4
]
. Related operators also appear in image processing
[
1
]
.

The integral equation (
1
) is, as mentioned above, related to magnitude – an invariant that has been extensively studied since it was introduced by Tom Leinster
[
27
]
. We presume no prerequisites from the reader on magnitude in this paper, but for the convenience of the reader we summarize the implications to magnitude here and expand on this relation in the follow up paper
[
13
]
. From its category-theoretic origin, magnitude has found unexpected applications from algebraic topology
[
15
,
29
,
41
]
and applied category theory
[
8
,
36
]
to data science
[
6
,
7
]
and mathematical biology
[
26
]
.

For a metric space
(X,\mathrm{d})
this invariant leads to a function
\mathcal{M}_{X}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. When the metric space
(X,R\mathrm{d})
is positive definite, and in particular for compact sets
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

[
33
]
,
\mathcal{M}_{X}(R)
is defined as
\mathcal{M}_{X}(R)=\int_{X}u_{R}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, where
u_{R}
satisfies the magnitude equation

(2)

\int_{X}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}u(y)\mathrm{d}y=1.

The work
[
34
]
provides an abstract Hilbert space framework in which to pose this equation.

In the case of compact sets
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, Meckes
[
34
]
gives an interpretation of the magnitude in potential theoretic terms, as a generalized capacity:

(3)

\mathcal{M}_{X}(R)=\frac{1}{Rn!\omega_{n}}\inf\left\{\|(R^{2}+\Delta)^{(n+1)/4}h_{R}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}:h_{R}\in H^{(n+1)/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ h_{R}=1\ \text{on}\ X\right\}\ .

Here
\omega_{n}
denotes the volume of the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The minimizer of (
3
) is attained at a function
h_{R}\in H^{(n+1)/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
solving the non-local exterior problem

(4)

\begin{cases}(R^{2}+\Delta)^{(n+1)/2}h_{R}=0,\;&\mbox{in $\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus X$},\\
h_{R}=1,\;&\mbox{in $X$}.\end{cases}

If
n
is odd, this was studied as a boundary value problem for the five-dimensional unit ball in
[
2
]
, and extended to odd-dimensional unit balls in
[
46
]
. Few explicit computations of magnitude are known outside the realm of compact domains in odd-dimensional Euclidean space, and even there the state-of-the art
[
11
,
12
]
can only provide asymptotic results in the semiclassical limit and ensure existence of meromorphic extensions. In particular, nothing was previously known about magnitude even in such a simple case as the unit disk
X=B_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We provide a framework for a refined analysis and explicit computations for solutions to Equation (
1
) when
X
is a smooth,
n
-dimensional, compact manifold with boundary, independent of the parity of
n
. The framework relies on recent advances for pseudodifferential boundary problems and initiates their semiclassical analysis. We work under certain regularity assumptions on the distance function
\mathrm{d}
, firstly that its square is
regular at the diagonal
(see Definition
2.2
) and secondly that it has
property (MR)
(see Definition
3.3
and
4.2
). Our first assumption ensures that the distance function behaves to leading term as a Euclidean distance, and is satisfied by any geodesic distance function or a pullback thereof under an embedding. The first assumption ensures that the diagonal behavior in Equation (
1
) is governed by a pseudodifferential operator of order
-n-1
which is elliptic with parameter. Our second assumption – property (MR) – is a technical condition to ensure that the off-diagonal behavior in Equation (
1
) is negligible. Property (MR) is satisfied for subspace distances in manifolds whose distance functions squared are smooth, but it in fact fails for higher dimensional tori and real projective spaces.

The reader can note that there is an extended arXiv version of this paper
[
14
]
containing more details and overview.

1.1.
Main results

Let us summarize the main results of this paper. The results all circle around the family of integral operators

\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)u(x):=\frac{1}{R}\int_{X}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}u(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}.

Here
X
is a compact manifold with boundary equipped with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
and a volume density
\mathrm{d}y
. We assume that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth in a small neighborhood of the diagonal
x=y
and there in local coordinates admits a Taylor expansion (for any
N>0
)

(5)

\mathrm{d}(x,y)^{2}=H_{\mathrm{d}^{2},x}(v)+\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}C^{j}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}(x;v)+O(|v|^{N+1}).

Here
v=x-y
, and where
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is a Riemannian metric on
X
and
C^{j}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
in local coordinates is a symmetric
j
-form in
v
. This condition can be summarized in the terminology that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal
, see Definition
2.2
and for more details on the Taylor expansion, see Equation (
9
). We fix a function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(X\times X)
such that
\chi=1
on a neighborhood of the diagonal
x=y
and
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on the support of
\chi
. The localization of
\mathcal{Z}
to near the diagonal is the integral operator

Q_{X}(R)u(x):=\frac{1}{R}\int_{X}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}u(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}.

We remark that if
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on all of
X\times X
, e.g. for a domain or a submanifold in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the induced metric, it holds that
\mathcal{Z}_{X}-Q_{X}
is smoothing with parameter on any sector
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{+}
with opening angle
<\pi/2
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal (see Definition
2.2
). The family of integral operators
Q_{X}
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter
R\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
of order
-n-1
, and its principal symbol is

\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(Q_{X})(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2},

where
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is the dual metric to
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
from the Taylor expansion (
5
). The properties of
Q_{X}
can be summarized as follows:

(1)

In each coordinate chart, the full symbol of
Q_{X}
can be computed by an iterative scheme as in Theorem
2.9
.

(2)

There exists an
R_{0}
such that

Q_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)\to\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X),

is invertible for
\mathrm{Re}(R)>R_{0}
and
\mathrm{arg}(R)<\pi/(n+1)
. Here
\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)
, respectively
\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)
, denote the Sobolev spaces of supported, respectively extendable distributions in
X
(see Section
4
).

(3)

If
\partial X=\emptyset
, then
Q_{X}(R)^{-1}
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator of order
n+1
whenever it exists. The full symbol of
Q_{X}^{-1}
can be computed by an iterative scheme as in Theorem
2.20
.

Moreover, if
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth then all the properties above hold also for
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
.

Theorem
1.1
is found in the bulk of the text as follows. The first statement and item (1) is found in Theorem
2.9
. Item (2) is proven in Theorem
4.1
, see also Corollary
2.21
of Theorem
2.22
for the simpler case of
\partial X=\emptyset
. Item (3) follows from Theorem
2.20
and Corollary
2.21
.

The operator
Q_{X}
is generally more well behaved than
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
; the off-diagonal singularities of
\mathrm{d}
can create problems in considering
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
as a map between Sobolev spaces. For examples of such phenomena, see Subsection
3.4
. We impose one of two conditions on
\mathrm{d}
; property (MR) and property (SMR) respectively to ensure that
Q_{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
share common functional analytic features as operators between Sobolev space. The precise definition of property (MR) and property (SMR) may be found in Definition
3.3
(for
\partial X=\emptyset
) and Definition
4.2
(for
\partial X\neq\emptyset
). We note that property (MR) and property (SMR) hold on any sector
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
as soon as
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on all of
X\times X
, e.g. for a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or more generally for a compact submanifold with boundary in a manifold with
\mathrm{d}^{2}
smooth.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary and let
\mathrm{d}
be a distance function such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal (see Definition
2.2
). The family of operators

Q_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)\to\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X),\;R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\},

is a holomorphic family of Fredholm operators that are invertible on a sector. The inverse
Q_{X}(R)^{-1}:\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)
,
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, is a meromorphic family of Fredholm operators.

If
\mathrm{d}
satisfies property (SMR) on a sector
\Gamma
, then also

\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)\to\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X),\;R\in\Gamma,

is a holomorphic family of Fredholm operators whose inverse family
\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)^{-1}:\bar{H}^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(X)
,
R\in\Gamma
,
is a meromorphic family of Fredholm operators.

For the purposes described above, we are interested in precise asymptotic information about solutions to
\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)u=f
. We describe the inverse operator
\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{-1}
via Wiener-Hopf factorizations.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. For some
R_{0}\geq 0
and any
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
, we can write

Q_{X}^{-1}=\chi_{1}A\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}+S,

where
A
is a pseudodifferential parametrix of
Q_{X}
,
\chi_{1},\chi_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(X^{\circ})
, and
\chi_{2},\chi_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(X)
are functions supported in a collar neighborhood
U_{0}
of
\partial X
in
X
such that

\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\chi_{j}^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{j})}=1,\;j=1,2,

\varphi:\partial X\times[0,1)\to U_{0}
is a collar identification, and the operators
S
,
W_{-}
and
W_{+}
satisfying the following as
R\to\infty
:

(1)

S:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
is a continuous operator with
\|S\|_{\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

(2)

W_{+}:L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
is the properly supported pseudodifferential operator of mixed-regularity
(\mu,0)
from Definition
5.18
which is invertible for large
R>0
and in local coordinates has an asymptotic expansion modulo
S^{\mu,-\infty}
as in Lemma
5.21
and preserves support in
\partial X\times[0,\infty)\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}+C^{\infty}_{c}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
with
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
, it holds that
\|\chi W_{+}\chi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to H^{-\mu}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

(3)

W_{-}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
is the properly supported pseudodifferential operator of mixed-regularity
(\mu,0)
from Definition
5.18
which is invertible for large
R>0
and in local coordinates has an asymptotic expansion modulo
S^{\mu,-\infty}
as in Lemma
5.21
and preserves support in
\partial X\times(-\infty,0]\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}+C^{\infty}_{c}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})
with
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
, it holds that
\|\chi W_{-}\chi^{\prime}\|_{H^{\mu}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

Theorem
1.3
is stated as Theorem
5.22
in the body of the text. A key feature of the construction in Theorem
1.3
is that it provides us with a method to compute the symbolic structure of the inverse
Q_{X}^{-1}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. In the sector
\mathrm{Re}(R)>R_{0}
and
\mathrm{arg}(R)<\pi/(n+1)
, we have a complete asymptotic expansion

\langle Q(R)^{-1}1_{X},1_{X}\rangle\sim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{n-k}+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty$ in $\Gamma$},

where the coefficients
c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})
are given as

c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})=\int_{X}a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial X}B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}(x)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

where

(1)

a_{k,0}(\cdot,1)\in C^{\infty}(X)
is an invariant polynomial in the entries of the Taylor expansion (
5
) as described in Theorem
2.26
and can be computed inductively using Lemma
2.24
, with
a_{k,0}=0
if
k
is odd; and

(2)

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}\in C^{\infty}(\partial X)
is an invariant polynomial in the entries of the Taylor coefficients of
\mathrm{d}^{2}
at the diagonal in
X
near
\partial X
as described in Proposition
6.9
and can be inductively computed using Lemma
5.19
.

In particular, we have that

\displaystyle c_{0}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{vol}(X)}{n!\omega_{n}},\quad c_{1}(X,\mathrm{d})=\frac{(n+1)\mathrm{vol}(\partial X)}{2n!\omega_{n}},

\displaystyle c_{2}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{6\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{X}s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{(n-1)(n+1)^{2}}{8\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{\partial X}H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

where the scalar curvature
s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is defined as in Theorem
6.1
and the mean curvature
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
of the distance function is defined as in Theorem
6.13
.

Theorem
1.4
is stated as Theorem
6.13
in the body of the text.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. Write
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
for the ground state energy.

(1)

If
\mathrm{d}
has property (MR) on a sector
\Gamma
, the ground state energy function
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
is a well defined meromorphic function of
R\in\Gamma
.

(2)

If
\mathrm{d}
has property (SMR) on a sector
\Gamma
, the ground state energy function
\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})
has the complete semiclassical asymptotic expansion

\mathcal{E}(R;X,\mathrm{d})\sim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{-n-k}+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty$ in $\Gamma$},

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{n!\omega_{n}}{\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)},\quad\varepsilon_{1}(X,\mathrm{d})=-\frac{(n+1)\mathrm{vol}_{n-1}(X)}{2\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{(n+1)^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{n-1}(X)^{2}}{4\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)^{2}}-\frac{n!\omega_{n}c_{2}(X,\mathrm{d})}{\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)},

and more generally

\varepsilon_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})=p_{k,n}\left(\frac{c_{1}(X,\mathrm{d})}{\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)},\ldots,\frac{c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})}{\mathrm{vol}_{n}(X)}\right),

for a universal polynomial
p_{k,n}
of total degree
k
(where each
c_{j}(X,\mathrm{d})
is declared to be of degree
j
).

1.2.
Notational conventions

To avoid confusion, we will use the terms Riemannian metrics and distance function to separate the notions of metrics that appear in Riemannian geometry and metric geometry, respectively.

The Fourier transform on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
\mathcal{F}
, where we use the convention

\mathcal{F}f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-ix\cdot\xi}f(x)\mathrm{d}x

for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We further write

D_{x}=-i\frac{\partial}{\partial x}.

For
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, we write
|\alpha|=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}
,
D^{\alpha}_{x}=D_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots D_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}
and
x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}
. In this convention, for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{F}(D_{x}^{\alpha}f)(\xi)=\xi^{\alpha}\mathcal{F}f(\xi)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{F}(x^{\alpha}f)(\xi)=(-D_{\xi})^{\alpha}\mathcal{F}f(\xi),

and the product of pseudodifferential symbols is up to smoothing operators defined from a symbol of the form

p\#q\sim\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}pD_{x}^{\alpha}q=\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}D_{\xi}^{\alpha}p\partial_{x}^{\alpha}q,

where
\alpha!=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}!
.

We write
M
for a manifold and
X
for a compact manifold with boundary, or occasionally a general compact metric space. We let
n
denote the dimension of
M
or
X
and use the notation

\textstyle{\mu:=\frac{n+1}{2}}.

We write
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}:=\{(x,x):x\in M\}
for the diagonal in
M\times M
. If
(X,\mathrm{d})
is a compact metric space such that the matrix
(\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
is positive definite for any finite subset
F\subseteq X
, we say that
(X,\mathrm{d})
is positive definite. If
(X,R\mathrm{d})
is positive definite for all
R>0
, we say that
(X,\mathrm{d})
is stably positive definite. This terminology follows
[
33
]
.

For a manifold
M
, we write
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
for the Fréchet space of compactly supported smooth functions and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
for its topological dual – the distributions on
M
. A domain
X\subseteq M
is a subset which coincides with the closure of its interior points. If
X
is a compact manifold with boundary, it can always be embedded as a domain in a manifold
M
and we write
C^{\infty}(X)\subseteq C(X)
for the restrictions to
X
of elements in
C^{\infty}(M)
.

A sector
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is a conical subset, i.e.,
\lambda\Gamma\subset\Gamma
for all
\lambda>1
. Standard examples we use throughout the paper are
\mathbb{C}_{+}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\mathrm{Re}(z)>0\}
and

\Gamma_{\alpha}(R_{0}):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\mathrm{Arg}(z)|<\alpha,\mathrm{Re}(z)>R_{0}\}.

If we make a claim concerning
R\to+\infty
, it is implicitly assumed to be a limit along the real line. We also note that for sectors
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{+}
of opening angle
\alpha<\pi/2
, there is a
C_{\alpha}>0
with
C_{\alpha}^{-1}|R|\leq\mathrm{Re}(R)\leq C_{\alpha}|R|
.

For two Banach spaces
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, we write
\mathbb{B}(V_{1},V_{2})
for the space of bounded operators
V_{1}\to V_{2}
and
\mathbb{K}(V_{1},V_{2})
for the space of compact operators
V_{1}\to V_{2}
. Both form Banach spaces in the norm topology.

We write
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}
for the set of natural numbers. The closed positive, respectively negative half-spaces are denoted by
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\pm}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\pm x_{n}\geq 0\}
.

1.3.
Acknowledgments

We are most grateful to the referees for their careful reading that helped us improve the paper. We thank Tony Carbery, Daniel Grieser, Gerd Grubb, Tom Leinster, Rafe Mazzeo, Mark Meckes, Niels Martin Møller, Grigori Rozenblum, Jan-Philip Solovej and Simon Willerton for fruitful and encouraging discussions.

MG was supported by the Swedish Research Council Grant VR 2018-0350, NL by The Maxwell Institute Graduate School in Analysis and its Applications, a Centre for Doctoral Training funded by the UK Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (Grant EP/L016508/01), the Scottish Funding Council, Heriot-Watt University and the University of Edinburgh.

2.
The symbolic structure near the diagonal

To better understand the operator
\mathcal{Z}
we first consider the case of a manifold
M
. This analysis describes compact manifolds (see Subsection
2.4
and Section
3
) and we carry it over to the interior of a compact manifold with boundary below in Section
4
and
5
. We formulate our results in terms of the operator

(6)

\mathcal{Z}(R)f(x):=\frac{1}{R}\int_{M}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,

whose dependence on
R\neq 0
is holomorphic under suitable assumptions studied in Section
3
below. Here we are implicitly using a volume density on
M
, and the operator depends on this choice. We shall later fix a certain choice adapted to the distance function. We shall specify the domain and codomain of this operator more precisely later on. For now, we can consider
\mathcal{Z}
an operator
C^{\infty}_{c}(M)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
by setting

\langle\mathcal{Z}\varphi,\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{R}\int_{M\times M}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}\varphi(y)\psi(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,\quad\mbox{for}\quad\varphi,\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(M).

2.1.
On a class of pseudodifferential operators with parameter

We pick a function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M\times M)
such that
\chi=1
near
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
and is supported in a small neighborhood of
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
. The precise choice of
\chi
will not play an important role, but we shall later specify conditions on its support. The operator
\mathcal{Z}
decomposes as

(7)

\mathcal{Z}=Q+L,\quad\mbox{where}\quad Q(R)f(x):=\frac{1}{R}\int_{M}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}f(y)\mathrm{d}y.

We call the operator
Q
the
localization of
\mathcal{Z}
near the diagonal
. In this section we focus our attention to
Q
. Distance functions might be non-smooth away from the diagonal despite being quite regular at the diagonal and this off-diagonal behavior of the distance function dictates whether or not
L
is negligible. The remainder
L
will be studied further in Section
3
.

This subsection is devoted to proving that the localized part
Q
of
\mathcal{Z}
is a parameter-dependent pseudodifferential operator, see Appendix
A
. We will treat a slightly more general class of operators than
Q
. Consider a family of operators that take the form

(8)

Q_{G,\chi}(R)f(x):=\frac{1}{|R|_{\rm Mc}}\int_{M}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-|R|_{\rm Mc}\sqrt{G(x,y)}}f(y)\mathrm{d}V(y).

Here we have written

|R|_{\rm Mc}:=\begin{cases}R,\;&\mathrm{Re}(R)>0,\\
-R,\;&\mathrm{Re}(R)<0,\end{cases}

for the McIntosh modulus which extends the absolute value to a holomorphic function in
\mathbb{C}\setminus i\mathbb{R}
. We shall mainly be concerned with the cases
R\in\mathbb{R}
and
R\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
. The cut-off function
\chi
is as above with the additional constraint that
G
is smooth on
\mathrm{supp}(\chi)
. The function
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
should be
regular at the diagonal
as made precise in Definition
2.2
below: to define this notion we first introduce further terminology. Note that
T(M\times M)=p_{1}^{*}TM\oplus p_{2}^{*}TM
where
p_{j}:M\times M\to M
,
j=1,2
, denotes the projection onto the
j
:th factor. Over the diagonal
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
, the map
Dp_{1}\oplus Dp_{2}:T(M\times M)|_{\mathrm{Diag}_{M}}\to TM\oplus TM
is an isomorphism. We define the transversal tangent bundle to the diagonal to be

T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}:=\ker(Dp_{1}+Dp_{2}:T(M\times M)|_{\mathrm{Diag}_{M}}\to TM)\subseteq T(M\times M)|_{\mathrm{Diag}_{M}}.

The restriction
Dp_{1}|:T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\to TM
is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.1
.

For a smooth function
G
defined in a neighborhood of
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
, we define the transversal Hessian
H_{G}
as the quadratic form on
T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
obtained from restricting the Hessian of
G
over the diagonal to the transversal tangent bundle.

Definition 2.2
.

A function
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
is said to be
regular at the diagonal
if there is a tubular neighborhood
U
of the diagonal
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
such that
G|_{U}\in C^{\infty}(U)
is a smooth function satisfying that

•

G|_{U}
and
\mathrm{d}G|_{U}
vanish on
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\subseteq U
;

•

G(x)>0
for
x\in U\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
; and

•

the transversal Hessian
H_{G}
is positive definite in all points of
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
.

Remark 2.3
.

The prototypical example of a function
G
regular at the diagonal is
G=\mathrm{d}^{2}
for suitable distance functions
\mathrm{d}
. The function
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal for the Euclidean distance, or when
\mathrm{d}
is the geodesic distance on a Riemannian manifold (see Example
2.17
below) or more generally a distance function induced from pulling back a geodesic distance along an embedding of
M
(see Example
2.16
below for an example).

To show that
Q_{G,\chi}(R)
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter, and to describe its full symbol, we shall use a slight detour. The basic idea used in computing the full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}(R)
is to do an inverse Fourier transform in
R
, and then Fourier transform all conormal variables. When we Fourier transform in the
R
-variable the Schwartz kernel – depending on
(x,y,R)
– transforms to a conormal distribution on
U\times\mathbb{R}
(conormal to
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\}
) – depending on
(x,y,\eta)
– that we then Fourier transform in all the transversal directions
(v,\eta)
where
v=x-y
, thus producing the full symbol depending on
(x,\xi,R)
. To compute the Fourier transform in the
R
-direction, we use the following elementary lemma.

Proposition 2.4
.

For a parameter
a\geq 0
, and
F_{a}(R):=\mathrm{F.P.}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-a|R|}}{|R|}
, we have that

\mathcal{F}F_{a}(\eta)=-\log(\eta^{2}+a^{2})+\log(2)-2\gamma,

where
\gamma
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

Proof.

By taking a derivative in the parameter
a
and using that the Fourier transform of
R\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-a|R|}
is
2a(\eta^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}
, we see that

\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\mathcal{F}F_{a}(\eta)=-2a(\eta^{2}+a^{2})^{-1}.

As such,
\mathcal{F}F_{a}(\eta)=-\log(\eta^{2}+a^{2})+c_{0}(\eta)
for some tempered distribution
c_{0}
. Setting
a=0
and using Proposition
A.1
, we see that
c_{0}
is a constant. By Proposition
A.1
we have that

c_{0}=\beta_{0,1}=2\log(2)+\frac{1}{2}\psi(1/2)-\gamma=\log(2)-2\gamma.

∎

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
be a function which is regular at the diagonal, see Definition
2.2
. For a neighborhood
U
of
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
on which
G
is smooth, define
\tilde{G}\in C^{\infty}(U\times\mathbb{R})
by

\tilde{G}(x,y,\eta):=\eta^{2}+G(x,y),

and the conormal distribution
\log(\tilde{G})\in I^{-n-1}(U\times\mathbb{R};\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\})
as in Proposition
A.2
. Then there exists a canonical metric
g_{G}
on
T^{*}M
such that

\sigma_{-n-1}(\log(\tilde{G}))(x,\xi,R)=-2\pi n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}.

The canonical metric
g_{G}
is dual to the transversal Hessian of
G
under the isomorphism
Dp_{1}|:T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\to TM
.

In the following, unless specified otherwise we shall consider
M
endowed with the Riemannian metric
g_{G}
. This allows, in particular, to define a Laplace operator on
M
.

Proof.

Since
G
is regular at the diagonal, the function
\tilde{G}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
A.2
and the result follows therefrom.
∎

To compute the full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}
we use a Taylor expansion in the direction transversal to the diagonal. Consider a function
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
which is regular at the diagonal, see Definition
2.2
. Consider a coordinate chart
U_{0}\subseteq M
. The coordinates on
U_{0}
induce coordinates
(x,y)
on
U_{0}\times U_{0}
and we can identify a neighborhood of
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\cap(U_{0}\times U_{0})
with a neighborhood of the zero section in
T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}|_{U_{0}}
via the map
(x,y)\mapsto(x,x-y)
. If the coordinate chart
U_{0}
on
M
satisfies that
G
is smooth on
U_{0}\times U_{0}
, Taylor’s theorem implies that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
we can on
U_{0}\times U_{0}
write

(9)

G(x,y)=H_{G,x}(x-y)+\sum_{j=3}^{N}C_{G}^{(j)}(x;x-y)+r_{N}(x,x-y),

for
|x-y|
small enough, where
r_{N}
is a smooth function with
r_{N}(x,v)=O(|v|^{N+1})
as
v\to 0
,
H_{G}
is the transversal Hessian of
G
, and
C_{G}^{(j)}:U_{0}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{j}(T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}|_{U_{0}})
takes values in the symmetric
j
-forms on the transversal tangent bundle
T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}|_{U_{0}}
. A short computation shows that
H_{G}
indeed is a Riemannian metric on
M
under the isomorphism
Dp_{1}|:T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\to TM
. However, each
C_{G}^{(j)}
depends on the choice of coordinates, we nevertheless suppress this dependence in the notation.

Since there is a canonical isomorphism
T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}|_{U_{0}}\cong TM|_{U_{0}}
, the symmetric
j
-form
C_{G}^{(j)}:U_{0}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{j}(T_{\rm tra}\mathrm{Diag}_{M}|_{U_{0}})
appearing in the Taylor expansion (
9
) of
G
defines a
j
:th order differential operator

C^{(j)}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi}):C^{\infty}(T^{*}M|_{U_{0}})\to C^{\infty}(T^{*}M|_{U_{0}}),

obtained by quantizing the coordinate functions, i.e.
C^{(j)}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi})
acts as multiplication operators by
C^{(j)}_{G}(x,v)
under the fiberwise inverse Fourier transform (in the
v
-direction). For a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and a multiindex
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}
, we can define a differential operator
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi})
on
T^{*}M|_{U_{0}}
by

C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi}):=\prod_{l=1}^{k}C^{(\gamma_{l})}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi}).

Since each
C^{(\gamma_{l})}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi})
acts as multiplication operators under the inverse Fourier transform, the differential operators
C^{(\gamma_{l})}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi})
,
l=1,\ldots,k
, commute. The order of
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,-D_{\xi})
is
|\gamma|:=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\gamma_{l}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
, define the finite set

I_{j}:=\{\gamma\in\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}:|\gamma|=j+2k\}.

For instance, we have that

I_{1}=\{3\},\;I_{2}=\{(3,3),4\},\;\mbox{and}\;I_{3}=\{(3,3,3),(4,3),(3,4),5\}.

The role of
I_{j}
will become clear in Theorem
2.9
below describing the full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}
from Equation (
8
) in a coordinate chart. For
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, we set
\mathrm{rk}(\gamma):=k
. In other words,
\gamma\in\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}
belongs to
I_{j}
if and only if
j=|\gamma|-2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)
. We remark that
|\gamma|\geq 3
and
\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)>0
is implicit for
\gamma\in I_{j}
since
I_{j}\subseteq\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}
. The number of elements in
I_{j}\cap\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}
is the same as the number of ways to write
j-k
as a sum of
k
natural numbers, and so

\#(I_{j}\cap\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3})=\begin{pmatrix}j-1\\
k-1\end{pmatrix}.

The following properties of
I_{j}
follows.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
j>0
. The set
I_{j}\subseteq\cup_{k>0}\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}
satisfies the following

•

\max\{|\gamma|:\gamma\in I_{j}\}=3j
and is attained at
\gamma=\vec{3}\in\mathbb{N}^{j}
.

•

\max\{\gamma_{i}:\gamma\in I_{j}\}=j+2
and is attained at
\gamma=j+2\in\mathbb{N}^{1}
.

•

\#I_{j}=2^{j-1}
.

For notational purposes, we introduce the following notation.

Definition 2.7
.

For an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup-2\mathbb{N}-1
, we introduce the notation

\omega_{n}:=\frac{\pi^{n/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)}.

If
n>0
, then
\omega_{n}
is the volume of the unit ball in
n
-dimensions.

To simplify the computations in the subsequent theorem, we note the following relations for the
\Gamma
-function.

Proposition 2.8
.

For natural numbers
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n>2k+1
, we have that

\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-k\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{n-2k}{2}+1\right)=\sqrt{\pi}\frac{\Gamma(n-2k+1)}{2^{n-2k}}{=\sqrt{\pi}\frac{(n-2k)!}{2^{n-2k}}}.

Moreover, we have the identities

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k!}\pi^{(n-1)/2}2^{n-2k}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-k\right)=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k+1}(n-2k)!\omega_{n-2k}\omega_{2k},\;

for
2k<n

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k!}\pi^{(n-1)/2}2^{n-2k}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-k\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{n/2+1}\omega_{2k}}{(2k-n)!\omega_{2k-n}}=

for
2k-n\in 2\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{n/2+1}(2k-n+1)}{2(2\pi)^{2k-n}}\omega_{2k-n+1}\omega_{2k},

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\pi^{(n-1)/2}}{2^{2k-n}\left(k-\frac{n+1}{2}\right)!k!}=

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{2k-n}}\omega_{2k}\omega_{2k-n-1},\;

for
2k-n\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

Proof.

The Legendre duplication formula
\Gamma(\zeta)\Gamma(\zeta+1/2)=2^{1-2\zeta}\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(2\zeta)
applied to
\zeta=n-2k+1
implies the first stated identity, and the second one follows from the identity
\Gamma(1/2-m)=(-4)^{m}m!/(2m)!
. Combining these identities with the definition of
\omega_{n}
produces the first and second identities. The third identity follows from the definition of
\omega_{n}
.
∎

We now arrive at the main result of this subsection, describing the full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}
. The reader should keep in mind that we are primarily interested in the function
G(x,y):=\mathrm{d}(x,y)^{2}
for a distance function
\mathrm{d}
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. In this case
Q_{G,\chi}(R)=Q(R)
is the localization of
\mathcal{Z}
at the diagonal for
\mathrm{Re}(R)>0
. We therefore formulate our results on the sector
\mathbb{C}_{+}
, albeit for
Q_{G,\chi}
they hold in the sector
\mathbb{C}\setminus i\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold and
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a function which is regular at the diagonal. We denote the Riemannian metric on
T^{*}M
dual to the transversal Hessian
H_{G}
by
g_{G}
, as in Proposition
2.5
. Consider the operator

Q_{G,\chi}(R)f:=\frac{1}{|R|_{\rm Mc}}\int_{M}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-|R|_{\rm Mc}\sqrt{G(x,y)}}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M\times M)
a function with
\chi=1
near
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
and supported only where
G
is smooth, and we use the Riemannian volume density defined from
g_{G}
.

We have that
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
is a classical elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter of order
-n-1
with principal symbol

\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(Q_{G,\chi})(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}.

In a coordinate chart
U_{0}
on
M
, the full symbol
q
of
Q_{G,\chi}
has a classical asymptotic expansion
q\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q_{j}
computed from the Taylor expansion (
9
) and each
q_{j}
is the homogeneous symbol of degree
-n-1-j
which for
j>0
and for
n
odd is given by

\displaystyle q_{j}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j},\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)<(n+1)/2}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}-

\displaystyle-\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j},\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)\geq(n+1)/2}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})\left[(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))\right],

and for
n
even given as

\displaystyle q_{j}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}

Here the coefficients are computed as

\mathfrak{c}_{k,n}:=\begin{cases}(-1)^{k}(n-2k)!\omega_{n-2k}\omega_{2k},\;&\mbox{for $2k<n$}\\
\frac{(-1)^{1-n/2}\omega_{2k}}{(2k-n)!\omega_{2k-n}},\;&\mbox{for $2k-n\in 2\mathbb{N}$}\\
\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{2k-n}}\omega_{2k}\omega_{2k-n-1},\;&\mbox{for $2k-n\in 2\mathbb{N}+1$}\end{cases}

Remark 2.10
.

Exact expressions for
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are given below in Proposition
2.13
and
2.14
, respectively.

Remark 2.11
.

From the expression for
q_{j}
in Theorem
2.9
, it is not clear that
q_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
-n-1-j
when
n
is odd. We shall see in the proof that this is in fact the case.

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

By Proposition
2.4
, the Schwartz kernel
\frac{1}{|R|}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-|R|\sqrt{G(x,y)}}
of
Q_{G,\chi}
is the Fourier transform in the
\eta
-direction of

K(x,y,\eta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\chi(x,y)\log(\eta^{2}+G(x,y)).

The extra
2\pi
is coming from Fourier inversion in one dimension.

Let
U
denote a neighborhood of the diagonal
\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
on which
G
is smooth. It follows from Proposition
2.5
(cf. Proposition
A.2
) that
K\in I^{-n-1}(U\times\mathbb{R};\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\})
with principal symbol

\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(K)(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}.

Define
K_{0}(x,y,\eta):=\log(\eta^{2}+G(x,y))\in I^{-n-1}(U\times\mathbb{R};\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\})
. We compute in a coordinate chart
U_{0}
that
K_{0}\in CI^{-n-1}(U\times\mathbb{R};\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\})
and using a uniform asymptotic expansion we use Proposition
A.5
to show that the Fourier transform in the
\eta
-direction of
K
is the Schwartz kernel of a pseudodifferential operator with parameter.

In a coordinate chart
U_{0}
, we introduce the coordinates
(x,v)=(x,x-y)
on
U_{0}\times U_{0}
. Using Equation (
9
), we can write

K_{0}(x,y,\eta)=-\log(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))-\log\left(1+\frac{\sum_{j=3}^{N}C_{G}^{(j)}(v,v)+r_{N}(x,v)}{\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v)}\right).

For small
v
, we can Taylor expand

K_{0}(x,y,\eta)=-\log(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\frac{(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}}{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\frac{C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(v)}{(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}}+\tilde{r}_{N}(x,v,\eta).

We note that, by the definition of
I_{j}
, each term in the second sum
\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\frac{(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}}{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\frac{C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(v)}{(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}}
is homogeneous of degree
j
. We also note that
\tilde{r}_{N}(x,v,\eta)=O((|\eta|+|v|)^{N+1})
and a short computation gives that
\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{v}\partial_{\eta}^{k}\tilde{r}_{N}=O((|\eta|+|v|)^{N+1-|\beta|-k})
for any multiindices
\alpha
,
\beta
and
k
. As such, we have a uniform asymptotic expansion
K\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}K_{j}
(cf. Definition
A.4
) where

(10)

K_{j}(x,v,\eta)=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v)),\quad&j=0,\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\frac{(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)+1}}{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\frac{C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(v)}{(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}},\quad&j>0.\end{cases}

We conclude from Proposition
A.5
that
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{R})
is a pseudodifferential operator with parameter. Proposition
A.5
implies that

\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(Q_{G,\chi})(x,\xi,R)=\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(K)(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}.

This is invertible in
C^{\infty}(S(T^{*}M\oplus\mathbb{R}))
so
Q_{G,\chi}
is elliptic with parameter. It is readily seen that
\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(Q_{G,\chi})(x,\xi,R)\neq 0
also for
R\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, and the following symbol computation shows that
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter.

Let us turn to the full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}
. We will compute it from Equation (
10
) and the Fourier transform computations of Section
A
of the appendix. For
k>0
, denote the Fourier transform of
(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{-k}
in the
(v,\eta)
-direction by
F_{k}(x,\xi,R)
, that is

F_{k}(x,\xi,R):=\int_{T_{x}M\oplus\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-i\xi.v-iR\eta}}{(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{k}}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\eta.

Using homogeneity and rotational symmetries,
F_{k}
can be computed as in Proposition
A.1
to be

\frac{\pi^{(n+1)/2}2^{-2k+n+1}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-k\right)}{(k-1)!}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+k},

for
2k-n-1\notin 2\mathbb{N}
, and

(11)

\frac{(-1)^{k-\frac{n+1}{2}}\pi^{(n+1)/2}}{2^{2k-n-1}\left(k-\frac{n+1}{2}\right)!(k-1)!}\left[(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+k}\left(-\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))+\beta_{k-(n+1)/2,n+1}\right)\right],

for
2k-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
It follows that for
\gamma\in I_{j}
, the symbol of the term
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(v)(\eta^{2}+H_{G}(v,v))^{-\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}
is given by
C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})F_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}(x,\xi,R)

which is computed to be

\frac{\pi^{(n+1)/2}2^{-2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)+n+1}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)\right)}{(\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-1)!}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)},

for
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\notin 2\mathbb{N}
, and

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-\frac{n+1}{2}}\pi^{(n+1)/2}}{2^{2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1}}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-\frac{n+1}{2}\right)!(\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-1)!\cdot

(12)

\displaystyle C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})

\displaystyle\left[(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\left(-\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))+\beta_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-(n+1)/2,n+1}\right)\right],

for
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
. We conclude that the symbol
q_{j}
of
K_{j}
, for
j>0
, is (up to the term
\beta_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n+1}
) given by the formula in the statement of the theorem for the pre-factors
\mathfrak{c}_{k,n}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{k,n}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{k!}\pi^{(n-1)/2}2^{n-2k}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}-k\right),\;&\mbox{for $2k-n-1\notin 2\mathbb{N}$,}\\
\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}\pi^{(n-1)/2}}{2^{2k-n}\left(k-\frac{n+1}{2}\right)!k!},\;&\mbox{for $2k-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}$.}\end{cases}

Therefore
\mathfrak{c}_{k,n}
takes the form prescribed in the theorem by Proposition
2.8
.

We finish the proof by showing that
\beta_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-(n+1)/2,n+1}
does not contribute in Equation (
12
) and that there is no logarithmic term when expanding the
\xi
-derivatives in Equation (
12
). If
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
, then
-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}
and so
(R^{2}+g_{\mathrm{d}}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}
is a polynomial of degree
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
in
\xi
. For
\gamma\in I_{j}\subseteq\cup_{k}\mathbb{N}_{\geq 3}^{k}
, we have that
j+2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)=|\gamma|\geq 3\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)
. Therefore, if
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
then
C_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}^{(\gamma)}(x,D_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{\mathrm{d}}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}
is a polynomial of degree
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1-|\gamma|\leq-\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1<0
and therefore it must be identically zero. In other words, we have
C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}=0
for
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
. This equality proves that
q_{j}
can not contain a term with a logarithmic factor, and as such
q_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
-n-1-j
for all
j
, and

\displaystyle C_{G}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle(x,-D_{\xi})\left[(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\left(-\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))+\beta_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-(n+1)/2,n+1}\right)\right]=

\displaystyle=-C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})\left[(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))\right].

∎

Remark 2.12
.

The computation that
q_{0}(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}
is compatible with known symbol computations in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
G(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
in which case
q=q_{0}
is the full symbol expansion when using Euclidean coordinates. Indeed, this statement follows from the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-R|v|}
is the Fourier transform of
n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+|\xi|^{2})^{-(n+1)/2}
, see for instance
[
2
, Equation (3)]
. We remark that the coordinate dependent symbol computations of Theorem
2.9
will be used also in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The reason for using Theorem
2.9
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is that to describe the operator near the boundary of a domain with the Wiener-Hopf factorization techniques of Section
5
below we need to “straighten out the boundary”, i.e. choose coordinates in which the boundary locally looks like a half-space. We make such computations more precise in Subsection
2.2
below.

Let us give some further details in computing the symbols
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
. The precise information contained in
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
will be used later to compute the first terms in the inverse of
Q
and the asymptotics of its expectation values in Section
6
. Before entering into the symbol computations of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
, let us introduce some notation. Since
g
is a metric on
T^{*}M
, it can be viewed as a symmetric tensor in
TM\otimes TM
and for a covector
\xi
, the contraction
\iota_{\xi}g_{G}
takes values in
TM
. In the coordinate chart, each
C^{j}_{G}
takes values in the symmetric
j
-forms on
TM|_{U_{0}}
. As such, expressions such as
C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})
or
C^{4}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})
, for instance, make sense. The reader should be aware that such expressions are individually not coordinate invariant, the transformation rules for these expressions can be deduced either from the Taylor expansion (
9
) or from the transformation rules for pseudodifferential operators. For computational purposes, we also note that

\mathrm{d}_{\xi}g_{G}(\xi,\xi)=2\iota_{\xi}g_{G}.

Here
\mathrm{d}_{\xi}
denotes the fiberwise exterior differential and we are implicitly using the canonical identification
T^{*}(T^{*}M)=\pi^{*}TM\oplus\pi^{*}T^{*}M
and that the
TM
-summand is where
\mathrm{d}_{\xi}
maps to.
A useful tool in formulating the computations is the Pochhammer
k
-symbol. For
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write

(x)_{n,k}:=\underbrace{x(x+k)(x+2k)\cdots(x+(n-1)k)}_{n\;\mbox{\tiny factors}},

with the convention that
(x)_{0,k}=1
for any
k
and
(0)_{n,k}=1
for any
n
and
k
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
M
and
Q_{G,\chi}
be as in Theorem
2.9
.
In a coordinate chart on
M
, the term
q_{1}
of degree
-n-2
appearing in the full symbol
q
of
Q_{G,\chi}
is given by

\displaystyle q_{1}(x,\xi,R)={-}i\bigg(

\displaystyle 6C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-2}-

\displaystyle-8C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-3}\bigg),

if
n=1
, and if
n>1
we have that

\displaystyle q_{1}(x,\xi,R)=-i(n^{2}-1)\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}\bigg(

\displaystyle 3C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-

\displaystyle-(n+3)C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}\bigg),

where
\iota_{\xi}g_{G}
denotes the contraction of the metric
g_{G}
on
T^{*}M
along the covector
\xi
.

Proof.

We note that
I_{1}=\{\gamma\in\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{N}^{k}_{\geq 3}:|\gamma|=1+2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)\}=\{3\}
. For
n=1
, we have
\mathfrak{c}_{1,1}=-1/2
and since we are in the critical case we compute as follows

\displaystyle q_{1}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-i\mathfrak{c}_{1,1}C^{3}(x,1)\partial_{\xi}^{3}\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))=iC^{3}(x,1)\partial_{\xi}^{2}\frac{\iota_{\xi}g_{G}}{R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi)}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle iC^{3}_{G}(x,1)\bigg(-\frac{{6}g_{G}\iota_{\xi}g_{G}}{(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{2}}+\frac{8(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{3}}{(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{3}}\bigg),

which proves the case
n=1
.

For
n>1
, we compute that

\displaystyle q_{1}(x,\xi,R)=i(n+1)(n-1)\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}\bigg(-

\displaystyle 3C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}+

\displaystyle+(n+3)C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}\bigg).

∎

The next proposition follows from a similar computation.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
M
and
Q_{G,\chi}
be as in Theorem
2.9
.
In a coordinate chart on
M
, the term
q_{2}
of degree
-n-3
appearing in the full symbol
q
of
Q_{G,\chi}
is given as follows; for
n=1
we have that

\displaystyle q_{2}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{2,1}C^{3}(x,1)^{2}\partial_{\xi}^{6}\left((R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))\right)+\mathfrak{c}_{1,1}C^{4}(x,1)\partial_{\xi}^{4}\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{C^{3}(x,1)^{2}}{16}\left(-\frac{2^{3}\cdot 15g_{G}^{3}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{2}}+\frac{2^{4}\cdot 90(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}g_{G}^{2}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{3}}-\frac{2^{4}\cdot 80(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{4}g_{G}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{4}}+\frac{2^{6}\cdot 24(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{6}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{5}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\frac{C^{4}(x,1)}{2}\left(-\frac{2^{2}\cdot 3g_{G}^{2}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{2}}+\frac{2^{3}\cdot 12(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}g_{G}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{3}}-\frac{2^{4}\cdot 6(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{4}}{(R^{2}+g_{G})^{4}}\right),

and for
n=3
, we have that

\displaystyle q_{2}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}C^{3}(x,\partial_{\xi})^{2}\left(\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))\right)+\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}C^{4}(x,\partial_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-1}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}240(C^{3}_{G}\otimes C^{3}_{G})(g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-3}-

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}45\cdot 96C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-4}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}120\cdot 96C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-5}-

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}80\cdot 96C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-6}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}24C^{4}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-3}-

\displaystyle{-\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}288C^{4}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-4}+}

\displaystyle{+\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}192C^{4}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-5}}

and finally for
n\neq 1,3
, we have that

\displaystyle q_{2}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}C^{3}(x,\partial_{\xi})^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi)))^{-(n-3)/2}+\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}C^{4}(x,\partial_{\xi})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n-1)/2}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-24\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+3)_{4,-2}C^{3}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+}

\displaystyle{+6\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})C^{3}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3}-}

\displaystyle{-\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+7)_{6,-2}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-4}{+}}

\displaystyle+3\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}(C^{3}\otimes C^{3})(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}+

\displaystyle{+3\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)C^{4}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-}

\displaystyle-6\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}C^{4}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+5)_{4,-2}C^{4}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3},

where
\iota_{\xi}g_{G}
denotes the contraction of the metric
g_{G}
on
T^{*}M
along the covector
\xi
.

2.2.
Examples and further structure in the symbol computations

In the preceding subsection we saw a detailed computation of the full symbol of
Q_{\chi,G}
in terms of the Taylor expansion. Let us consider a few important special cases where further structures can be visible in the symbol expansion, and proceed with a structural statement of for the entries in the full symbol in the general case.

Example 2.15

(Symbol computations near a boundary in Euclidean space)
.

We consider the manifold
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the function
G(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
which is regular at the diagonal. This example fits into the bigger picture of the paper seeing that
G(x,y)=\mathrm{d}(x,y)^{2}
. It will later in the paper be crucial to describe the symbol of
Q=Q_{G,\chi}
near the boundary of a domain
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with smooth boundary. Fix a point
x_{0}\in\partial X
. Up to a rigid motion, we can assume that
x_{0}=0
and that the normal vector of
\partial X
in
x_{0}
is orthogonal to the plane
x_{n}=0
, where we write
x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})
for
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. There is a neighborhood
U_{0}=U_{00}\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a smooth function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(U_{00})
such that
X\cap U_{0}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in U_{0}:\varphi(x^{\prime})\leq x_{n}\}
. Since the normal vector of
\partial X
in
x_{0}
is orthogonal to the plane
x_{n}=0
,
\nabla_{x^{\prime}}\varphi(0)=0
. Near
x_{0}=0
, we use the coordinates

(x^{\prime},x_{n})\mapsto(x^{\prime},x_{n}-\varphi(x^{\prime})).

In these new coordinates, the domain
X
looks like the half-space
\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):x_{n}\geq 0\}
locally near
x_{0}=0
. We compute that in these coordinates
G=G(x,y)
can be written as

\displaystyle|(x^{\prime},x_{n}-\varphi(x^{\prime}))-

\displaystyle(y^{\prime},y_{n}-\varphi(y^{\prime}))|^{2}=|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}+(x_{n}-y_{n}-(\varphi(x^{\prime})-\varphi(y^{\prime})))^{2}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x-y|^{2}-2(x_{n}-y_{n})(\varphi(x^{\prime})-\varphi(y^{\prime}))+(\varphi(x^{\prime})-\varphi(y^{\prime}))^{2}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|v|^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}|=j-1}\frac{-2\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}!}v_{n}(v^{\prime})^{\alpha^{\prime}}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=2}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha^{\prime}|+|\beta^{\prime}|=j,\\
|\alpha^{\prime}|,|\beta^{\prime}|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}!\beta^{\prime}!}(v^{\prime})^{\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}}+O(|v|^{N+1}),

where the sums over
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
ranges over
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}
and we have written
v=x-y
,
v^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
and
v_{n}=x_{n}-y_{n}
. Introducing the notation

\nabla^{j}\varphi(x^{\prime};v^{\prime}):=\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}|=j}\frac{\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}!}(v^{\prime})^{\alpha^{\prime}},

we have in these coordinates that

\displaystyle G(x,y)=

\displaystyle|v|^{2}-2v_{n}\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\cdot v^{\prime}+(\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\cdot v^{\prime})^{2}+\sum_{j=3}^{N}-2v_{n}\nabla^{j-1}\varphi(x^{\prime};v^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=3}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+l=j,\\
k,l>0\end{subarray}}\nabla^{k}\varphi(x^{\prime};v^{\prime})\nabla^{l}\varphi(x^{\prime};v^{\prime})+O(|v|^{N+1})

In particular, we can conclude that

H_{G}(v)=|v|^{2}-2v_{n}\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\cdot v^{\prime}+(\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\cdot v^{\prime})^{2},

so
H_{G}
is represented by the
n\times n
-matrix

H_{G}=\begin{pmatrix}1_{n-1}+\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})^{T}&-\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\\
-\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})^{T}&1\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, in the same basis we have that

g_{G}=H_{G}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1_{n-1}&\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})\\
\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})^{T}&1+|\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})|^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

We note that
H_{G}|_{T\partial X}
is the Riemannian metric on
\partial X
induced from the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the inclusion
\partial X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We also conclude that

(13)

C^{j}_{G}(x;v)=v_{n}C^{j,1}_{G}(x;v^{\prime})+C^{j,0}_{G}(x;v^{\prime})

where

C^{j,1}_{G}(x;v^{\prime}):=-2\nabla^{j-1}\varphi(x^{\prime};v^{\prime})\quad\mbox{and}\quad C^{j,0}_{G}(x;v^{\prime}):=\sum_{k+l=j,k,l>0}\nabla^{k}\varphi(x^{\prime};v)\nabla^{l}\varphi(x^{\prime};v).

Therefore, in these coordinates near the boundary of a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have for
\gamma\in I_{j}
that

\displaystyle C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,-D_{\xi})=(-1)^{|\gamma|}\prod_{l=1}^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\left[\sum_{|\alpha^{\prime}|=|\gamma_{l}|-1}\frac{-2\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}!}D_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}D_{\xi_{n}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha^{\prime}|+|\beta^{\prime}|=|\gamma_{l}|,\\
|\alpha^{\prime}|,|\beta^{\prime}|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}\varphi(x^{\prime})}{\alpha^{\prime}!\beta^{\prime}!}D_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}}\right].

This gives a method for computing the homogeneous symbol
q_{j}
of degree
-n-1-j
for any
j
following Theorem
2.9
.

The principal symbol is computed as in Theorem
2.9
. By Proposition
2.13
we compute for
n>1
that

\displaystyle q_{1}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-i(n^{2}-1)\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}\bigg(6g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-2(n+3)\nabla^{2}\varphi(\iota_{\xi}g_{G},\iota_{\xi}g_{G})g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}\bigg)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i(n^{2}-1)\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}\bigg(6\xi_{n}g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-2(n+3)\xi_{n}\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}\bigg),

By Proposition
2.14
we compute for
n\neq 1,3
that

\displaystyle q_{2}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-24\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+3)_{4,-2}4(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}(g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n}))^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+6\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}4g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)(g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n}))^{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi(\iota_{\xi}g_{G},\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3}-

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+7)_{6,-2}4(\nabla^{2}\varphi(\iota_{\xi}g_{G},\iota_{\xi}g_{G}))^{2}(g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n}))^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-4}+

\displaystyle+3\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}\cdot 4(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}g_{G}(\nabla\varphi-e_{n},\nabla\varphi-e_{n})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}+

\displaystyle{+3\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)\bigg(2(g_{G}\otimes g_{G})(\nabla^{3}\varphi\otimes(\nabla\varphi-e_{n}))+(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-}

\displaystyle-6\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}\bigg(2(g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(\nabla^{3}\varphi)g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n})+

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))(\nabla^{2}\varphi(\iota_{\xi}g_{G},\iota_{\xi}g_{G}))\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+5)_{4,-2}\bigg(2(\iota_{\xi}g_{G})^{\otimes 3}(\nabla^{3}\varphi)g_{G}(\xi,\nabla\varphi-e_{n})+

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(\nabla^{2}\varphi(\iota_{\xi}g_{G},\iota_{\xi}g_{G}))^{2}\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3}=

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt=

\displaystyle-48\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+3)_{4,-2}(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+24\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)\xi_{n}^{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3}-

\displaystyle-4\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+7)_{6,-2}(\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2}))^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-4}+

\displaystyle+(12\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}+3\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1))(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}+

\displaystyle-6\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}\bigg(-2\xi_{n}\nabla^{3}\varphi(1_{n-1}\otimes(\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi))+

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))(\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2})\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+5)_{4,-2}\bigg(-2\xi_{n}(\nabla^{3}\varphi)((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 3})+

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2})^{2}\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-3},

and for
n=3
that

\displaystyle q_{2}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}240\cdot 4+\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}24)(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-3}-

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}45\cdot 96\cdot 4(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-4}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}120\cdot 96\cdot 4g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)\xi_{n}^{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-5}

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}80\cdot 96\cdot 4(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-6}+

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}288\bigg(-2\xi_{n}(\nabla^{3}\varphi)(1_{n-1}\otimes(\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi))+

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt(g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi))(\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2}))\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-4}+

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}192\bigg(-2\xi_{n}(\nabla^{3}\varphi)((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 3})+(\nabla^{2}\varphi((\xi^{\prime}+\xi_{n}\nabla\varphi)^{\otimes 2}))^{2}\bigg)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-5}.

We note that
g_{G}(\nabla^{2}\varphi)(x_{0})
is
(n-1)/2
times the mean curvature in
x_{0}
.

Example 2.16

(Symbol computations for a submanifold of Euclidean space)
.

We consider a submanifold
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the function
G(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
which is regular at the diagonal. This example fits into the bigger picture of the paper seeing that
G(x,y)=\mathrm{d}(x,y)^{2}
where
\mathrm{d}
is the distance function on
M
making the inclusion
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
isometric. To Taylor expand
G
as in (
9
), we take coordinates around a point
x_{0}\in M
such that
M
near
x_{0}
is parametrized by

\begin{cases}x_{l}=x_{l},\;&l=1,\ldots,n\\
x_{l}=\varphi_{l}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\;&l=n+1,\ldots,N\end{cases},

for some functions
\varphi_{n+1},\ldots,\varphi_{N}
. Write
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. In these coordinates,

\displaystyle G(x,y)

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{2}+\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}|\varphi_{l}(x)-\varphi_{l}(y)|^{2}=

\displaystyle=|v|^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{N_{0}}\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|+|\beta|=j,\\
|\alpha|,|\beta|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi_{l}(x)\partial_{x}^{\beta}\varphi_{l}(x)}{\alpha!\beta!}v^{\alpha+\beta}+O(|v|^{N_{0}+1}),

where
v=x-y
. Therefore, we conclude that

H_{G}(v)=|v|^{2}+\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}(\nabla\varphi_{l}(x)\cdot v)^{2},

and for
j>2
,

C^{j}_{G}(x,v)=\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|+|\beta|=j,\\
|\alpha|,|\beta|>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varphi_{l}(x)\partial_{x}^{\beta}\varphi_{l}(x)}{\alpha!\beta!}v^{\alpha+\beta}=\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+k=j,\\
i,k>0\end{subarray}}(\nabla^{i}\varphi_{l}\otimes\nabla^{k}\varphi_{l})(v).

Computations similar to those in Example
2.15
can be carried out also for submanifolds. To preserve the reader’s sanity, we spare the details.

Example 2.17

(Symbol computations for geodesic distances)
.

Consider a manifold
M
equipped with a Riemannian metric
g_{M}
. To avoid having to prescribe the distance between different components, we assume that
M
is connected. The geodesic distance
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
is defined by

\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}(x,y):=\inf\{L(c):\;\mbox{$c$ is a smooth path from $x$ to $y$}\},

where the length
L(c)
of a path
c:[0,1]\to M
is defined by

L(c):=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{g_{M,c(t)}(\dot{c}(t),\dot{c}(t))}\mathrm{d}t.

Here we write
\dot{c}:[0,1]\to TM
for the derivative of the path, so
\dot{c}(t)\in T_{c(t)}M
. For a suitable neighborhood
U\subseteq TM
of the zero section, the Riemannian metric defines an exponential map
\mathrm{exp}:\boldsymbol{U}\to M
. More precisely, for a small enough
v\in T_{x}M
,
\exp_{x}(v)=\exp(x,v)\in M
is defined in local coordinates as
\exp_{x}(v)=w_{x}(v;1)
where
w_{x}(v;\cdot):[0,1]\to TM
is the solution to the second order ordinary differential equation

(14)

\begin{cases}\ddot{w}_{x}(v,t)+\Gamma_{w_{x}(v;t)}(\dot{w}_{x}(v,t),\dot{w}_{x}(v,t))=0,\\
w(v;0)=x,\\
\dot{w}(v,0)=-v,\end{cases}

where
\Gamma
is the affine connection defined from
g
, which in local coordinates is a vector valued symmetric bilinear form on the tangent bundle. In these local coordinates, for
x
and
y
close enough we have that

\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}(x,y)=|\exp_{x}^{-1}(y)|_{g}^{2}.

In particular,
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}
is smooth in a neighborhood of the diagonal.

Let us compute the Taylor expansion of
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}
as in Equation (
9
) and prove that
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. We use a coordinate neighborhood as above, and write
v=x-y
. We are looking for the Taylor expansion in
v
of the coordinate function
X_{x}(v)=\exp_{x}^{-1}(x-v)
. We Taylor expand

w_{x}(v,t)=x-vt+\sum_{k=2}^{N}\frac{w^{(k)}_{x}(v;0)}{k!}t^{k}+O(|tv|^{N+1}).

We note that
v\mapsto w^{(k)}_{x}(v;0)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
, we denote this by
W_{k}(x;v)
. It follows from Equation (
14
) that

(15)

\begin{cases}W_{1}(v)=-v,\\
W_{2}(v)=-\Gamma(v,v),\\
W_{3}(v)=-\mathrm{d}\Gamma(v,v,v)-2\Gamma(v,\Gamma(v,v)).\end{cases}

The higher order terms
W_{4},W_{5},\ldots
can be computed inductively from Equation (
14
). Write the Taylor expansion of the unknown function
X_{x}
in
v
as

X_{x}(v)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}X^{(k)}(x;v)+O(|v|^{N+1}),

where
X^{(k)}(x;\cdot)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
in
v
.
The identity
X_{x}(v)=\exp_{x}^{-1}(x-v)
is equivalent to
\exp_{x}(X_{x}(v))=x-v
which implies that

(16)

-\sum_{k=1}^{N}X^{(k)}(x;v)+\sum_{k=2}^{N}\frac{W_{k}(x,\sum_{l=1}^{N}X^{(l)}(x;v))}{k!}=-v+O(|v|^{N+1}).

Considering the first order term, we see that
X^{(1)}(v)=v
. The higher order terms can be inductively determined by considering each homogeneous term separately:

(17)

X^{(k)}(v)=\left[\sum_{j=2}^{k}\frac{W_{j}(x,\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}X^{(l)}(x;v))}{j!}\right]_{(k)},

where
[\cdot]_{(k)}
denotes the homogeneous term of degree
k
. Using Equation (
15
) and (
16
), we compute the first terms to be

\begin{cases}X^{(1)}(v)&=v,\\
X^{(2)}(v)&=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}(x;v)=-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma(v,v),\\
X^{(3)}(v)&=\frac{1}{6}W_{3}(x;v)+\frac{1}{2}\left[W_{2}\left(x;v-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma(v,v)\right)\right]_{(3)}=\\
&=-\frac{1}{6}\mathrm{d}\Gamma(v,v,v)+\frac{1}{6}\Gamma(v,\Gamma(v,v)).\end{cases}

We summarize these computations in a proposition.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
M
be a manifold equipped with a Riemannian metric
g_{M}
and let
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}
denote the geodesic distance. Then
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal and in a coordinate neighborhood the Taylor expansion as in Equation (
9
) takes the form

\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}(x,y)=|v|^{2}_{g_{M}}+C^{3}_{\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}}(x;v)+C^{4}_{\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}}(x;v)+O(|v|^{5}_{g_{m}}),

where

\displaystyle C^{3}_{\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}}(x;v)=

\displaystyle-g_{M}(v,\Gamma(v,v)),

\displaystyle C^{4}_{\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}}(x;v)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}|\Gamma(v,v)|^{2}_{g_{M}}+\frac{1}{3}\left(g(v,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(v,v,v))-g(v,\Gamma(v,\Gamma(v,v)))\right).

The higher order terms
C^{j}_{\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}}
can be computed inductively from Equation (
17
).

Let us return to the general case and describe the overall structure of the terms appearing in the full symbol expansion of
Q_{G,\chi}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
M
and
G
be as in Theorem
2.9
. The entries in the full symbol expansion
q\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q_{j}
of
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
takes the form

q_{j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k=0}^{3j}P_{k,j}(x,\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-\frac{n+1+j+k}{2}},

where
P_{k,j}
are of the form

(1)

P_{k,j}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
in
\xi
and

P_{k,j}\equiv 0\quad\mbox{if $j-k\notin 2\mathbb{Z}$}.

(2)

If
j-k\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, the polynomial
P_{k,j}
takes the form

P_{k,j}(x,\xi)=\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\rho_{\gamma,k,j}C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,\underbrace{\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}}_{k\;times}\otimes\underbrace{g_{G}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{G}}_{\frac{|\gamma|-k}{2}\;times}),

for some coefficients
\rho_{\gamma,k,j}\in\mathbb{Q}\pi^{n/2}+\mathbb{Q}\pi^{(n+1)/2}+\mathbb{Q}\pi^{(n-1)/2}
.

In the last item, we note that if
j-k\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, then
|\gamma|-k\in 2\mathbb{Z}
for all
\gamma\in I_{j}
.

Proof.

It follows from the computations in Theorem
2.9
that
q_{j}
can be written as a finite sum

q_{j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k\geq 0}P_{k,j}(x,\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-\frac{n+1+j+k}{2}},

where
P_{k,j}
is a polynomial in
\xi
whose coefficients (as a polynomial in
\xi
) are all polynomials in the Taylor coefficients of
G
at the diagonal. For the degrees to match,
P_{k,j}
must be of degree
k
. Since the powers
R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi)
must differ from
-(n+1)/2
by an integer,
P_{k,j}=0
unless
j-k\notin 2\mathbb{Z}
. Finally, the largest possible degree
k
for which
\xi^{\alpha}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-\frac{n+1+j+k}{2}}
(with
|\alpha|=k
) can be a summand in
q_{j}
is if the derivative
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,D_{\xi})
acts only on
(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}
(or
(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2+\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)}\log(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))
if
2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-n-1\in 2\mathbb{N}
) and in that case
\alpha=\gamma
, so the maximal degree of
P_{k,j}
is the size of the largest index in
I_{j}
, i.e.
3j
. This proves item (1). To prove item (2), one notices that by Theorem
2.9
, all possible entries in
q_{j}
consists of terms of the form

C^{(\gamma)}_{G}(x,\underbrace{\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}}_{k\;times}\otimes\underbrace{g_{G}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{G}}_{\frac{|\gamma|-k}{2}\;times})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-\frac{n+1+j+k}{2}},

with a coefficient being a rational number times either
\pi^{n/2}
,
\pi^{(n+1)/2}
or
\pi^{(n-1)/2}
.
∎

2.3.
The symbol structure of the parametrix

The operator
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
considered in the previous subsection is elliptic with parameter by Theorem
2.9
. In particular, it admits a parametrix
A_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
, that is an operator with parameter so that
A_{G,\chi}Q_{G,\chi}-1,Q_{G,\chi}A_{G,\chi}-1\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
.

Theorem 2.20
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold and
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a function which is regular at the diagonal. The full symbol
a
in local coordinates of the parametrix
A_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
of
Q_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
has an asymptotic expansion
a\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}
where
a_{j}
is constructed inductively from the symbol expansion
q\sim\sum_{j}q_{j}
of Theorem
2.9
by

a_{j}=-a_{0}\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j,\,l<j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{k}D_{x}^{\alpha}a_{l}.

Here
a_{0}(x,\xi,R)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2}
. The next term is given by

\displaystyle n=1:\quad a_{1}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(\partial_{\xi}g_{G})(\partial_{x}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-1}+\frac{3i}{2}C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})-

\displaystyle-2iC^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-1}

\displaystyle n>1:\quad a_{1}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle{-}\frac{(n+1)^{2}i}{n!\omega_{n}}g_{G}(\mathrm{d}_{x}g_{G}(\xi,\xi),\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+\frac{3i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{(n!)^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}}C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-1}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{(n!)^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}}C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-2}.

The homogeneous terms
a_{j}
each takes the form

a_{j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k=0}^{3j}\tilde{P}_{k,j}(x,\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{\frac{n+1-j-k}{2}},

where
\tilde{P}_{k,j}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
in
\xi
and

\tilde{P}_{k,j}\equiv 0\quad\mbox{if $j-k\notin 2\mathbb{Z}$}.

The coefficients of
\tilde{P}_{k,j}
as a polynomial in
\xi
are all polynomials in derivatives of the Taylor coefficients
(C^{(\gamma)}_{G})_{\gamma\in\cup_{k\leq j}I_{k}}
of
G
at the diagonal (from Equation (
9
)) of total degree
j
.

In the structural statement at the end of the proposition, the total degree refers to the degree of the polynomial when counting an order
i
derivative of a Taylor coefficient
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}
for
\gamma\in I_{k}
to have degree
k+i
.

Proof.

It follows from the parametrix construction for elliptic operators (see e.g.
[
39
, Chapter I.5]
) that
a\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}
where
a_{0}=q_{0}^{-1}
and
a_{j}:=-a_{0}\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j,\,l<j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{k}D_{x}^{\alpha}a_{l}
for
j>0
.

Let us prove the structural statement about
a_{j}
by induction on
j
. It is clear for
j=0
. Assume that the structural statement holds for
l<j+1
. We have that

\displaystyle q_{0}a_{j+1}=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j+1,\,l<j+1}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{k}D_{x}^{\alpha}a_{l}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j+1,\,l<j+1}\sum_{i_{1}=0}^{N(k)}\sum_{i_{2}=0}^{\tilde{N}(l)}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\left[P_{i_{1},k}(x,\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-\frac{n+1+k+i_{1}}{2}}\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\cdot D_{x}^{\alpha}\left[\tilde{P}_{i_{2},l}(x,\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{\frac{n+1-l-i_{2}}{2}}\right],

which proves that
a_{j+1}
has the claimed structure.

What remains is to compute
a_{1}
. We write

\displaystyle a_{1}(x,\xi,R)=

\displaystyle-a_{0}\sum_{|\alpha|=1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}q_{0}D^{\alpha}_{x}a_{0}-a_{0}^{2}q_{1}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2}\sum_{|\alpha|=1}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2}D^{\alpha}_{x}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2}

\displaystyle{+\frac{i(n^{2}-1)\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}}{(n!)^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}}(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{n+1}\bigg(3C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-1}-}

\displaystyle{\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt-(n+3)C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{-(n+1)/2-2}\bigg)=}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-}\frac{(n+1)^{2}i}{n!\omega_{n}}g_{G}(\mathrm{d}_{x}g_{G}(\xi,\xi),\xi)(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-2}+

\displaystyle+\frac{3i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{(n!)^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}}C^{3}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-1}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{(n!)^{2}\omega_{n}^{2}}C^{3}_{G}(x,\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G}\otimes\iota_{\xi}g_{G})(R^{2}+g_{G}(\xi,\xi))^{(n+1)/2-2}

∎

We use the notation
\Gamma_{\alpha}(R_{0}):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\mathrm{Arg}(z)|<\alpha,\mathrm{Re}(z)>R_{0}\}
.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold and
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a function which is regular at the diagonal. For some
R_{0}>0
, the operator
Q_{G,\chi}(R)\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M)
is invertible as an operator
H^{-(n+1)/2}(M)\to H^{(n+1)/2}(M)
for any
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and

Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}-A_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})).

In particular,
Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}
is a pseudodifferential operator with parameter in
\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
whose full symbol
a
in local coordinates has an asymptotic expansion
a\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}
where
a_{j}
is as in Theorem
2.20
.

Corollary
2.21
follows from Theorem
2.9
and
2.20
using standard techniques for pseudodifferential operator with parameter. For the convenience of the reader, we include its proof.

Proof.

Let
\Delta
denote a positive Laplace operator on
M
whose principal symbol coincides with the metric
g_{G}
dual to the transversal Hessian of
G
. It follows from Theorem
2.9
that
\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}((R^{2}+\Delta)^{-\mu})=\overline{\sigma}_{-n-1}(Q)
. Hence, the operator
\mathfrak{r}(R):=1-(R^{2}+\Delta)^{\mu/2}Q(R^{2}+\Delta)^{\mu/2}
