is a parameter-dependent pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
. We write

Q_{G,\chi}=(R^{2}+\Delta)^{-\mu/2}(1-\mathfrak{r})(R^{2}+\Delta)^{-\mu/2}.

The order of
\mathfrak{r}
is
-1
, so by
[
39
, Theorem 9.1]
we have that
\|\mathfrak{r}(R)\|_{L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-1})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty
. We conclude that there exists an
R_{0}>1
such that
\|\mathfrak{r}(R)\|_{L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)}\leq\frac{1}{2}
for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Since
(R^{2}+\Delta)^{-\mu/2}:H^{s}(M)\to H^{s+\mu}(M)
is invertible for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(0)
and any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we can for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
invert
Q_{G,\chi}
as the absolutely convergent series of operators
H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M)
given by

Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(R^{2}+\Delta)^{\mu/2}\mathfrak{r}(R)^{k}(R^{2}+\Delta)^{\mu/2}.

It remains to prove that
Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}-A_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))
. By the construction above,
Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))
. Therefore the classes
[Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}]
and
[A_{G,\chi}]
in the formal symbol algebra
\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)/\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))
are both inverses to

[Q_{G,\chi}]\in\cup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\Psi^{k}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))/\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})).

By the uniqueness of inverses,
[Q_{G,\chi}^{-1}]=[A_{G,\chi}]\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))/\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))
.
∎

2.4.
Analytic results for
Q
on compact manifolds

The results of the previous subsections have analytic implications in the case that
M
is a compact manifold. We consider the scale of Hilbert spaces
H^{s}_{R}(M):=(R^{2}+\Delta)^{-s/2}L^{2}(M)
defined for
R\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
with the Hilbert space structure making
(R^{2}+\Delta)^{-s/2}:L^{2}(M)\to H^{s}_{R}(M)
unitary. Here
\Delta
could be any choice of Laplacian, but for the sake of simplicity we fix the Laplacian associated with the Riemannian metric associated with a function regular at the diagonal. By elliptic regularity,
H^{s}(M)=H^{s}_{R}(M)
as vector spaces with equivalent norms independently of the choice of Laplacian, but the Hilbert space structure differs in a non-uniform way as
R
varies. At this stage, we shall start to concern ourselves with extensions of
Q
to the complex numbers, so we phrase our results in terms of the operator

(18)

Q(R)f(x):=\frac{1}{R}\int_{M}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,

where
\mathrm{d}
is a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal and
\chi
a function being
1
near the diagonal such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on the support of
\chi
. We note that
Q(R)=Q_{\mathrm{d}^{2},\chi}(R)
for
\mathrm{Re}(R)>0
.

Theorem 2.22
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. Set
\mu:=(n+1)/2
. The operator

Q(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M),

defined from the expression (
18
) is a well defined Fredholm operator for all
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and there is an
R_{0}
such that
Q(R)
is invertible for all
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
.
Moreover, the following holds:

a)

For each
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,
Q(R)\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M)
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator and the family of operators

(Q(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}}

depends holomorphically on
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Moreover, we can extend the holomorphic family
(Q(R)^{-1}:H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M))_{R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})}

meromorphically to
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

b)

There is a
C>0
such that

C^{-1}\|f\|_{H^{-\mu}_{|R|}(M)}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Re}\langle f,Q(R)f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{-\mu}_{|R|}(M)}^{2},

for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and
f\in H^{-\mu}(M)
. In particular, for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
, the norm
\mathrm{Re}\langle\cdot,Q(R)\cdot\rangle
is uniformly equivalent to the norm on
H^{-\mu}_{|R|}(M)
.

Proof.

The first statements of the theorem follows from Corollary
2.21
.

Part a) follows from the meromorphic Fredholm theorem (see
[
31
, Proposition 1.1.8]
) upon proving that
R\mapsto Q(R)\in\mathbb{B}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))
is holomorphic with values in the set of Fredholm operators. We can write
Q(R)
as the integral operator with Schwartz kernel

(19)

\frac{1}{R}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}=\frac{\chi(x,y)}{R}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{R^{k}}{(k+1)!}\chi(x,y)\mathrm{d}(x,y)^{k+1}.

Using the fact that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal, an argument as in the proof of Theorem
2.9
shows that
Q(R)
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator of order
-n-1
. By Proposition
A.1
, the principal symbol of
Q(R)
(for fixed
R
) is given by

\sigma_{-n-1}(Q(R))(x,\xi)=\pi^{(n-1)/2}2^{n}\Gamma\left(\frac{n+1}{2}\right)|\xi|_{g_{G}}^{-n-1}=n!\omega_{n}|\xi|^{-n-1}_{g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}}.

Therefore
R\mapsto Q(R)\in\mathbb{B}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))
takes values in the set of Fredholm operators. The expression (
19
), and again an argument as in the proof of Theorem
2.9
, shows that
R\mapsto Q(R)\in\mathbb{B}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))
depends holomorphically on
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Part b) follows from the Gårding inequality (see Theorem
A.3
on page
A.3
) using that
\mathrm{Re}(Q)
has positive principal symbol as a pseudodifferential operator with parameter by Theorem
2.9
on page
2.9
).
∎

2.5.
Evaluation at
\xi=0
of some symbols

For later purposes, we will be interested in knowing the value of the homogeneous component of the full symbol of the parametrix of
Q
at
\xi=0
, constructed as in Theorem
2.20
. The following lemma shows that the evaluations of symbols of operators with parameter provides coordinate invariant expressions, therefore containing invariants of a pseudodifferential operator with parameter.

Lemma 2.23
.

Assume that
M
is a manifold and that
A\in\Psi^{m}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)
is a properly supported pseudodifferential operator with parameter. Then there exists a sequence
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M\times\Gamma)
such that

i)

Each
a_{j,0}=a_{j,0}(x,R)
is homogeneous of degree
m-j
in
R
.

ii)

In each local coordinate chart,
a_{j,0}(x,R)=a_{j}(x,0,R)

where
a\sim\sum_{j}a_{j}
is a homogeneous expansion of the full symbol of
A
in that chart.

Moreover, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

(20)

[A(R)1](x)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}(x,R)+r_{N}(x,R)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}(x,1)R^{m-j}+r_{N}(x,R),

where
r_{N}\in C^{\infty}(M\times\Gamma)
is a function such that for any compact
K\subseteq M
it holds that

\sup_{x\in K}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{R}^{k}r_{N}(x,R)|=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{m-N+|\alpha|+k}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$}.

Proof.

Choose a partition of unity
(\chi_{j})_{j}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
subordinate to a locally finite covering by coordinate charts, and choose
(\tilde{\chi}_{j})_{j}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{c}(M)
such that
\tilde{\chi}_{j}
is supported in a coordinate chart and
\tilde{\chi}_{j}=1
on
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{j})
. We have that
\sum_{j}\chi_{j}A\tilde{\chi}_{j}
converges in weak sense to a properly supported operator, and
A-\sum_{j}\chi_{j}A\tilde{\chi}_{j}\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)
. Therefore, we can assume that
A
is supported in a coordinate chart. In a coordinate chart, and a homogeneous expansion
a\sim\sum_{j}a_{j}
of the full symbol of
A
, the method of stationary phase (see for instance
[
24
, Chapter VII.7]
) implies that
[A(R)1](x)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{j}(x,0,R)+r_{N}(x,R)
as in Equation (
20
). It is clear that that the function
A(R)1\in C^{\infty}(M)
and its asymptotic expansion is independent of choice of coordinates, and the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold,
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
be a function regular at the diagonal, and
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{C}_{+})
the sequence defined as in Lemma
2.23
from
A_{G,\chi}\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
(cf. Theorem
2.20
). The functions
a_{j,0}
are determined in local coordinates by the properties that

a_{j,0}(x,R)=0,\quad\mbox{for all $j$ odd},

and for even
j
,
a_{j,0}(x,R)
is determined inductively from
a_{0,0}(x,R)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1}
and for
j>0
,

(21)

a_{j,0}(x,R)=-\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+2l+p=j\\
2l<j,\,2|k+p\end{subarray}}i^{p}q_{k,p}(x,R).\nabla_{x}^{p}a_{j-2k,0}(x,R),

where
q_{k,p}(x,R)
denotes the
p
-linear form

q_{k,p}(x,R).v:=\sum_{|\alpha|=p}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{k}(x,\xi,R)v^{\alpha}|_{\xi=0}=\partial_{t}^{p}q_{k}(x,tv,R)|_{t=0}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.23
, we can perform all computations in a coordinate chart. The structural description of
a_{j}
from Lemma
2.20
implies that for any
(x,\xi,R)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
it holds that

a_{j}(x,-\xi,R)=(-1)^{j}a_{j}(x,\xi,R).

We conclude that
a_{j}(x,0,R)=0
, and even that
\nabla_{x}^{p}a_{j,0}(x,R)=0
for any
p
, when
j
is odd. To compute
a_{j}
for even
j
, we note that since
a_{j}=-a_{0}\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j,\,l<j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{k}D_{x}^{\alpha}a_{l}
, the formula (
21
) follows using that the only contributions are for even
l
, and evenness of
j
implies that
2|k+p
.

∎

Remark 2.25
.

The formulas in Theorem
2.9
shows that for
n
odd, and
j
even,

\displaystyle q_{j,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle R^{-n-1-j}\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j},\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)<(n+1)/2}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-1}\left(|\gamma|/2\right)!(n+1-2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma))_{|\gamma|/2,-2}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes|\gamma|/2})+

\displaystyle-R^{-n-1-j}\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j},\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)\geq(n+1)/2}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-1}\left(|\gamma|/2\right)!(n+1-2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma))_{|\gamma|/2-1,-2}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes|\gamma|/2}),

and for
n
even, and
j
even,

\displaystyle q_{j,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle R^{-n-1-j}\sum_{\gamma\in I_{j}}\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}(-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma)-1}\left(|\gamma|/2\right)!(n+1-2\mathrm{rk}(\gamma))_{|\gamma|/2,-2}C_{G}^{(\gamma)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes|\gamma|/2}),

and
\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{rk}(\gamma),n}
is as in Theorem
2.9
.
Note that by definition of the set
I_{j}
,
\gamma\in I_{j}
satisfies that
|\gamma|
is even if and only if
j
is even.

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional manifold,
G:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
be a function regular at the diagonal, and
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M\times\mathbb{C}_{+})
the restriction to
0
of the full symbol of
A_{G,\chi}
. Then for each
j>0
,
a_{j,0}
is a polynomial in
(C^{(\gamma)}_{G})_{\gamma\in\cup_{k\leq j}I_{k}}
and its derivatives contracted by the metric
g_{G}
and its derivatives of total degree
j
where each
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}
,
\gamma\in I_{k}
, has degree
k
, the metric has degree zero and
x
-derivatives increase the order by
1
.

In the special case
j=0
we have

a_{0,0}(x,R)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1},

and for
j=2
and
n=3
, we have that

\displaystyle a_{2,0}(x,R)=-\frac{24R^{2}}{(3!\omega_{3})^{2}}\bigg(10\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}C^{4}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})-\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}(C^{3}_{G}\otimes C^{3}_{G})(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})\bigg),

while for
j=2
and
n\neq 1,3
, we have that

(22)

\displaystyle a_{2,0}(x,R)=-\frac{3R^{n-1}}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\bigg(\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)

\displaystyle C^{4}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})-

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}(C^{3}_{G}\otimes C^{3}_{G})(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})\bigg),

and for for
j=4
we have the identity

\displaystyle a_{4,0}(x,R)=-\frac{R^{2n+2}}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}

\displaystyle q_{4,0}(x,R)-\frac{n+1}{R^{2}}g_{G}(\nabla^{2}_{x}a_{2,0}(x,R))-

(23)

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{R^{2}n!\omega_{n}}(C^{3}_{G}\otimes\nabla_{x}a_{2,0})(g_{G}\otimes g_{G})-\frac{R^{n+1}}{n!\omega_{n}}q_{2,0}(x,R)a_{2,0}(x,R).

Proof.

The structural statement about
a_{j,0}(x,R)
is readily deduced from Lemma
2.24
and induction by using the property that if
\gamma\in I_{j}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in I_{j^{\prime}}
then
(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\in I_{j+j^{\prime}}
.

The equation for
a_{0,0}
is immediate from Lemma
2.20
. Using Lemma
2.24
, we have that
a_{2,0}(x,R)=-a_{0,0}^{2}q_{2,0}
and the formula (
22
) follows from that

\displaystyle q_{2,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle 3\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)C^{4}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})R^{-n-3}+{3}\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{5,-2}(C^{3}_{G}\otimes C^{3}_{G})(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})R^{-n-3},

for
n\neq 1,3
and a similar expression for
n=3
.

Let us compute
a_{4,0}(x,R)
. By Lemma
2.24
we have that

\displaystyle a_{4,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle-\frac{R^{n+1}}{n!\omega_{n}}\bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{4}i^{-k}q_{k,4-k}(x,R).\nabla_{x}^{4-k}\frac{R^{n+1}}{n!\omega_{n}}-\sum_{k=0}^{2}i^{-k}q_{k,2-k}(x,R).\nabla_{x}^{2-k}a_{2,0}(x,R)\bigg)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{R^{n+1}}{n!\omega_{n}}\bigg(q_{0,2}(x,R).\nabla_{x}^{2}a_{2,0}(x,R)-iq_{1,1}(x,R).\nabla_{x}a_{2,0}(x,R)-q_{2,0}(x,R)a_{2,0}(x,R)\bigg)-

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{R^{2n+2}}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}q_{4,0}(x,R),

and the computation is complete upon using Proposition
2.13
.
∎

Remark 2.27
.

The full expression for
a_{4,0}
can be computed from Equation (
23
). We omit the full details, but let us note an expression for
q_{4,0}
. Since

I_{4}=\{6,(3,5),(4,4),(5,3),(3,3,4),(3,4,3),(4,3,3),(3,3,3,3)\},

we have that

\displaystyle q_{4,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}3!(n-1)_{3,-2}C_{G}^{6}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 3})-R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}4!(n-3)_{4,-2}C_{G}^{(3,5)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 4})-

\displaystyle-R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}4!(n-3)_{4,-2}C_{G}^{(4,4)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 4})-R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}4!(n-3)_{4,-2}C_{G}^{(5,3)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 4})+

\displaystyle+R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{3,n}5!(n-5)_{5,-2}C_{G}^{(3,3,4)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 5})+R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{3,n}5!(n-5)_{5,-2}C_{G}^{(3,4,3)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 5})+

\displaystyle+R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{3,n}5!(n-5)_{5,-2}C_{G}^{(4,3,3)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 5})-R^{-n-5}\mathfrak{c}_{4,n}6!(n-7)_{6,-2}C_{G}^{(3,3,3,3)}(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 6}).

Example 2.28

(Evaluations of symbols for domains in Euclidean space)
.

Let us return to the computations on Euclidean space from Example
2.15
. We consider
G(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
– the square of the Euclidean distance. Since
q(x,\xi,R)=n!\omega_{n}(R^{2}+|\xi|^{2})^{-(n+1)/2}
is a full symbol of
Q_{G,\chi}
in Euclidean coordinates,
a(x,\xi,R)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}(R^{2}+|\xi|^{2})^{(n+1)/2}
is a full symbol of
A_{G,\chi}
. Therefore

(24)

a_{j,0}(x,R)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1},\;&j=0,\\
0,\;&j>0.\end{cases}

By Lemma
2.24
this holds in any coordinate system on Euclidean space. We remark that the bulk of computations carried out in Example
2.15
will mainly be of interest when inverting
Q
near the boundary of a domain, while the computation (
24
) relates to interior terms.

Example 2.29

(Evaluations of symbols for submanifolds of Euclidean space)
.

We return to submanifolds
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the function
G(x,y)=|x-y|^{2}
which is regular at the diagonal as in Example
2.16
above. We take coordinates as in Example
2.16
. By Theorem
2.26
we have that
a_{0,0}(x,R)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1}
and

\displaystyle a_{2,0}(x,R)=

\displaystyle-\frac{3\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+k=4,\\
i,k>0\end{subarray}}(\nabla^{i}\varphi_{l}\otimes\nabla^{k}\varphi_{l})(g_{G}^{\otimes 2})R^{n-1}-

\displaystyle-\frac{3\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)_{3,-2}}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\sum_{l_{1},l_{2}=n+1}^{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}+k_{1}=i_{2}+k_{2}=3,\\
i_{1},i_{2},k_{1},k_{2}>0\end{subarray}}(\nabla^{i_{1}}\varphi_{l_{1}}\otimes\nabla^{k_{1}}\varphi_{l_{1}}\otimes\nabla^{i_{2}}\varphi_{l_{2}}\otimes\nabla^{k_{2}}\varphi_{l_{2}})(x,g_{G}^{\otimes 3})R^{n-1},

where
g_{G}
is the dual metric to the transversal Hessian:

H_{G}(v)=|v|^{2}+\sum_{l=n+1}^{N}(\nabla\varphi_{l}(x)\cdot v)^{2}.

Example 2.30

(Evaluations of symbols for geodesic distances)
.

Consider the geodesic distance on a Riemannian manifold
M
as in Example
2.17
. Let
g_{M}
denote the Riemannian metric, and recall from Example
2.17
that
g_{M}
coincides with the transversal Hessian of
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}^{2}
at the diagonal. We can compute
a_{2,0}
in this case by means of known Riemannian curvatures. We fix a point
x
and choose coordinates so that
\Gamma
vanishes in that point (normal coordinates). In these coordinates,
C^{3}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}_{\rm geo}}(x,v)=0
and
C^{4}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}_{\rm geo}}(x,\cdot)
is a third of the Riemannian curvature in
x
by Proposition
2.18
. By Theorem
2.26
we conclude that for
n=3
, we have
a_{2,0}(x,R)=-\frac{80R^{2}}{(3!\omega_{3})^{2}}s_{g}(x)
,
while for
n\neq 1,3
, we have that

a_{2,0}(x,R)=-\frac{R^{n-1}}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)s_{g}(x),

where
s_{g}
denotes the scalar curvature.

3.
Global behavior of
\mathcal{Z}
on compact manifolds

In the previous section we studied the localization
Q
of
\mathcal{Z}
near the diagonal. Here
\mathcal{Z}
is the operator with parameter
R
defined from a distance function as in Equation (
6
). We now turn to study
\mathcal{Z}
by considering distance functions for which
L:=\mathcal{Z}-Q
in a certain sense is negligible so that
Q
dominates.

3.1.
Controlling the off-diagonal part of
\mathcal{Z}

In this subsection we shall study the remainder
L=\mathcal{Z}-Q
. We note that

L(R)f(x)=\frac{1}{R}\int_{M}(1-\chi(x,y))\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}f(y)\mathrm{d}y.

We start making two initial observations concerning the remainder term
L
. The first observation concerns the behavior of the remainder term in
L^{2}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function on
M
. Then
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\ni R\mapsto L(R)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(L^{2}(M))

defines a holomorphic Hilbert-Schmidt valued function. Moreover,
L
satisfies that

\|L(R)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(L^{2}(M))}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty})\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$.}

By the standard norm estimate
\|K\|_{\mathbb{B}}\leq\|K\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}
, the statement in the proposition also holds in the operator norm. We remark that by Theorem
2.9
and
[
39
, Theorem 9.1]
, the localization to the diagonal satisfies
\|Q(R)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{p}(L^{2}(M))}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{\frac{n}{p}-n-1})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
, for any
p>1+1/n
(the bound is not uniform in
p
).

Proof.

The function
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\ni R\mapsto\frac{1}{R}(1-\chi(\cdot,\cdot))\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(\cdot,\cdot)}\in C(M\times M)
is clearly holomorphic in the norm on
C(M\times M)
. Since
\chi=1
in a neighborhood of the diagonal, compactness of
M
implies that
\|\frac{1}{R}(1-\chi(\cdot,\cdot))\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(\cdot,\cdot)}\|_{C(M\times M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
. Since
M
is compact, an integral operator
K
with kernel
k\in C(M\times M)
satisfies the estimate for the Hilbert-Schmidt norm
\|K\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(L^{2}(M))}\leq\mathrm{vol}(M)\|k\|_{C(M\times M)}
. The proposition follows.
∎

The second observation concerns how an a priori estimate with respect to the off-diagonal remainder
L
affects distributional solutions to the magnitude equation
\mathcal{Z}u=1
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function on
M
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Gamma
be a sector. Assume that
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and that
(u_{R})_{R\in\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
is a family of solutions to

\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=f

satisfying that
\langle L(R)u_{R},\psi\rangle=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-N})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
in
\Gamma
for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Then for large enough
\mathrm{Re}(R)
,

u_{R}=Q(R)^{-1}f+v_{R},

where
(v_{R})_{R\in\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
is a family satisfying that
\langle v_{R},\psi\rangle=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-N+n+1})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
in
\Gamma
for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(M)
. In particular, for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

\langle u_{R},\psi\rangle=\langle Q(R)^{-1}f,\psi\rangle+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-N+n+1}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma$}.

Proof.

The equation
\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=f
implies that
u_{R}=Q(R)^{-1}f-Q(R)^{-1}L(R)u_{R}
.
Consider the distribution
v_{R}:=-Q(R)^{-1}L(R)u_{R}\equiv u_{R}-Q(R)^{-1}f
. For
\psi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have that

\langle v_{R},\psi\rangle=\langle L(R)u_{R},Q(R)^{-1}\psi\rangle=O(R^{-N+n+1}),

because of the assumption on
L(R)u_{R}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-N})
in a weak sense and the fact that
Q(R)^{-1}
is a pseudodifferential operator with parameter of order
n+1
preserving
C^{\infty}(M)
with uniform norm estimates
\|Q(R)^{-1}f\|_{C^{k}}\leq C_{k}(1+\mathrm{Re}(R))^{n+1}\|f\|_{C^{n+k+1}}
.
∎

Proposition
3.1
implies that
L
is small as an operator on
L^{2}
, and Proposition
3.2
implies that the resulting remainder term will not alter the asymptotic properties of solutions given an a priori estimate. However, as
Q
is of negative order and acts compactly on
L^{2}
, well-posedness of the problem in
L^{2}
is not assured. We shall circumvent this problem by imposing a regularity assumption on the distance function that forces the magnitude equation naturally into a Sobolev space framework. We discuss examples satisfying this assumption, as well as counterexamples, below in the Subsections
3.2
and
3.4
, respectively.

Definition 3.3

(Property (MR) of distance functions)
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function on
M
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. Set
\mu:=(n+1)/2
. For a sector
[1,\infty)\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we say that
\mathrm{d}
has
property (MR)
on
\Gamma
if for any
R\in\Gamma
,
L(R)
extends to a continuous mapping
H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M)
with

\|L(R)\|_{H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma$}.

If
L(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M)
is a compact operator for
R\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma\ni R\mapsto L(R)\in\mathbb{K}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))

is holomorphic in norm sense, we say that
\mathrm{d}
has
property (SMR)
on
\Gamma
.

The acronyms MR and SMR stand for magnitude regularity and strong magnitude regularity, respectively. Assuming these properties, the operator
\mathcal{Z}
inherits relevant analytic and geometric properties from
Q
. Property (MR) will be used to compute asymptotic solutions to the magnitude equation
R\mathcal{Z}u=1
, while property (SMR) will be used for constructing meromorphic extensions of
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}
. If
\Gamma
is a sector on which a distance function has property (MR), it is clear that
0\notin\Gamma
. We consider such results for compact manifolds below in Subsection
3.3
.

3.2.
Examples of distance functions satisfying property (MR) and (SMR)

Let us give a method to produce distance functions with property (SMR):

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold embedded into a Riemannian manifold
i:M\to W
such that the square of its geodesic distance
\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},W}^{2}
is smooth, for instance
W=\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
W=\mathbb{H}_{N,\mathbb{R}}
or
W=\mathbb{H}_{N,\mathbb{C}}
, for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The distance function
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
,
\mathrm{d}(x,y):=\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},W}(i(x),i(y))
satisfies

i)

\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on
M\times M
and regular at the diagonal.

ii)

L\in\Psi^{-\infty}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
L(R)\in\Psi^{-\infty}(M)
for any
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

iii)

\mathrm{d}
has property (SMR) on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},W}^{2}
is smooth, it is clear that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on
M\times M
. It follows from Proposition
2.18
that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal, and in fact
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is the pullback metric
i^{*}g_{W}
from the Riemannian metric
g_{W}
on
W
. Therefore the singular support support of
\mathrm{d}
is the diagonal, and the integral kernel of
L(R)
is smooth so
L(R)
extends to a continuous mapping
L(R):H^{s}(M)\to H^{t}(M)
for any
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for any vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
on
M\times M
we can estimate

\left|X_{1}\cdots X_{m}\left((1-\chi)\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}}\right)\right|\leq C_{m}|R|^{m-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon\mathrm{Re}(R)}.

Again, we write
\varepsilon:=\inf\{\mathrm{d}(x,y):(x,y)\in\chi^{-1}(1)\}>0
. In particular, we readily can deduce that
L\in\Psi^{-\infty}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
, and for any
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\|L(R)\|_{H^{s}(M)\to H^{t}(M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as}\quad\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty.

Therefore
\mathrm{d}
has property (SMR) on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
∎

In light of Proposition
3.4
, we note the following corollary of Theorem
2.22
.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold equipped with a distance function
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth, e.g. a subspace distance as in Proposition
3.4
. Then
\mathcal{Z}(R)\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M)
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator for any
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{Z}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
is elliptic with parameter and its full symbol coincides with that of
Q
as given in Theorem
2.9
.

Another example of distance functions with property (MR) arises on spheres.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathrm{d}
denote the geodesic distance on a sphere
S^{n}
in its round metric. The distance function
\mathrm{d}
has property (MR) on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
but fails to satisfy property (SMR) on any sector.

Proof.

The square of the geodesic distance is regular at the diagonal by Proposition
2.18
. We note that
\mathrm{d}
is smooth on
\{(x,y)\in S^{n}\times S^{n}:x\neq\pm y\}
. Consider the operator
Uf(x):=f(-x)
. The operator
U
acts via pullback along the antipodal mapping
\varphi(x):=-x
which acts isometrically due to
O(n)
-invariance of the Riemannian metric on
S^{n}
. The operator
U
extends to a unitary on all Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(S^{n})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Since it holds that
\mathrm{d}(x,y)=\pi-\mathrm{d}(x,\varphi(y))
we can conclude that the integral kernel of
L(R)U
is given by
\chi_{\varphi}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi R}
, where
\chi_{\varphi}(x,y):=1-\chi(x,\varphi(y))
. In particular, since
\chi_{\varphi}
satisfies that
\chi_{\varphi}=1
on the diagonal and
\chi_{\varphi}=0
on a neighborhood of the off-diagonal singularities of
\mathrm{d}
, the operator
L(R)U\mathrm{e}^{\pi R}
is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter of order
-n-1
by the same argument as in Theorem
2.9
. It follows that property (SMR) fails on any sector. Using that
U
is unitary,
L(R)
extends to a continuous operator
H^{-\mu}(S^{n})\to H^{\mu}(S^{n})
with
\|L(R)U\mathrm{e}^{\pi R}\|_{H^{-\mu}(S^{n})\to H^{\mu}(S^{n})}=O(1)
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty
. Since
U
is unitary, we deduce that

\|L(R)\|_{H^{-\mu}(S^{n})\to H^{\mu}(S^{n})}=O(\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{Re}(R)})=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty})\quad\mbox{as}\quad\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty.

∎

3.3.
Analytic results for the operator
\mathcal{Z}

We are now ready to extend the results of Section
2
for
Q
to results on the operator
\mathcal{Z}
for compact manifolds with a distance function satisfying property (MR) as defined in Definition
3.3
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function on
M
with property (MR) on the sector
\Gamma
. Set
\mu:=(n+1)/2
. Then there is an
R_{0}\geq 0
such that

\mathcal{Z}(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M),

is invertible for all
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Moreover, for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})

\mathcal{Z}^{-1}=Q^{-1}+\mathcal{R},

where
Q^{-1}\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0}))
is the elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter constructed in Corollary
2.21
and
\mathcal{R}:H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M)
is a family of operators parametrized by
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
such that

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})$}.

Moreover, there is a constant
C>0
such that

(25)

C^{-1}\|f\|_{H^{-\mu}_{|R|}(M)}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Re}\langle f,\mathcal{Z}(R)f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{-\mu}_{|R|}(M)}^{2},

for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and
f\in H^{-\mu}(M)
. In particular, for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
,
\mathcal{Z}(R)
is coercive in form sense on
L^{2}(M)
for the
H^{-\mu}
-norm.

Proof.

The operator
Q
is invertible by Corollary
2.21
. We can therefore write

Q^{-1}\mathcal{Z}=1+Q^{-1}L,

as operators on
H^{-\mu}(M)
. Since
Q^{-1}
is a pseudodifferential operator with parameter, we have that

(26)

\displaystyle\|Q(R)^{-1}L(R)\|_{H^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(M)\to H^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}(M)}

\displaystyle=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),

\displaystyle\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})$}.

Therefore, for
\mathrm{Re}(R)\gg 0
the operator
(1+Q^{-1}L)^{-1}Q^{-1}
exists and is a left inverse to
\mathcal{Z}
. By an analogous argument,

(27)

\displaystyle\|L(R)Q(R)^{-1}\|_{H^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(M)\to H^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(M)}

\displaystyle=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),

as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
in
\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
, and
\mathcal{Z}
has the right inverse
Q^{-1}(1+LQ^{-1})^{-1}
for
\mathrm{Re}(R)\gg 0
. Therefore
(1+Q^{-1}L)^{-1}Q^{-1}=Q^{-1}(1+LQ^{-1})^{-1}
and this operator is an inverse to
\mathcal{Z}
. By the estimates (
26
) and (
27
), it follows that

\mathcal{R}=Z^{-1}-Q^{-1}=Q^{-1}\left((1+LQ^{-1})^{-1}-1\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}Q^{-1}(LQ^{-1})^{k},

as a norm convergent sum and has the required decay property as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
.

The estimate (
25
) follows from the decay property of
\mathcal{R}
and Theorem
2.22
.
∎

The following result is immediate from Lemma
2.23
and Theorem
3.7
.

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
M
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold and
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
a distance function on
M
with property (MR) on the sector
\Gamma
. Take the sequence of homogeneous functions
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M\times\Gamma\cap\mathbb{C}_{+})
as in Lemma
2.24
. Then, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

[\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}1](x)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}(x,R)+r_{N}(x,R),

where
r_{N}\in C(\Gamma\cap\mathbb{C}_{+},H^{-\mu}(M))
, for
\mu=(n+1)/2
, is a function such that

\|r_{N}(\cdot,R)\|_{H^{-\mu}(M)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{n+1-N}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma$}.

Remark 3.9
.

We remark that the role that property (MR) plays in Corollary
3.8
is to ensure existence of a distributional solution to
\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=1
. For
computing
the integrated asymptotics
\langle 1,\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}1\rangle
, property (MR) is not necessary. Indeed, with no assumptions of property (MR), but assuming that
\mathrm{d}:M\times M\to[0,\infty)
is a distance function on
M
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal and that
(u_{R})_{R>R_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
is a family of solutions to

\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=1

satisfying that
\langle L(R)u_{R},\psi\rangle=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-N})
as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty
for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have that

\langle u_{R},1\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{M}a_{j}(x,R)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{n+1-N}),

for any
N
. This follows from Lemma
2.23
and Proposition
3.2
.

One instance where solutions to
\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=1
exist, yet the distance function need not satisfy property (MR), is the geodesic distance on a compact symmetric space
M=G/H
. See Proposition
3.17
below for examples of symmetric spaces failing to satisfy property (MR). The problem
\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=1
was studied for compact symmetric spaces in
[
45
]
. For a compact symmetric space
M=G/H
, we use the normalized
G
-invariant measure induced by the Haar measure. By symmetry, the function

u_{R}(x)=\frac{1}{\int_{G/H}\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)}\mathrm{d}y},

is constant and therefore solves
\mathcal{Z}(R)u_{R}=1
. It is readily verified that
L(R)u_{R}=O(R^{-\infty})
in distributional sense. We conclude that each
a_{j}(x,R)
is constant and that

u_{R}(x)=u_{R}(eH)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_{j}(eH,R)+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{n+1-N}),

for any
N
. For examples of computations of
u_{R}
for compact symmetric spaces, see
[
45
]
.

The next result poses an obstruction to property (MR) for distance functions and should be viewed as complementary to Theorem
3.16
. Recall the following terminology from
[
33
]
: a compact metric space
(X,\mathrm{d})
is said to be positive definite if for any finite subset
F\subseteq X
, the matrix
(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{d}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
is positive definite. If
(X,R\mathrm{d})
is positive definite for all
R>0
, we say that
(X,\mathrm{d})
is stably positive definite.

Corollary 3.10
.

Assume that
\mathrm{d}
is a distance function on a compact manifold
M
with property (MR) on
[1,\infty)
. Then there exists an
R_{0}\geq 0
such that
(M,R\mathrm{d})
is positive definite for all
R>R_{0}
.

Proof.

Consider the quadratic form
\mathfrak{q}_{R}(u)=\langle u,\mathcal{Z}(R)u\rangle_{L^{2}}
,
u\in H^{-\mu}(M)
. By Theorem
3.7
,
\mathfrak{q}_{R}
is positive definite for
R>R_{0}
, for some
R_{0}\geq 0
. In particular, for any subspace
V\subseteq H^{-\mu}(M)
the restriction of
\mathfrak{q}_{R}
to
V
is also positive definite for
R>R_{0}
.
For a finite subset
F\subseteq M
, consider the subspace
V_{F}\subseteq H^{-(\mu}(M)
spanned by
\{\delta_{x}:x\in F\}
. In the basis
(\delta_{x})_{x\in F}
, the quadratic form
\mathfrak{q}_{R}|_{V}
is represented by the
|F|\times|F|
-matrix
(\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
and so it is positive definite for
R>R_{0}
.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

It was proven in
[
33
, Subsection 3.2]
that if
M
is a compact Riemannian manifold with
\pi_{1}(M)\neq 0
, the geodesic distance is not stably positive definite, i.e. there exists an
R>0
and a finite subset
F\subseteq M
such that
(\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
fails to be positive definite. The manifold
M=S^{1}
is not simply connected, and therefore fails to be stably positive definite. but nevertheless Proposition
3.6
implies that
M=S^{1}
with its geodesic distance has property (MR). Therefore, property (MR) does not imply stably positive definiteness of the metric space but only an asymptotic version thereof.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
having property (SMR) on
\Gamma
and set
\mu=(n+1)/2
. Then the operator

\mathcal{Z}(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M),

is a well defined Fredholm operator for all
R\in\Gamma
invertible for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Moreover, the operator
\mathcal{Z}(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M)
depends holomorphically on
R
in
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}:H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M)
depends holomorphically on
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and admits a meromorphic extension to
\Gamma
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.22
,
Q
is a holomorphic function on
\Gamma
with values in the Fredholm operators and by property (SMR),
L
is a compact valued holomorphic function on
\Gamma
. Therefore
\mathcal{Z}
defines a holomorphic function on
\Gamma
with values in the Fredholm operators. Since
\mathcal{Z}
is invertible for a large enough
R
, see Theorem
3.7
, the theorem follows from the meromorphic Fredholm theorem, see
[
31
, Proposition 1.1.8]
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

To ensure holomorphicity of
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{-1}
on sectors, the full property (SMR) is not needed. Indeed, if
\mathrm{d}
has property (MR) on a sector
\Gamma
and
\Gamma\ni R\mapsto L(R)\in\mathbb{B}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))
is additionally holomorphic (in norm sense) then by Theorem
3.7
, for some
R_{0}\geq 0
, the mapping

\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})\ni R\mapsto\mathcal{Z}(R)\in\mathbb{B}(H^{-\mu}(M),H^{\mu}(M))

is a holomorphic family of invertible operators. These properties are inherited by its inverse,

\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})\ni R\mapsto\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}\in\mathbb{B}(H^{\mu}(M),H^{-\mu}(M)).

By the proof of Proposition
3.6
, this discussion applies to
S^{n}
showing that for some
R_{0}
,
\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}
is holomorphic for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
in this case.

The following result follows from Corollary
3.5
and Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on
M\times M
and regular at the diagonal (cf. Proposition
3.4
), then
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(R):H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{\mu}(M)
depends holomorphically on
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and the operator
\mathcal{Z}(R)^{-1}:H^{\mu}(M)\to H^{-\mu}(M)
extends meromorphically to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

3.4.
Examples of distance functions that fail to satisfy property (MR)

Property (MR) of a distance function, as defined in Definition
3.3
, is a notable restriction on the singular support and singularity structure of the distance function. We remark here that by a singular point, we mean any point in the singular support, i.e. one in which the function is not
C^{\infty}
. To better understand how these singularities affect the operator theoretic properties of
L
, the reader is encouraged to review the proof of Proposition
3.6
where a crucial feature used in the proof is that the geodesic distance on spheres near an off-diagonal singularity has the same singular features as it has near the antipode of the singularity. An important property used there can be stated as having control of the dimension of the off-diagonal singular support of the metric. We make an elementary observation that follows from the smoothness of the function
(0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\sqrt{t}\in(0,\infty)
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\mathrm{d}
be a distance function on a manifold
M
. Then it holds that

\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{M}=\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d}^{2})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{M}.

Let us give a sufficient condition for a distance function (regular at the diagonal)
not
to satisfy property (MR). We note that an additional obstruction was provided above in Proposition
3.10
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
M
be a compact manifold and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. Assume that there exists a submanifold
N\subseteq M\times M
with
N\subseteq\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d}^{2})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{M}
such that any point
z_{0}\in N
admits a neighborhood
U_{0}
in
M\times M
and a coordinate chart

\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{\dim(N)}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{\dim(M)-\dim(N)}_{s}\to U_{0},

with
\varphi(0)=x_{0}
,
\varphi^{-1}(U_{0}\cap N)=\mathbb{R}^{\dim(N)}_{t}\times\{0\}
and
(t,s)\mapsto\varphi^{*}\mathrm{d}(t,s)-|s|
being smooth in a neighborhood of
0
.
Then
L(R)
does not extend to a continuous operator

H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{s}(M),

for any
s>(3\dim(M)+1)/2-\dim(N)
where
\mu=(\dim(M)+1)/2
. In particular, if
\dim(N)>\dim(M)
then
\mathrm{d}
does not have property (MR) (see Definition
3.3
) on any sector containing a half-ray
[R_{0},\infty)
.

Proof.

We pick a point
(x_{0},y_{0})\in N
and neighborhoods
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
of
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
respectively, such that there exists a coordinate chart
\varphi
as in item ii) above on
U_{0}=U_{1}\times U_{2}
. Pick functions
\chi_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U_{1})
and
\chi_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U_{2})
such that
\chi_{1}=1
near
x_{0}
,
\chi_{2}=1
near
y_{0}
and such that
(t,s)\mapsto\varphi^{*}\mathrm{d}(t,s)-|s|
is smooth on
\varphi^{-1}(\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{1})\times\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{2}))
. Clearly, it suffices to prove that for any
R>0
, the operator
\chi_{1}L(R)\chi_{2}
does not extend to a continuous operator
H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{s}(M)
for any
s>(3\dim(M)+1)/2-\dim(N)
.

Set
k=\dim(N)
and
n=\dim(M)
. The Schwartz kernel of
\chi_{1}L(R)\chi_{2}
is a distribution on
M\times M
supported in
U_{0}=U_{1}\times U_{2}
and pulling this kernel back along
\varphi
, we arrive at the distribution

K(t,s)=\chi_{0}(s,t)\mathrm{e}^{-R|s|},

where
\chi_{0}=\varphi^{*}[(\chi_{1}\otimes\chi_{2})(1-\chi)]\mathrm{e}^{-R\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
for
\psi(t,s)=\varphi^{*}\mathrm{d}(t,s)-|s|
. It follows from Proposition
A.1
and combining a Taylor expansion with asymptotic completeness, that
K\in CI^{k-2n-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathbb{R}^{k})
. We conclude that
\chi_{1}L(R)\chi_{2}
is a Fourier integral operator of order
k-2n-1
and this operator is elliptic in a neighborhood of
(x_{0},y_{0})
. Therefore, since
\chi_{1}L(R)\chi_{2}
is elliptic near
(x_{0},y_{0})
of order
k-2n-1
it does not extend to a continuous operator
H^{-(n+1)/2}(M)\to H^{s}(M)
for any
s>-(n+1)/2-(k-2n-1)=(3n+1)/2-k
.
∎

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
n>1
. The geodesic distance on the
n
-dimensional torus
M=\mathbb{T}^{n}
or the real projective space
M=\mathbb{R}P^{n}
fails to satisfy property (MR) (see Definition
3.3
) on any sector containing a half-ray
[R_{0},\infty)
. In fact,
L(R)
does not extend to a continuous map
H^{-\mu}(M)\to H^{s}(M)
for
s>-n/2+3/2
.

Proof.

The proofs for both cases follow the same lines and rely on Theorem
3.16
. For
\mathbb{R}P^{n}
we give a more geometric argument, and for
\mathbb{T}^{n}
we give a coordinate oriented argument.

We first prove the result for real projective space. The projective space
\mathbb{R}P^{n}
is the quotient of
S^{n}
by the antipodal map
x\mapsto-x
. This quotient map is a covering map, and it is locally isometric with respect to the geodesic distance. For
x\in\mathbb{R}P^{n}
, the equator
\tilde{E}(x)\subseteq S^{n}
is defined by

\tilde{E}(x)=\{v\in S^{n}:v\cdot x=0\}.

The condition
v\cdot x=0
being invariant under the antipodal map,
\tilde{E}(x)
only depends on
x\in\mathbb{R}P^{n}
and not on a choice of pre-image of
x
in
S^{n}
. We let
E(x)\subseteq\mathbb{R}P^{n}
denote the image under the quotient map.
The off-diagonal singular support of the geodesic distance on
\mathbb{R}P^{n}
is the set

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}P^{n}\times\mathbb{R}P^{n}:y\in E(x)\}.

Indeed, any geodesic in
\mathbb{R}P^{n}
from a point
x
lifts uniquely up to a geodesic on
S^{n}
up until the point it crosses
E(x)
where the geodesic distance
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}(x,\cdot)
has a kink. The projection mapping
p_{1}:\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathbb{R}P^{n}}\to\mathbb{R}P^{n}
is a locally trivial
\mathbb{R}P^{n-1}
-bundle on
\mathbb{R}P^{n}
. Therefore,
N=\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathbb{R}P^{n}}\to\mathbb{R}P^{n}
is a manifold of dimension
2n-1>n
and suitable local trivializations of
p_{1}:N\to\mathbb{R}P^{n}
satisfy the assumptions of item ii) in Theorem
3.16
. We conclude from Theorem
3.16
that
L(R)
has no bounded extension to an operator
H^{-\mu}(\mathbb{R}P^{n})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{R}P^{n})
for any
s>-n/2+3/2
.

We now prove the result for the
n
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Write
\mathbb{T}^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}/\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. While
\mathbb{T}^{n}
is a symmetric space, i.e.
\mathbb{T}^{n}=G/H
for
G=\mathbb{T}^{n}
and
H=1
, it is instructive to consider
\mathrm{d}(x,y)
for
x=0
. We have that
\mathrm{d}(0,y)=|y|
where we represent
y
by as an element of the fundamental domain
[-1/2,1/2)^{n}
, and
\mathbb{T}^{n}\ni y\mapsto\mathrm{d}(0,y)
as a function on
\mathbb{T}^{n}
is the
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
-periodic extension of
[-1/2,1/2)^{n}\ni y\mapsto|y|
. Therefore
y\mapsto\mathrm{d}(0,y)
has kinks on the image of
\partial\left([-1/2,1/2)^{n}\right)
in
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. In particular, we see that the off-diagonal singular support of
\mathrm{d}
is the set

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}:x-y\in\partial\left([-1/2,1/2)^{n}\right)+\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}.

Consider the submanifold
N_{0}:=\{1/2\}\times(-1/4,1/4)^{n-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and define the
2n-1
-dimensional submanifold

N:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{T}^{n}:x-y\in N_{0}+\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}\subseteq\mathrm{singsupp}(\mathrm{d}^{2})\setminus\mathrm{Diag}_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}.

Consider a point

x_{0}=(x,1/2,y^{\prime})\in N.

On the open ball of radius
1/4
centred at
x_{0}
, we introduce the coordinates
u=x_{1}-y_{1}-1/2
and
t=(t^{\prime},y)
where
t^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}
in terms of standard coordinates
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})
and
y=(y_{1},y^{\prime})
. In these coordinates, we have that

\mathrm{d}(x,y)=\sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{2}-|u|\right)^{2}+|t^{\prime}|^{2}}=\sqrt{-|u|+|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2}}.

By shrinking the neighborhood of
x_{0}
, we can Taylor expand

\displaystyle\sqrt{-|u|+|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2}}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2}}\sqrt{1-\frac{|u|}{|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k}\frac{|u|^{k}}{(|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2})^{k-1}}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2k+1}\frac{|u|^{2k}}{(|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2})^{2k}}}_{g(u,t^{\prime})}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2k}\frac{|u|^{2k}}{(|t^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}+|u|^{2})^{2k-1}}}_{\tilde{g}(u,t^{\prime})}.

The functions
g
and
\tilde{g}
are smooth near
0
. Since
g(0,0)\neq 0
, we can define the new coordinate
s:=u\tilde{g}(u,t^{\prime})
. We conclude that in these coordinates
\mathrm{d}(x,y)-|s|
is smooth. We conclude from Theorem
3.16
that
L(R)
has no bounded extension to an operator
H^{-\mu}(\mathbb{T}^{n})\to H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{n})
for any
s>-n/2+3/2
.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The analytical issues arising from the remainder term
L
reflect fundamental problems in Riemannian geometry.
The singular support of the geodesic distance is akin to the conjugate locus of the Riemannian metric, which in general is hard to describe, see
[
5
,
43
]
and for related technical issues arising in the
X
-ray transform on a Riemannian manifold see
[
22
]
. Furthermore, Theorem
3.16
shows that even when the singular support is a tractable set, i.e. when it looks like a submanifold near some point, dimensional obstructions to property (MR) appear. This gives rise to the analytic problem that the operator
L
in the decomposition
\mathcal{Z}=Q+L
is in general of order higher than
-n-1
in the Sobolev order, while it is infinitely decaying in the parameter
R
. In this case
L
is of higher order than
Q
, which is elliptic with parameter of order
-n-1
and which determines the analytic and geometric properties of
\mathcal{Z}
in this article.

4.
The operator
\mathcal{Z}
on Sobolev spaces for a manifold with boundary

We now turn to compact manifolds with boundary. For simplicity, we tacitly assume that
X
is a compact domain in a manifold
M
and, for the purposes of this section, it suffices to assume that
X
has a
C^{0}
-boundary. We say that
X\subseteq M
is a domain if it coincides with the closure of its interior points. Recall that a domain is said to have
C^{0}
-boundary if its boundary can be realized locally as the graph of a continuous function. We call such spaces
X
a compact manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary. We may then study the operators
\mathcal{Z}
and
Q
in
M
and deduce results in
X
by restriction to distributions supported in
X
. In this section we study analytic properties and meromorphic extensions. Asymptotic properties are studied in the following section under additional regularity assumptions on the boundary. For notational clarity, we indicate the manifold on which an operator is defined by a subscript, e.g.
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{M}
for the corresponding operator on
X
and
M
, respectively.

We shall make use of the following scales of Sobolev spaces. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
, write

\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(X):=\{u\in H^{s}_{R}(M):\mathrm{supp}(u)\subseteq X\},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X):=H^{s}_{R}(M)/\dot{H}^{s}_{R}({\overline{M\setminus X}}).

An approximation argument shows that
C^{\infty}_{c}(X^{\circ})\subseteq\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
is dense for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, see
[
32
, Theorem 3.29]
. We equip these Sobolev spaces with Hilbert space structure induced from
H^{s}_{R}(M)
, i.e.
\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(X)\subseteq H^{s}_{R}(M)
as a subspace and
\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
as a quotient. We call the quotient mapping
H^{s}_{R}(M)\to\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
the restriction mapping because, by duality, it identifies
\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
with a space of distributions in
X^{\circ}
. Indeed, the continuous inclusion map
H^{s}_{R}(M)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M)
induces a continuous map
\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X^{\circ})
by mapping the equivalence class
u+\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(M\setminus X^{\circ})\in\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
to the distribution
u|_{X^{\circ}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X^{\circ})
. The map
\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(X^{\circ})
is a continuous embedding by the following argument. If
u\in H^{s}_{R}(M)
satisfies that
u|_{X^{\circ}}=0
then
\mathrm{supp}(u)\subseteq M\setminus X^{\circ}
and
u+\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(M\setminus X^{\circ})=0+\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(M\setminus X^{\circ})
defines the zero class in
\overline{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
.

We note that for
s=0
,
\dot{H}^{0}_{R}(X)=\overline{H}^{0}_{R}(X)=L^{2}(X)
. The
L^{2}
-pairing between
\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(X)
and
\overline{H}^{-s}_{R}(X)
is a perfect pairing and induces an isomorphism
\dot{H}^{s}_{R}(X)^{*}\cong\overline{H}^{-s}_{R}(X)
(uniformly in
R
). By an abuse of notation, we write
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}:\dot{H}^{s}(X)\times\overline{H}^{-s}(X)\to\mathbb{C}
for the
L^{2}
-pairing. For
R=1
, we omit
R
from the notation. We remark that any pseudodifferential operator with parameter
A\in\Psi^{m}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)
induces a continuous operator

A_{X}:\dot{H}^{s}(X)\to\overline{H}^{s-m}(X),

defined by the composition

\dot{H}^{s}(X)\hookrightarrow H^{s}_{c}(M)\xrightarrow{A}H^{s-m}_{\rm loc}(M)\to\overline{H}^{s-m}(X).

Here the last map is the quotient map,
H^{s}_{c}(M)
denotes the space of compactly supported distributions that are
s
-Sobolev regular and
H^{s-m}_{\rm loc}(M)
denotes the space of distributions that are locally
s-m
-Sobolev regular. We use the notation
A_{X}
but remark that this operator maps distributions supported in
X
to distributions restricted to
X^{\circ}
.

Let us make two remarks regarding the operator
A_{X}:\dot{H}^{s}(X)\to\overline{H}^{s-m}(X)
. Firstly, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
density ensures that
A_{X}:\dot{H}^{s}(X)\to\overline{H}^{s-m}(X)
is determined by continuity and the restriction
A_{X}:C^{\infty}_{c}(X^{\circ})\to C^{\infty}(X)
. Secondly, if
s=-m/2
and
A
is formally self-adjoint, then
A_{X}
is determined from the polarization identity by the continuous quadratic form

q_{A_{X}}(u):=\langle u,Au\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad u\in\dot{H}^{-m/2}(X),

defined from
A
and the perfect
L^{2}
-pairing
\dot{H}^{m/2}(X)\times\overline{H}^{-m/2}(X)\to\mathbb{C}
.

The analogue of Theorem
2.22
for
Q_{X}
is the following theorem.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function on
X
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. Set
\mu=(n+1)/2
. Then the family of operators

Q_{X}:=Q_{M}|_{X^{\circ}}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X),

is a well defined family of Fredholm operators for all
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. For some
R_{0}\geq 0
,
Q_{X}(R)
is invertible for all
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Moreover, the following holds:

a)

The family of operators

(Q_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}},

depends holomorphically on
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Moreover, we can extend the holomorphic family
(Q_{X}(R)^{-1}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X))_{R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})}
meromorphically to
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

b)

There are
C,R_{0}>0
such that

C^{-1}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}_{|R|}(X)}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Re}\langle f,Q_{X}(R)f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}_{|R|}(X)}^{2},

for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and
f\in\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
. In particular, for
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
, the norm
{\mathrm{Re}}\langle\cdot,Q_{X}(R)\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
is uniformly equivalent to the norm on
\dot{H}^{-\mu}_{R}(X)
.

Proof.

We first prove part b). This is a direct consequence of adapting Theorem
2.22
, part b), to compactly supported distributions that are
-\mu
-Sobolev regular and using that
\dot{H}^{s}_{|R|}(X)\subseteq H^{s}_{|R|}(M)
is an isometric inclusion.

To prove part a), we note that for
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
,
Q_{M}(R)
is a lower-order perturbation of
Q_{M}(R_{0})
for any
R_{0}\gg 0
. Therefore, the Rellich lemma implies that the quadratic form

\mathfrak{q}_{Q,R}(u):=\langle u,Q(R)u\rangle_{L^{2}}\equiv\langle u,Q_{X}(R)u\rangle_{L^{2}},\quad u\in H^{-\mu}(X),

is a compact perturbation of
\mathfrak{q}_{Q,R_{0}}
. Therefore, the difference

Q_{X}(R)-Q_{X}(R_{0}):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X),

is a compact operator. Since part b) implies that
Q_{X}(R_{0})
is invertible for a large enough
R_{0}\gg 0
, we conclude that
(Q_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}}
is a Fredholm family.

It remains to prove the assertion for the inverse of
Q_{X}(R)
The family of operators
(Q_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}}
is obtained from the holomorphic family of operators
(Q_{M}(R):H^{-\mu}_{c}(M)\to H^{\mu}_{\rm loc}(M))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}}
(see Theorem
2.22
, part a) via inclusions and projections, so it is also holomorphic. As such,
(Q_{X}(R)^{-1}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X))_{R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})}
extends meromorphically to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
by the meromorphic Fredholm theorem (see
[
31
, Proposition 1.1.8]
).
∎

Similarly to the ideas in Section
3
, we shall transfer the results of Theorem
4.1
to the operator
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
using property (MR). For a domain, let us make the notion of property (MR) more precise.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
X
be a compact manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function on
X
such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is regular at the diagonal. For a sector
[1,\infty)\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, we say that
\mathrm{d}
has
property (MR)
on
\Gamma
if
(X,d)
is isometrically embedded as a domain with smooth boundary in a manifold
M
equipped with a distance function
\mathrm{d}_{M}
, such that
\mathrm{d}^{2}_{M}
is regular at the diagonal and and for any
R\in\Gamma
,
L(R)
extends to a continuous mapping
H^{-\mu}_{c}(M)\to H^{\mu}_{\rm loc}(M)
with

\|L(R)\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}(K)\to\bar{H}^{\mu}(K^{\prime})}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma$},

for any compact subsets
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq M
.

If, for any compact subsets
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq M
, the operator
L(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(K)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(K^{\prime})
is compact for
R\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma\ni R\mapsto L(R)\in\mathbb{K}(\dot{H}^{-\mu}(K),\overline{H}^{\mu}(K))
is holomorphic in norm sense, we say that
\mathrm{d}
has
property (SMR)
on
\Gamma
.

In the absence of a boundary, the definition of property (MR) for a manifold with boundary (Definition
4.2
) is readily seen to be equivalent to property (MR) for a compact manifold (Definition
3.3
). The reader is encouraged to think of the definition of property (MR) for a manifold with boundary as the distance function having “property (MR) on a neighborhood of the manifold with boundary”. Let us consider two examples of distance functions with property (SMR).

Example 4.3

(Domains in Riemannian manifolds with small diameter)
.

Assume that
X\subseteq M
is a compact domain with
C^{0}
-boundary in a Riemannian manifold with geodesic distance
\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},M}
. If the diameter of
X
is strictly smaller than the injectivity radius of
M
,
\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},M}^{2}
is smooth on a neighborhood of
X
. The same argument as in Proposition
3.4
shows that the distance function
\mathrm{d}_{\rm geo}:=\mathrm{d}_{{\rm geo},M}|_{X}
on
X
has property (SMR) on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. In fact, in this case,
L\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X;\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
L(R)\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X)
for any
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

Example 4.4

(Submanifolds with boundary)
.

Assume that
X
is a compact manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary embedded in a manifold
i:X\to W
and
\mathrm{d}_{W}
is a distance function on
W
such that the square
\mathrm{d}_{W}^{2}
is smooth on
W\times W
and regular at the diagonal. This arises for instance for
W=\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
W=\mathbb{H}_{N,\mathbb{R}}
or
W=\mathbb{H}_{N,\mathbb{C}}
, for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, with their geodesic distance.

The subspace distance function
\mathrm{d}:X\times X\to[0,\infty)
,
\mathrm{d}(x,y):=\mathrm{d}_{W}(i(x),i(y))
will then satisfy

i)

\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on
X\times X
and regular at the diagonal.

ii)

L\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X;\mathbb{C}_{+})
and
L(R)\in\Psi^{-\infty}(X)
for any
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

iii)

\mathrm{d}
has property (SMR) on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

This follows by the same arguments as in Proposition
3.4
by choosing a submanifold
M\subseteq W
in which
i(X)
is a compact domain (with
C^{0}
-boundary).

For a distance function with property (MR), we tacitly assume that the manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary is embedded into the manifold
M
implementing property (MR). The next result is proven exactly as Theorem
3.7
but using Theorem
4.1
instead of Corollary
2.21
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function on
X
with property (MR) on the sector
\Gamma
. Then there is an
R_{0}\geq 0
such that

\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X),

is invertible for all
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Moreover,

\mathcal{Z}^{-1}_{X}=Q^{-1}_{X}+\mathcal{R}_{X},

where
Q^{-1}_{X}
is the inverse of
Q_{X}
(existing by Theorem
4.1
) and
\mathcal{R}_{X}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
is a family of operators such that

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})$}.

Moreover, there is a
C>0
such that

(28)

C^{-1}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}_{|R|}(X)}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Re}\langle f,\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}_{|R|}(X)}^{2},

for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
and
f\in\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
. In particular, for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
,
\mathrm{Re}\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)
is positive in form sense on
L^{2}(X)
for the
H^{-\mu}
-norm.

The next result poses an obstruction to property (MR) for distance functions on manifolds with boundary. It is proven in the same way as Corollary
3.10
but using Theorem
4.5
instead of Theorem
3.7
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Assume that
\mathrm{d}
is a distance function on a manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary
X
with property (MR) on
[1,\infty)
. Then there exists an
R_{0}\geq 0
such that for all finite subsets
F\subseteq X
the
|F|\times|F|
-matrix
(\mathrm{e}^{-R\mathrm{d}(x,y)})_{x,y\in F}
is positive definite for all
R>R_{0}
.

Using Theorem
4.1
instead of Theorem
3.7
, the next result is proven ad verbatim as Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary and assume that distance function
\mathrm{d}
has property (SMR) on
\Gamma
. There is an
R_{0}\geq 0
such that the operator

\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X),

is a well defined Fredholm operator for all
R\in\Gamma
and invertible for
R\in\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
. Moreover, the operator

\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)
depends holomorphically on
R
in
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)^{-1}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
depends meromorphically on
R\in\Gamma
.

Similarly to Corollary
3.5
, we deduce the following special case of Theorem
4.7
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with
C^{0}
-boundary and assume that
\mathrm{d}
is a distance function satisfying that
\mathrm{d}^{2}
is smooth on
X\times X
and regular at the diagonal (e.g. as in Example
4.3
or
4.4
). Then the family of operators

(\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R):\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X))_{R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}},

depends holomorphically on
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. Moreover, for some
R_{0}
, we have a holomorphic family
(\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R)^{-1}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X))_{R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})}
that extends meromorphically to
R\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.

5.
Structure of the inverse operator in the presence of a boundary

Consider a compact manifold with boundary
X
equipped with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
whose square is regular at the diagonal. As above, we set
\mu:=(n+1)/2
where
n:=\dim(X)
. If
\mathrm{d}
has property (MR), Theorem
4.5
ensures that computations for
Q_{X}
relate to computations for
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
up to a term of infinitely low order in
R
(as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty
), so we focus on the operator
Q_{X}
. As proved in Theorem
4.1
above, the localized operator
Q_{X}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)
is an isomorphism for large enough
R
in the sector
\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}
. We shall now describe the inverse of
Q_{X}
in more precise terms under the assumption that the boundary is smooth. The inverse
Q_{X}^{-1}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
will be computed as a sum of

•

a pseudodifferential operator (with parameter) in the interior;

•

a composition of two
mixed-regularity
pseudodifferential operator near the boundary, where the two factors are obtained from inverting a Wiener-Hopf factorization of the magnitude operator at the boundary; as well as

•

an error term that acts as order
2\mu
, mapping
\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu+1}(X)
, whose norm is
O(|R|^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
.

Asymptotically, only the two first terms play a role, and in the next section we compute the asymptotics of conditional expectations from the symbols of these first two terms. To describe the decomposition, we shall need further terminology.

5.1.
Mixed-regularity symbols and Sobolev spaces

Definition 5.1
.

Let
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Write coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\xi=(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. We define the Sobolev space of mixed-regularity
(s,t)
as

H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{t}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi<\infty\right\}.

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a domain (so
\Omega=\overline{\Omega^{\circ}}
), we define

\dot{H}^{s,t}(\Omega):=\{f\in H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\mathrm{supp}(f)\subseteq\Omega\},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{H}^{s,t}(\Omega):=H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n})/\dot{H}^{s,t}(\overline{\Omega^{c}}).

We also define
\dot{H}^{s,t}_{c}(\Omega)
and
\overline{H}^{s,t}_{c}(\Omega)
as the elements with compact support. The local Sobolev spaces of mixed-regularity are defined as

\displaystyle\dot{H}^{s,t}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\chi f\in\dot{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)\forall\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\},

\displaystyle\overline{H}^{s,t}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=H^{s,t}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n})/\dot{H}^{s,t}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\Omega^{c}}).

We note that
\dot{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)\subseteq H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a closed subspace. We call the quotient mapping
{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\overline{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)
the restriction mapping. For large enough
s>0
, we can identify
\dot{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)
with a subspace of
\overline{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)
(but not for small
s<0
). A standard computation with Fourier transforms shows that the the identity operator induces continuous mappings
\overline{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)\to\overline{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\Omega)
and
\dot{H}^{s,t}(\Omega)\to\dot{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\Omega)
if and only if
s\geq s^{\prime}
and
s+t\geq s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}
(which is locally compact if and only if
s>s^{\prime}
and
s+t>s^{\prime}+t^{\prime}
).

Definition 5.2
.

Let
u,m\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Let
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be a sector and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
an open subset. Write coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\xi=(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. We say that
a\in C^{\infty}(U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Gamma)
is a symbol with parameter of mixed-regularity
(u,m)
if for any compact
K\subseteq U
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{x\in K}\left|\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{k}\partial_{(\xi^{\prime},R)}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a(x,\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n},R)\right|\leq{C}\langle(\xi,R)\rangle^{u-k}\langle(\xi^{\prime},R)\rangle^{m-|\beta|},

for all
(\xi,R)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We let
S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
denote the space of symbols with parameter of mixed-regularity
(u,m)
. We set
S^{u,-\infty}(U;\Gamma):=\cap_{m\in\mathbb{R}}S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
.

For
a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
we define

Op(a):C^{\infty}_{c}(U)\to C^{\infty}(U),\quad Op(a)f(x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(x,\xi,R)\hat{f}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi.

Let
\Psi^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
denote the linear space of operators
C^{\infty}_{c}(U)\to C^{\infty}(U)
spanned by
\{Op(a):a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)\}
and smoothing operators with parameter. We set
\Psi^{u,-\infty}(U;\Gamma):=\cap_{m\in\mathbb{R}}\Psi^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
.

Example 5.3
.

Assume that
0\notin\Gamma
. Suppose that
a(x,\xi,R)=b(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{u}
where
b
is a homogeneous symbol with parameter of order
m
and
h
is a homogeneous symbol with parameter of order
1
. A short computation shows that
\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{k}\partial_{(\xi^{\prime},R)}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a
is a sum of terms of the form
\tilde{b}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{u-k-l}
where
\tilde{b}
is homogeneous of order
m-|\beta|+l
. Therefore
a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
, and in fact
a\in S^{u_{0},m+u-u_{0}}(U;\Gamma)
for any
u_{0}\leq u
.

We note that since differentiation in the
(\xi^{\prime},R)
-direction improves the order of decay, we have for
a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
that

Op(a)f(x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}a(x,\xi,R)\mathcal{F}_{y_{n}\to\xi_{n}}f(y^{\prime},\xi_{n})\mathrm{e}^{i\xi_{n}x_{n}+i\xi^{\prime}(x^{\prime}-y)^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}y^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\xi,

as an oscillatory integral. Moreover, we can conclude the following result from standard techniques of oscillatory integrals (cf.
[
39
, Chapter I.1]
).

Proposition 5.4
.

Assume that
A\in\Psi^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
. Then for any
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(U)
with
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
it holds that
\chi A\chi^{\prime}\in\Psi^{u,-\infty}(U;\Gamma)
. In particular, if
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
satisfies
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
then
\|\chi A\chi^{\prime}\|_{H^{s}\to H^{s^{\prime}}}=O(|R|^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
for all
s\geq s^{\prime}+u
.

The last statement of the proposition follows from the next theorem.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
u,m,s,t,s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Let
A\in\Psi^{u,m}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\Gamma)
. Then
A
extends to a continuous operator

A:H^{s,t}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

if
s\geq s^{\prime}+u
and
t\geq t^{\prime}+m
. In this case, we have for any
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that there exists a
C=C(s,s^{\prime},A,\chi,\chi^{\prime})>0

\|\chi A\chi^{\prime}\|_{H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(1+|R|)^{t^{\prime}-t+m}.

Proof.

The first part follows from the Calderón-Vaillancourt theorem. The second part follows from noting that Calderón-Vaillancourt’s theorem proves the case
t^{\prime}+m=t
and for
A\in\Psi^{u,m}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\Gamma)
compactly supported, then
(R^{2}+\Delta^{\prime})^{(t-t^{\prime}-m)/2}A\in\Psi^{u,t-t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\Gamma)
(where
\Delta^{\prime}
denotes the Laplacian in the
x^{\prime}
-direction).
∎

Definition 5.6
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider a sequence
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of mixed-regularity symbols with
a_{j}\in S^{u,m_{j}}(U;\Gamma)
for a sequence
m_{j}\to-\infty
. Set
m:=\max_{j}m_{j}
. If
a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
satisfies that for any
N
, there is an
M
such that
a-\sum_{j=0}^{M}a_{j}\in S^{u,-N}(U;\Gamma)
, we write

a\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j},

and call
a
the asymptotic sum of
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{R}
. For any sequence
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of mixed symbols with
a_{j}\in S^{u,m_{j}}(U;\Gamma)
for a sequence
m_{j}\to-\infty
, the asymptotic sum
a\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}
exists in
S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
, where
m:=\max_{j}m_{j}
. The asymptotic sum is uniquely determined modulo
S^{u,-\infty}(U;\Gamma)
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is carried out ad verbatim as in
[
25
, Proposition 18.1.3]
upon replacing the
\xi
in
[
25
]
with
(\xi^{\prime},R)
.
∎

Again, using that differentiation in the
(\xi,R)
-direction improves the order of decay, we can conclude several results from the standard situation (cf.
[
39
, Chapter I]
). For instance, the analogue of
[
39
, Theorem 3.1, Chapter I.3]
extends modulo
\Psi^{u,-\infty}
to
\Psi^{u,m}
which implies asymptotic expansions of products and adjoints.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
A\in\Psi^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
and
B\in\Psi^{u^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(U;\Gamma)
be mixed-regularity pseudodifferential operators out of which at least one is properly supported. Then
AB\in\Psi^{u+u^{\prime},m+m^{\prime}}(U;\Gamma)
is a mixed-regularity pseudodifferential operator. Moreover, if
a\in S^{u,m}(U;\Gamma)
and
b\in S^{u^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(U;\Gamma)
are symbols with
A-Op(a)
and
B-Op(b)
being smoothing with parameter, then
AB-Op(c)
is smoothing with parameter where
c\in S^{u+u^{\prime},m+m^{\prime}}(U;\Gamma)
is uniquely determined modulo
S^{u+u^{\prime},-\infty}(U;\Gamma)
as the asymptotic sum

c\sim\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}aD^{\alpha}_{x}b.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
u,m,s,t,s^{\prime},t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Let
a\in S^{u,m}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\Gamma)
be compactly supported in the
x
-direction and assume that
a
extends to a holomorphic function in
\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})<0
. Set
A:=Op(a)
. Then
A
restricts to and induces, respectively, continuous operators

\dot{A}:\dot{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to\dot{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{A}:\overline{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-})\to\overline{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-}),

if
s\geq s^{\prime}+u
and
t\geq t^{\prime}+m
.

Proof.

Note that
\overline{A}
is well defined as the operator induced by
A:H^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to H^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as soon as
A
preserves supports in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. Because the same holds for
\dot{A}:\dot{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to\dot{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, we only need to show that
A
preserves supports in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. By standard density arguments, it suffices to prove that
A
preserves supports in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
when applied to test functions.

Using Proposition
5.8
, we note that upon replacing
A
with the operator

A\ Op((1+i\xi_{n}+|(\xi^{\prime},R)|)^{-m_{0}}(1+|(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n},R)|^{2})^{-u_{0}}),

for sufficiently large
u_{0}
and
m_{0}
, we can assume that
a
is integrable in
\xi
. Set
K_{R}(x,z):=\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to z}a(x,\xi,R)
. Then for any test function
f
,

Af(x)=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{R}(x,x-y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y.

The Paley-Wiener theorem combined with the assumption that
a
extends to a holomorphic function in
\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})<0
implies that
K_{R}
is supported in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\Gamma
. We conclude that if
\mathrm{supp}(f)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, then
Af(x)=(2\pi)^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}K_{R}(x,x-y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y
is also supported in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.9
, Theorem
5.5
implies that for any
s
and
s^{\prime}
with
s\geq s^{\prime}+u
there is a
C>0
such that for
t\geq t^{\prime}+m

\|A\|_{\dot{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to\dot{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\leq C(1+|R|)^{t^{\prime}-t+m}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|A\|_{\overline{H}^{s,t}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-})\to\overline{H}^{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-})}\leq C(1+|R|)^{t^{\prime}-t+m}.

5.2.
Wiener-Hopf factorization of
Q_{X}
near the boundary

Using the machinery of the previous subsection, we shall now factorize the operator
Q_{X}
near the boundary into factors that extend holomorphically into the lower, respectively upper half-plane, and use Theorem
5.9
to (near the boundary) invert these individual factors as operators
\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to L^{2}(X)
and
L^{2}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)
, respectively. The reader should recall the structure of the full symbol of
Q
from Theorem
2.9
. We shorten the notation and write
C^{j}
for
C^{j}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
, where
C^{j}_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
are the Taylor coefficients of
\mathrm{d}^{2}
as in Equation (
9
).

As above, we consider a compact manifold with boundary
X
and a distance function
\mathrm{d}
on
X
whose square is regular at the diagonal (cf. Definition
2.2
). We tacitly fix a manifold
M
containing
X
as a smooth compact domain to which
\mathrm{d}
extends as a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. In particular, we can Taylor expand
\mathrm{d}^{2}
near any point in the diagonal as in Equation (
9
) and its Taylor coefficients enter the full symbol of
Q
as in Theorem
2.9
. To study the behavior at the boundary, we first reduce to the model case that
X=\partial X\times[0,\infty)
, as a domain in
\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
. We remark that
\partial X\times[0,\infty)
is not compact, but we shall later on only use the constructed operators in a form localized to near the compact boundary.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
X
be a compact manifold with boundary with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
whose square is regular at the diagonal, embedded into a manifold
M
as in the preceding paragraph. Consider the compact manifold
Y=\partial X
and choose a tubular neighborhood
U\subseteq M
of
\partial X
and a diffeomorphism
\varphi:U\to Y\times(-1,1)
, with
\partial X=\varphi^{-1}(Y\times\{0\})
. Then there exists a classical elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter
Q^{\partial}\in\Psi^{-n-1}_{\rm cl}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}_{+})
and a number
\varepsilon>0
such that

•

Q^{\partial}
is translation invariant outside a compact subset in the sense that there exists a
t_{0}>0
such that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Y\times\mathbb{R})
is supported in
\{(y,t):\pm t>t_{0}\}
then
[Q^{\partial}f](\cdot\mp s)=Q^{\partial}[f(\cdot\mp s)]
for all
s>0
.

•

For all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Y\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))
it holds that
Q(\varphi^{*}f)
is supported in
U
and

Q(\varphi^{*}f)=\varphi^{*}(Q^{\partial}f).

•

the principal symbol of
Q^{\partial}
is given by

\sigma_{-n-1}(x,\xi,R)=c(R^{2}+g(\xi,\xi))^{-\mu},

where
g
is a Riemannian metric on
Y\times\mathbb{R}
which is translation invariant in the
\mathbb{R}
-direction outside a compact and coincides with
\varphi^{*}g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
on
Y\times(-1,1)
.

Proof.

Construct
g
from interpolating between
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
near
0
and something at infinity. Construct
Q^{\partial}
from interpolation along the real line between
Q
near
0
and
(R^{2}+\Delta_{g})^{-\mu}
at infinity. The second part follows from that
Q
by construction has small propagation.
∎

Henceforth, we shall fix a choice of
Q^{\partial}
and
g
as in Proposition
5.11
.

Remark 5.12
.

To fix a choice of a diffeomorphism
\varphi:U\to Y\times(-1,1)
is (up to a self-diffeomorphism of
Y
) equivalent to choosing a vector field defined near
Y=\partial X
which is transversal to the boundary. This choice of vector field, or equivalently, the last entry of
\varphi:U\to Y\times(-1,1)
, gives rise to a coordinate that we denote by
x_{n}:U\to(-1,1)
. We remark that it is always possible to use the transversal vector field to be the metric normal to the boundary, in which case we have that on
\partial X
:

g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}=\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}+g_{\partial X,\mathrm{d}^{2}},

where
g_{\partial X,\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is the induced Riemannian metric on
\partial X
. For computational purposes, it becomes clumsy to restrict to the the case when the transversal vector field is orthogonal to the boundary but for some considerations it simplifies the formulas.

Following the notation of Subsection
5.1
, we write
x_{n}
for this transversal coordinate and
x^{\prime}
for coordinates on
\partial X
. Similarly,
\xi_{n}
denotes the cotangent variable in the transversal direction and
\xi^{\prime}
denotes the cotangent variables along
\partial X
.

We let
q^{\partial}\in S^{-2\mu}_{\rm cl}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
denote the full symbol of
Q^{\partial}
. We note that
q^{\partial}\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q^{\partial}_{j}
in
S^{-2\mu}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
where each
q^{\partial}_{j}\in S^{-2\mu-j}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
is a homogeneous symbol in
(\xi,R)
of order
-2\mu-j=-n-1-j
and near
t=0
, we have in any local coordinates on
Y
that
q^{\partial}_{j}=q_{j}
where
q_{j}
is computed as in Theorem
2.9
using the coordinates induced from
Y
and
\varphi
.

Proposition 5.13
.

There are unique homogeneous degree
1
symbols

h_{\pm}=h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)\in S^{1}(T^{*}Y\times\mathbb{R},Y\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}),

that determine the complex solutions to the equation
R^{2}+g_{x}((\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n}),(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n}))=0
for fixed
(x,\xi^{\prime},R)
with
\pm\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
. Furthermore, there is a unique function
h_{0}\in C^{\infty}(Y\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{>0})
such that

R^{2}+g(\xi,\xi)=h_{0}(x)(\xi_{n}-h_{+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))(\xi_{n}-h_{-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)).

Moreover, we have that

h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=-\frac{\xi^{\prime}(b(x))}{h_{0}(x)}\pm i\frac{\sqrt{R^{2}+g_{Y}(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})-(\xi^{\prime}(b))^{2}}}{\sqrt{h_{0}(x)}},

for suitable
b
and
g_{Y}
determined from the metric.

Proof.

It is not hard to see that
h_{0}
and
h_{\pm}
are well defined and unique, but let us construct them explicitly. We can decompose

(29)

g=\begin{pmatrix}h_{0}&b\\
b^{T}&g_{Y}\end{pmatrix},

where
g_{Y}
is a metric on
Y
on each slice, and
b\in C^{\infty}(Y\times\mathbb{R},TY)
. Since
g
is translation invariant outside a compact,
h_{0}
,
b
and
g_{Y}
are translation invariant outside a compact. We have that

R^{2}+g(\xi,\xi)=R^{2}+h_{0}\xi_{n}^{2}+2\xi^{\prime}(b)\xi_{n}+g_{Y}(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}).

We see that
h_{0}
in the above definition is the
h_{0}
in Equation (
29
) and that the complex roots are as prescribed. The proposition follows.
∎

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
q^{\partial}\in S^{-2\mu}_{\rm cl}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
be as above. Then there exists
q^{\partial}_{\pm}\in S^{-\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
that are translation invariant outside a compact such that

(1)

q^{\partial}_{\pm}\in S^{-\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
admits asymptotic expansions (in
S^{-\mu,0}
in the sense of Definition
5.6
on page
5.6
)

q^{\partial}_{\pm}\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q^{\partial}_{\pm,j},

where
q^{\partial}_{\pm,0}\in S^{-\mu,0}
and for
j>0
,

q^{\partial}_{\pm,j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k=-1}^{j-1}b_{\pm,j,k}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{-\mu-j+k}\in S^{-\mu-1,-j+1},

where
b_{\pm,j,k}
is homogeneous of degree
-k
in
(\xi^{\prime},R)
and can be computed by an iterative scheme of partial fraction decompositions as a homogeneous rational function in derivatives of
h_{0}
,
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
. The first terms are given by

(30)

\displaystyle q^{\partial}_{+,0}=

\displaystyle n!\omega_{n}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-\mu},

\displaystyle q^{\partial}_{-,0}

\displaystyle=h_{0}^{-\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-\mu},

and
q_{\pm,1}
are computed in Proposition
5.16
below.

(2)

The mixed-regularity symbols
