q^{\partial}_{\pm}\in S^{-\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
and
q^{\partial}_{\pm,j}\in S^{-\mu-1,-j+1}
admit holomorphic extensions to
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
.

(3)

It holds that

q^{\partial}=\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{-}^{\partial}D^{\alpha}_{x}q_{+}^{\partial}\mod S^{-2\mu,-\infty}.

Proof.

Let us first massage the statements of the theorem. We want to construct
q^{\partial}_{\pm}\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q^{\partial}_{\pm,j}\in S^{-\mu,0}
admitting holomorphic extensions to
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
and satisfying
q^{\partial}=\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{-}^{\partial}D^{\alpha}_{x}q_{+}^{\partial}\mod S^{-2\mu,-\infty}
. We remark that to ensure item (2), i.e. the holomorphic extension of
q^{\partial}_{\pm}
, it suffices to construct each
q_{\pm,j}^{\partial}
so that it admits a holomorphic extension to
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
. We also note that the requirement on the composition is equivalent to

(31)

q^{\partial}_{j}=\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{-,k}^{\partial}D^{\alpha}_{x}q_{+,l}^{\partial}.

We take the formula (
30
) as a definition, and note that
q^{\partial}_{\pm,0}
satisfy the structural statement in item (1), extends holomorphically to
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
and
q^{\partial}_{0}=q^{\partial}_{-,0}q^{\partial}_{+,0}
. Using an idea described in
[
23
]
, Equation (
31
) is for
j>0
equivalent to

\frac{q^{\partial}_{+,j}}{q^{\partial}_{+,0}}+\frac{q^{\partial}_{-,j}}{q^{\partial}_{-,0}}=\frac{q^{\partial}_{j}}{q^{\partial}_{0}}-\frac{1}{q^{\partial}_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+l+|\alpha|=j\\
k,l<j\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{-,k}^{\partial}D^{\alpha}_{x}q_{+,l}^{\partial}

We proceed by induction. Assume that we have constructed
q_{\pm,k}
for
k<j
satisfying the statements of items (1), (2), and (3) in the relevant degrees. Using Lemma
2.19
and item (1) for
q_{\pm,k}
for
k<j
, we can use Lemma
B.1
to uniquely partial fraction decompose

\frac{q^{\partial}_{j}}{q^{\partial}_{0}}-\frac{1}{q^{\partial}_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+l+|\alpha|=j\\
k,l<j\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{-,k}^{\partial}D^{\alpha}_{x}q_{+,l}^{\partial}=\mathfrak{q}_{+,j}+\mathfrak{q}_{-,j},

where
\mathfrak{q}_{\pm,j}
by Proposition
2.6
and Theorem
2.9
takes the form

\mathfrak{q}_{\pm,j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k=-1}^{j-1}\mathfrak{b}_{\pm,j,k}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{-j+k}\in S^{-1,-j+1},

where
\mathfrak{b}_{\pm,j,k}
is homogeneous of degree
-k
in
(\xi^{\prime},R)
and can be explicitly computed from the results of Appendix
B
and Theorem
2.9
. We now define

q^{\partial}_{\pm,j}:=q^{\partial}_{\pm,0}\mathfrak{q}_{\pm,j},

and note that it by construction satisfies items (1), (2), and (3) in the relevant degrees.

∎

Remark 5.15
.

As noted in Proposition
5.13
, the symbols
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
are directly determined from the metric and the choice of transversal to the boundary. Moreover, as the proof of Theorem
5.14
shows, each of the symbols
b_{\pm,j,k}=b_{\pm,j,k}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)
depends

(1)

polynomially on
(C^{(\gamma)}_{G})_{\gamma\in\cup_{k\leq j}I_{k}}
and its derivatives contracted by
g_{G}
and
\iota_{n}g_{G}
and its derivatives

(2)

polynomially on
h_{+}
,
h_{-}
and rationally on
h_{+}-h_{-}
and
h_{0}^{-1/2}
,

The total degree is
j
where each
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}
,
\gamma\in I_{k}
, has degree
k
, the metric and
h_{0}
have degree zero,
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
have degree
1
and
x
-derivatives increase the order by
1
.

Proposition 5.16
.

For
n>1
, the terms
q_{\pm,1}
appearing in the expansion of
q_{\pm}
in Theorem
5.14
are given by:

\displaystyle q^{\partial}_{+,1}=

\displaystyle n!\omega_{n}\mathfrak{a}_{0,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-\mu-1}+n!\omega_{n}\mathfrak{a}_{1,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-\mu-2},

\displaystyle q^{\partial}_{-,1}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{0,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)h_{0}^{-\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-\mu-1}+\mathfrak{a}_{1,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)h_{0}^{-\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-\mu-2},

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{0,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=

\displaystyle-\frac{3i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{n!\omega_{n}}\left(C^{3}(x,g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{+}+C^{3}(x,g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\right)\frac{h_{0}^{-1}}{h_{+}-h_{-}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{{n!}\omega_{n}}\frac{h_{0}^{-2}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{3}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigg[C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{+}^{2}(h_{+}-3h_{-})-6h_{+}h_{-}C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-3(h_{+}+h_{-})C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)-2C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\bigg]-

\displaystyle+\frac{i(n+1)^{2}}{4}\frac{(\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}h_{-}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{+}-\partial_{x_{n}}h_{+})}{h_{+}-h_{-}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{1,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=

\displaystyle\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{{n!}\omega_{n}}\frac{h_{0}^{-2}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigg[C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{+}^{3}+3h_{+}^{2}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+3h_{+}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\bigg],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{0,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=

\displaystyle\frac{3i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{n!\omega_{n}}\left(C^{3}(x,g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{-}+C^{3}(x,g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\right)\frac{h_{0}^{-1}}{h_{+}-h_{-}}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{{n!}\omega_{n}}\frac{h_{0}^{-2}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{3}}

\displaystyle\qquad\bigg[C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{-}^{2}(h_{-}-3h_{+})-6h_{+}h_{-}C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-{3}(h_{+}+h_{-})C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)-2C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\bigg]+

\displaystyle-\frac{i(n+1)^{2}}{4}\frac{(\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}h_{-}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{+}-\partial_{x_{n}}h_{+})}{h_{+}-h_{-}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{1,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=

\displaystyle\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n+3)_{3,-2}}{{n!}\omega_{n}}\frac{h_{0}^{-2}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigg[C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)h_{-}^{3}+3h_{-}^{2}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+3h_{-}C^{3}(x,\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g\otimes\iota_{\xi^{\prime}}g)\bigg].

The proof is computational and based on Lemma
B.1
using the explicit forms in Corollary
B.3
. The details can be found in the arXiv version of this paper
[
14
]
.

Lemma 5.17
.

The operators
Q^{\partial}_{\pm}:=Op(q^{\partial}_{\pm})\in\Psi^{-\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
satisfy that

(1)

Q-Q_{-}Q_{+}\in\Psi^{-2\mu,-\infty}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
;

(2)

Q_{+}
restricts to a well defined operator
\dot{H}^{-\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty)\to L^{2}(Y\times[0,\infty))
which is invertible for large
R
;

(3)

Q_{-}
restricts to a well defined operator
L^{2}(Y\times[0,\infty))\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty))
which is invertible for large
R
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows from Proposition
5.8
and Theorem
5.14
. Parts (2) and (3) follow from Theorem
5.5
and Theorem
5.9
.
∎

Definition 5.18
.

Define
w_{\pm,j}\in S^{\mu,-j}
inductively by

w_{\pm,0}(x,\xi,R):=(q^{\partial}_{\pm,0})^{-1}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}(\xi_{n}-h_{+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{\mu},\;&\mbox{for $+$},\\
{}\\
h_{0}(x)^{\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{\mu},\;&\mbox{for $-$},\end{cases}

and then

w_{\pm,j}:=-w_{\pm,0}\sum_{k+l+|\alpha|=j,\,l<j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{\pm,k}^{\partial}D_{x}^{\alpha}w_{\pm,l}.

We also define
w_{\pm}:=\sum_{j}w_{\pm,j}\in S^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
and
W_{\pm}\in\Psi^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
is defined as a properly supported modification of
Op(w_{\pm})
with the same full symbol which is translation invariant outside a compact subset.

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
w_{\pm}\in S^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
be as above. Then

(1)

The asymptotic expansion (in
S^{\mu,0}
in the sense of Definition
5.6
on page
5.6
) of
w_{\pm}\in S^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)

w_{\pm}\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\pm,j},

can for
j>0
be expanded in a finite sum

w_{\pm,j}(x,\xi,R)=\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,j,k}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R))^{\mu-j+k}\in S^{\mu-1,-j+1},

where
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,j,k}
is homogeneous of degree
-k
in
(\xi^{\prime},R)
and can be computed by an iterative scheme as a rational function of derivatives of
h_{0}
,
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
.

(2)

The mixed-regularity symbols
w_{\pm}\in S^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
and
w_{\pm,j}\in S^{\mu-1,-j+1}
admit holomorphic extensions to
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\xi_{n})>0
.

(3)

The symbols
\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,j,k}=\mathfrak{w}_{\pm,j,k}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)
depend

(a)

polynomially on
(C^{(\gamma)}_{G})_{\gamma\in\cup_{k\leq j}I_{k}}
and its derivatives contracted by
g_{G}
and
\iota_{n}g_{G}
and its derivatives

(b)

polynomially on
h_{+}
,
h_{-}
and rationally on
h_{+}-h_{-}
and
h_{0}^{-1/2}
,

The total degree is
j
where each
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}
,
\gamma\in I_{k}
, has degree
k
, the metric and
h_{0}
have degree zero,
h_{+}
and
h_{-}
have degree
1
and
x
-derivatives increase the order by
1
.

Proof.

Items (1) and (2) follow from a short induction argument with the construction (in Definition
5.18
) and Theorem
5.14
.
∎

We now compute
w_{\pm,1}
. By definition, we have that

w_{\pm,1}=-w_{\pm,0}^{2}q_{\pm,1}^{\partial}-w_{\pm,0}\sum_{|\alpha|=1}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}q_{\pm,0}^{\partial}D_{x}^{\alpha}w_{\pm,0}.

A short algebraic manipulation with the computation of
q_{\pm,1}
from Proposition
5.16
gives the following formulas.

Proposition 5.20
.

For
n>1
, the terms
w_{\pm,1}
appearing in the expansion of
w_{\pm}
in Lemma
5.19
are given by:

\displaystyle w_{+,1}(x,\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n},R)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}\mathfrak{a}_{0,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{\mu-1}-\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}\mathfrak{a}_{1,+}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{\mu-2}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i(n+1)^{2}}{4\cdot n!\omega_{n}}(\partial_{x_{n}}h_{+}-\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}h_{+}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{+})(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{\mu-2},

\displaystyle w_{-,1}(x,\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n},R)=

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{a}_{0,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)h_{0}^{\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{\mu-1}-\mathfrak{a}_{1,-}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)h_{0}^{\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{\mu-2}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i(n+1)^{2}}{4}(\partial_{x_{n}}h_{-}-\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}h_{-}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{-})h_{0}^{\mu}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{\mu-2}-

\displaystyle-\frac{i(n+1)^{2}}{4}(\partial_{x_{n}}h_{0}-\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}h_{-}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{0})h_{0}^{\mu-1}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{\mu-1},

where the homogeneous symbols
\mathfrak{a}_{\pm,0}
(of degree
0
) and
\mathfrak{a}_{\pm,1}
(of degree
1
) were explicitly given in Proposition
5.16
above.

Lemma 5.21
.

The operators
W_{\pm}\in\Psi^{\mu,0}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
satisfy that

(1)

1-W_{\pm}Q_{\pm}^{\partial},1-Q_{\pm}^{\partial}W_{\pm}\in\Psi^{0,-\infty}(Y\times\mathbb{R};\Gamma)
;

(2)

W_{-}
preserves supports in
Y\times(-\infty,0]
and restricts to a well defined operator
\overline{H}^{\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty))\to L^{2}(Y\times[0,\infty)
which is invertible for large
R
;

(3)

W_{+}
preserves supports in
Y\times[0,\infty)
and restricts to a well defined operator
L^{2}(Y\times[0,\infty))\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty)
which is invertible for large
R
.

In particular, the operators

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle:=1-W_{+}W_{-}Q^{\partial}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty))\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty)),\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle:=1-Q^{\partial}W_{+}W_{-}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty))\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(Y\times[0,\infty)),

are normbounded by
O(R^{-\infty})
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows from Proposition
5.8
and Lemma
5.19
. Parts (2) and (3) follow from Theorem
5.5
and Theorem
5.9
.
∎

5.3.
Decomposition of the inverse magnitude operator

Theorem 5.22
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. Set
\mu:=(n+1)/2
. Let
Q_{X}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)
denote the restriction of
Q_{\chi,\mathrm{d}^{2}}
to
X
and let
A\in\Psi^{n+1}_{\rm cl}(X;\mathbb{C}_{+})
denote a parametrix of
Q_{\chi,\mathrm{d}^{2}}
. For some
R_{0}\geq 0
and any
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})
, we can write

Q_{X}^{-1}=\chi_{1}A\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}+S,

where
\chi_{2},\chi_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(X)
are functions supported in a collar neighborhood
U_{0}
of
\partial X
in
X
and
\chi_{1},\chi_{1}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(X^{\circ})
are functions such that

\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\chi_{j}^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{j})}=1,\;j=1,2,

\varphi:\partial X\times[0,1)\to U_{0}
is a collar identification, and the operators
S
,
W_{-}
and
W_{+}
satisfy the following as
R\to\infty
:

(1)

S:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
is a continuous operator with
\|S\|_{\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

(2)

W_{+}:L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
is the properly supported pseudodifferential operator of mixed-regularity
(\mu,0)
from Definitions
5.18
which is invertible for large
R>0
and in local coordinates has an asymptotic expansion modulo
S^{\mu,-\infty}
as in Lemma
5.21
and preserves support in
\partial X\times[0,\infty)\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}+C^{\infty}_{c}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
with
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
, it holds that
\|\chi W_{+}\chi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to H^{-\mu}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

(3)

W_{-}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))\to L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
is the properly supported pseudodifferential operator of mixed-regularity
(\mu,0)
from Definitions
5.18
which is invertible for large
R>0
and in local coordinates has an asymptotic expansion modulo
S^{\mu,-\infty}
as in Lemma
5.21
and preserves support in
\partial X\times(-\infty,0]\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}+C^{\infty}_{c}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})
with
\chi\chi^{\prime}=0
, it holds that
\|\chi W_{-}\chi^{\prime}\|_{H^{\mu}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\partial X\times\mathbb{R})}=O(R^{-\infty})
.

Proof.

The properties of
W_{+}
and
W_{-}
listed in items (2) and (3) follow from the results of Subsection
5.1
. We note that it follows from the previous subsection that

\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X),

is a well defined continuous operator. Pick a
\chi_{3}
supported close to
\partial X
with
\chi_{3}=1
on
\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{2}^{\prime})
. We compute that

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+

\displaystyle\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime})Q_{X}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}+\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}Q_{X}\chi_{3}+

\displaystyle+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}\chi_{3}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}Q_{X}(1-\chi_{3})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}+\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}Q^{\partial}\varphi^{*}\chi_{3}+

\displaystyle+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}\chi_{3}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}Q_{X}(1-\chi_{3})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{1}+\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}+\chi_{2}+(\varphi^{-1})^{*}S_{0}\varphi^{*}\chi_{3}+

\displaystyle+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}\chi_{3}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}Q_{X}(1-\chi_{3})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}+(\varphi^{-1})^{*}S_{0}\varphi^{*}\chi_{3}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}-1)Q_{X}\chi_{3}

\displaystyle+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}Q_{X}(1-\chi_{3})=1+S_{2}+(\varphi^{-1})^{*}S_{0}\varphi^{*}\chi_{3}+S_{3}+S_{4}.

Since
\chi_{1}(\chi_{1}^{\prime}-1)=0
,
S_{2}
is a smoothing operator with parameter. Similarly, since
\chi_{2}^{\prime}(1-\chi_{3})=0
,
S_{4}
is a smoothing operator with parameter. Using Proposition
5.4
and Lemma
5.21
, respectively, we conclude that
S_{3}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
and
(\varphi^{-1})^{*}S_{0}\varphi^{*}\chi_{3}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
are continuous with norms bounded by
O(R^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
. In particular,

S_{5}:=(\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime})Q_{X}-1,

satisfies that
S_{5}:\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
is continuous and
\|S_{5}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
. We conclude that
(1+S_{5})^{-1}
exists for large
R
and
\|1-(1+S_{5})^{-1}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
. We therefore have that

Q_{X}^{-1}=(1+S_{5})^{-1}(\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime})=\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}+S,

where

S:=(1-(1+S_{5})^{-1})(\chi_{1}Q_{M}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}).

Since
\|1-(1+S_{5})^{-1}\|_{\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
as
R\to\infty
, the same holds for
S
and the proof is complete.
∎

By combining Theorem
4.5
with Theorem
5.22
, we arrive at the following corollary.

Corollary 5.23
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function with property (MR) on
\Gamma
. For some
R_{0}\geq 0
and any
R\in\Gamma_{\pi/(n+1)}(R_{0})\cap\Gamma
, we can write

\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{-1}=\chi_{1}A\chi_{1}^{\prime}+\chi_{2}(\varphi^{-1})^{*}W_{+}W_{-}\varphi^{*}\chi_{2}^{\prime}+\tilde{S},

where
A
,
W_{+}
,
W_{-}
and
\chi_{1},\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(X)
are as in Theorem
5.22
and
\tilde{S}:\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)
is continuous with
\|\tilde{S}\|_{\overline{H}^{\mu}(X)\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}(X)}=O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty})
, as
\mathrm{Re}(R)\to\infty
in
\Gamma
.

6.
Conditional expectations of
Q_{X}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{-1}

A large motivation for this paper is the relation of the operator
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
with magnitude. For that purpose, we shall be interested in computing conditional expectations of
Q_{X}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{-1}
against the constant function
1
. In an accompanying paper
[
13
]
, we prove that this conditional expectation of
(R\mathcal{Z}_{X}(R))^{-1}
coincides with the magnitude function. The section is divided into three subsections: firstly, we study the case of no boundary, secondly we proceed to compute the asymptotic expansion of the conditional expectation of
Q_{X}^{-1}
and finally we produce explicit formulas for the asymptotic expansion and consider examples. As in the previous sections, we perform computations for
Q
that later translates into results for
\mathcal{Z}
under assumptions of property (MR).

6.1.
Asymptotic expansions for compact manifolds

Let us consider the case that
X=M
is a compact manifold. Starting from Lemma
2.23
we compute the asymptotics of
\langle Q(R)^{-1}1,1\rangle
as
R\to\infty
for a pseudodifferential operator with parameter
{R}
. Let
\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{d}}(M)
denote the volume of
M
in the Riemannian metric defined from the transversal Hessian of
\mathrm{d}^{2}
at the diagonal.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
whose square is regular at the diagonal. Let
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
denote the sequence of homogeneous functions obtained from restriction to
\xi=0
of the full symbol of
Q_{M}^{-1}
, as in Lemma
2.23
. It holds that

\langle 1,Q_{M}(R)^{-1}1\rangle\sim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$},

where

c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})=\int_{M}a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x.

Here
\mathrm{d}x
is the Riemannian volume density defined from
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
. The functions
a_{k,0}(x,1)
depend on the Taylor expansion (
9
) as described in Theorem
2.26
and can be computed inductively using Lemma
2.24
. In particular,

\displaystyle c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})=\begin{cases}0,\;&\mbox{when $k$ is odd,}\\
\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{d}}(M)}{n!\omega_{n}},\;&\mbox{when $k=0$},\\
\frac{n+1}{6\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{X}s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x,\;&\mbox{when $k=2$},\end{cases}

where
s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
in local coordinates is computed as the polynomial in the Taylor coefficients of
\mathrm{d}^{2}
at the diagonal given as

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}(x):=

\displaystyle 3C^{4}(x,g\otimes g)-3\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2,n}(n+5)(n^{2}-9)}{\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}}(C^{3}\otimes C^{3})(x,g\otimes g\otimes g),\quad\mbox{if $n\neq 1,3$}

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}(x):=

\displaystyle 3\bigg(10C^{4}_{G}(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G})-\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2,3}}{\mathfrak{c}_{1,3}}(C^{3}_{G}\otimes C^{3}_{G})(x,g_{G}\otimes g_{G}\otimes g_{G})\bigg),\quad\mbox{if $n=3$}

Proof.

The asymptotic expansion
\langle Q_{M}^{-1}1,1\rangle\sim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(R^{-\infty})

where
c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})=\int_{M}a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x
follows directly from Lemma
2.23
and the fact that
Q_{M}^{-1}
is of order
n+1
. It follows from Lemma
2.24
that
a_{k}(x,0,1)=0
for odd
k
. It follows from Theorem
2.26
that
c_{0}
and
c_{2}
take the prescribed form.
∎

The justification for the notation
s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
in Theorem
6.1
comes from Example
2.30
which shows that for the geodesic distance on a Riemannian manifold,
s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is the scalar curvature. We further conclude the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
M
be a compact Riemannian manifold equipped with its geodesic distance. Then

\langle 1,Q_{M}(R)^{-1}1\rangle=\frac{\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{d}}(M)}{n!\omega_{n}}R^{n+1}+\frac{n+1}{6\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{M}s\mathrm{d}xR^{n-1}+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{n-3}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$},

where
s
denotes the scalar curvature of
M
.

Combining Theorem
3.7
with Theorem
6.1
we arrive at the following corollary.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
M
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with a distance function
\mathrm{d}
with property (MR) on
\Gamma
. It holds that

\langle 1,\mathcal{Z}_{M}(R)^{-1}1\rangle\sim\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(\mathrm{Re}(R)^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $\mathrm{Re}(R)\to+\infty$ in $\Gamma$},

where
c_{k}(M,\mathrm{d})
is as in Theorem
6.1
.

6.2.
A lengthy exercise in integration by parts

To study the asymptotic expansions of
\langle 1,Q_{X}^{-1}1\rangle
in the presence of a boundary, we need a series of smaller lemmas. The reader should recall the notation from Theorem
5.22
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. It holds that

\langle Q_{X}^{-1}1,1\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}=\langle A1,\chi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}+\langle W_{-}1,(W_{+})^{*}(\chi_{2}\circ\varphi)\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))}+O(R^{-\infty}).

Proof.

We first note that
W_{-}
preserves supports in
\partial X\times(-\infty,0]\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
by Lemma
5.21
and
(W_{+})^{*}
preserves supports in
\partial X\times(-\infty,0]\subseteq\partial X\times\mathbb{R}
since
W_{+}
preserves supports in
\partial X\times[0,\infty)
by Lemma
5.21
. Therefore, viewing
1
as an element of
\overline{H}^{\mu}_{\rm loc}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
and
\chi_{2}\circ\varphi
as an element of
\overline{H}^{\mu}_{c}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
, the images
W_{-}1\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
and
(W_{+})^{*}(\chi_{2}\circ\varphi)\in L^{2}_{c}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))
are well defined and
\langle W_{-}1,(W_{+})^{*}(\chi_{2}\circ\varphi)\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))}
is well defined. By the same token,
\langle A1,\chi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}
is defined as the inner product of
\chi_{1}\in L^{2}(X)
with the restriction of
A1_{M}\in L^{2}(M)
to
X
.

Since
\chi_{j}^{\prime}|_{\mathrm{supp}(\chi_{j})}=1
, for
j=1,2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle A1,\chi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}

\displaystyle=\langle A\chi_{1}^{\prime},\chi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}+O(R^{-\infty})\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\langle W_{-}1,(W_{+})^{*}(\chi_{2}\circ\varphi)\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))}

\displaystyle=\langle W_{-}(\chi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\varphi),(W_{+})^{*}(\chi_{2}\circ\varphi)\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial X\times[0,\infty))}+O(R^{-\infty}).

The last equality follows from Proposition
5.4
. Therefore, Theorem
5.22
reduces the statement of the theorem to the property that
\langle S1,1\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}=O(R^{-\infty})
. This is clear from the property of
S
that
\|S\|_{\overline{H}^{\mu}\to\dot{H}^{-\mu}}=O(R^{-\infty})
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. Let
(a_{j,0})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M;\mathbb{C}_{+})
denote the sequence of homogeneous functions obtained from restriction to
\xi=0
of the full symbol of
A
, as in Lemma
2.23
. It holds that

\langle A1,\chi_{1}\rangle{\sim}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k,\chi_{1}}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(R^{-\infty}),

where
c_{k,\chi_{1}}(M,\mathrm{d})=\int_{X}\chi_{1}(x)a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x
and
\mathrm{d}x
denotes the Riemannian volume density defined from
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
.

Proof.

The lemma follows immediately from Lemma
2.23
since
\chi_{1}
has compact support in
X^{\circ}
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
a=a(x^{\prime},\xi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a polynomially bounded smooth function with compact support in
x^{\prime}
, and
\chi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
a real even Schwartz function. Then as
R\to+\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle a(x^{\prime},\xi)R^{n}\hat{\chi}(-R\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-Rix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\chi(0)}{\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}D^{\alpha}_{\xi=0}\left(a(x^{\prime},\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\right)\mathrm{d}xR^{-|\alpha|}+O(R^{-\infty}).

In particular, if
\chi
is locally constant near
0
, then

\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(x^{\prime},\xi)R^{n}\hat{\chi}(-R\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-Rix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=\chi(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}a(x^{\prime},0)\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty}).

Proof.

Consider the distribution
u_{R}(\xi):=R^{n}\hat{\chi}(-R\xi)
. For any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we compute that

\displaystyle(u_{R},\varphi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\chi}(-\xi)\varphi(\xi/R)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\varphi(0)}{\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{\chi}(-\xi)\xi^{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\xi R^{-|\alpha|}+O(R^{-\infty})=

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\chi(0)}{\alpha!}R^{-|\alpha|}(\delta^{\alpha},\varphi)+O(R^{-\infty})

We conclude that in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have an asymptotic expansion

u_{R}=(2\pi)^{n}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\chi(0)}{\alpha!}R^{-|\alpha|}\delta^{\alpha}+O(R^{-\infty}).

Using standard methods for oscillatory integrals, we see that the same expansion holds also for in the weak topology against polynomially bounded smooth functions.

We compute that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle a(x^{\prime},\xi)R^{n}\hat{\chi}(-R\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-Rix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(x^{\prime},\xi)u_{R}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-Rix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\chi(0)}{\alpha!}R^{-|\alpha|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}(\delta^{\alpha}_{\xi},a(x^{\prime},\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty})=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{D^{\alpha}_{x}\chi(0)}{\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}D^{\alpha}_{\xi=0}\left(a(x^{\prime},\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\right)\mathrm{d}xR^{-|\alpha|}+O(R^{-\infty}).

∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. We denote the symbols of
W_{\pm}
by
w_{\pm}
(as in Definition
5.18
and Lemma
5.18
). Then it holds that

\langle W_{-}1,W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}\rangle{\sim}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k,\chi_{2}}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(R^{-\infty}),

where

\displaystyle c_{k,\chi_{2}}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\int_{X}\chi_{2}(x)a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=|\beta|+\gamma_{n}+j+l\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{i^{|\beta|+|\gamma_{n}|}(-1)^{|\beta|+1}}{\beta^{\prime}!(\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n})!}\int_{\partial X}\partial_{x}^{\beta}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+(0,\gamma_{n})}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

Here
\mathrm{d}x
is the Riemannian volume density on
X
defined from
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
and
\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
the induced Riemannian volume density on
\partial X
.

Proof.

The computation can be reduced to one in local coordinates, so we can assume that
w_{+}
and
w_{-}
are symbols of mixed-regularity
(-\mu,0)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and up to
O(R^{-\infty})
we can treat
W_{+}
and
W_{-}
as compactly based. As such, we replace
M
by
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
X
by
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
in all computations. Let
w^{*}_{+}
denote the symbol of
W_{+}^{*}
. By the same arguments as in
[
39
, Chapter I.3]
, we have that

(32)

w_{+}^{*}(x,\xi,R)\sim\sum_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}D_{x}^{\alpha}\overline{w_{+}(x,\xi,R)},

in the sense of Definition
5.6
. We note that Equation (
32
) only identifies
w_{+}^{*}
up to
S^{\mu,-\infty}
but this suffices as symbols from
S^{\mu,-\infty}
will only contribute to the conditional expectation with
O(R^{-\infty})
.

Using that
W_{-}
and
W_{+}^{*}
preserves supports in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-}
, we can consider
\chi_{2}
as an element of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and write

\langle W_{-}1,W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}[W_{-}1](x)\overline{[W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}](x)}\mathrm{d}x,

where

W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}(x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{+}^{*}(x,\xi,R)\hat{\chi_{2}}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi,

is computed from the action of
(Q_{+}^{-1})^{*}
on
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We compute that

\displaystyle\langle W_{-}1

\displaystyle,W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{-}(x,0,R)\overline{w_{+}^{*}(x,\xi,R)\hat{\chi}_{2}(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{ix\xi}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{-}(x,0,R)\overline{w_{+}^{*}(x,\xi,R)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle{\sim}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{(2\pi)^{n}\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{-}(x,0,R)\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}D_{x}^{\alpha}w_{+}(x,\xi,R)\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty})=

\displaystyle{\sim}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}}{(2\pi)^{n}\alpha!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(w_{-}(x,0,R)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi})D_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}w_{+}(x,\xi,R)\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty})=

\displaystyle{\sim}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\!\!\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime}=\alpha^{\prime}}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}}{(2\pi)^{n}\beta^{\prime}!\gamma^{\prime}!\alpha_{n}!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}w_{-}(x,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\alpha_{n}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}w_{+}(x,\xi,R)(-i\xi^{\prime})^{\gamma^{\prime}}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty})=

\displaystyle{\sim}

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma,\beta}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}\beta!\gamma!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta}w_{-}(x,0,R)\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma+\beta}w_{+}(x,\xi,R)(-i\xi)^{\gamma}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma,\beta,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-}(x^{\prime},0,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,R)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+

\displaystyle+O(R^{-\infty}).

where

b_{\gamma,\beta,k}=\frac{i(-1)^{|\gamma_{n}|+1}\beta_{n}!}{(2\pi)^{n}\beta^{\prime}!\gamma^{\prime}!(\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n})!k!(\beta_{n}-k)!}

By the composition formula for pseudodifferential operators (see
[
39
, Chapter I.3]
), we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma,\beta}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}\beta!\gamma!}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta}w_{-}(x,0,R)\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma+\beta}w_{+}(x,\xi,R)(-i\xi)^{\gamma}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x{\sim}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\chi_{2}(x)a(x,0,R)\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty})=\langle Q_{M}^{-1}1,\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}+O(R^{-\infty}).

We can therefore continue our calculation

\displaystyle\langle W_{-}1

\displaystyle,W_{+}^{*}\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}{\sim}\langle Q_{M}^{-1}1,\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma,\beta,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-}(x^{\prime},0,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,R)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+

\displaystyle+O(R^{-\infty})

To obtain an asymptotic expansion, we expand
w_{\pm}
in its defining homogeneous expansion
w_{\pm}\sim\sum w_{\pm,l}
from Definition
5.18
(see also Lemma
5.21
). We see that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma,\beta,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-}(x^{\prime},0,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,R)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix\xi}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty}){\sim}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=|\beta|+|\gamma|+j+l,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,R)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x

Let us consider each of the terms

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=|\beta|+|\gamma|+j+l,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,R)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,R)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle=R^{2\mu-i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=|\beta|+|\gamma|+j+l,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{\beta_{n}}{b}_{\gamma,\beta,k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,\frac{\xi}{R},1)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-ix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x.

We can compute each of the terms using Lemma
6.6
which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}D_{x}^{\beta-(0,k)}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+\gamma}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,\xi,1)(i\xi)^{(\gamma^{\prime},k)}R^{n}\hat{\chi}_{2}(-R\xi)\mathrm{e}^{-Rix^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}x=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}O(R^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{if}\quad(\gamma^{\prime},k)\neq 0,\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}D_{x}^{\beta}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+(0,\gamma_{n})}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{if}\quad(\gamma^{\prime},k)=0\end{cases}

We conclude that

\displaystyle\langle Q_{-}^{-1}1

\displaystyle,(Q_{+}^{-1})^{*}\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}-\langle Q_{M}^{-1}1,\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=

\displaystyle{\sim}R^{2\mu}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=|\beta|+\gamma_{n}+j+l,\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{i(-1)^{|\gamma_{n}|+1}R^{-i}}{\beta^{\prime}!(\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n})!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}D_{x}^{\beta}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)D_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+(0,\gamma_{n})}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\mathrm{d}x+O(R^{-\infty}).

After using
D=-i\partial
, the boundary contributions have been computed.
The lemma now follows from Lemma
2.23
giving the asymptotic expansion

\langle Q_{M}^{-1}1,\chi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}{\sim}\sum_{k}R^{n+1-k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\chi_{2}(x)a_{k}(x,{0,1})\mathrm{d}x.

∎

6.3.
Asymptotic expansions for compact manifolds with boundary

We now study asymptotic expansions of
\langle 1,Q_{X}^{-1}1\rangle
for a compact manifold with boundary, and give a procedure to compute the coefficients. An important difference to the case of empty boundary is the boundary contributions: we identify the from Lemma
6.7
as follows.

Definition 6.8
.

If
X
is an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal, then we define the sequence of functions
(B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k})_{k>0}\subseteq C^{\infty}(\partial X)
by

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}(x^{\prime}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=|\beta|+\gamma_{n}+j+l\\
\gamma_{n}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{i^{|\beta|+|\gamma_{n}|}(-1)^{|\beta|+1}}{\beta^{\prime}!(\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n})!}\partial_{x}^{\beta}w_{-,j}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{x_{n}}^{\gamma_{n}-1}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta+(0,\gamma_{n})}w_{+,l}(x^{\prime},0,0,1).

For notational simplicity, we set
B_{0}:=0
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary,
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal, and
(B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k})_{k>0}\subseteq C^{\infty}(\partial X)
as in Definition
6.8
. Then for each
k>0
,
B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}
is a polynomial in
(C^{(\gamma)}_{G})_{\gamma\in\cup_{k\leq j}I_{k}}
and its derivatives contracted by the metric
g_{G}
, its contraction along the normal to the boundary, and its derivatives of total degree
j
where each
C^{(\gamma)}_{G}
,
\gamma\in I_{k}
, has degree
k
, the metric has degree zero and
x
-derivatives increase the order by
1
.

Computing from the results of Appendix
C
, we can describe the special cases
k=1
and
k=2
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
(X,\mathrm{d})
be as in Proposition
6.9
. Then

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},1}(x^{\prime})=\frac{(n+1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}\sqrt{h_{0}(x^{\prime})}}.

In particular, if
x_{n}
is the transversal coordinate defined from the unit normal to
\partial X
(in
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
), then

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},1}(x^{\prime})=\frac{(n+1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}}.

Proof.

By definition, we have that

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},1}(x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle-iw_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i(n+1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}}h_{0}(x^{\prime})(-h_{-}(x^{\prime},0,1)))=\frac{(n+1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}\sqrt{h_{0}(x)}}.

Here we have used the identities from Appendix
C
.
If
x_{n}
is the transversal coordinate defined from the unit normal to
\partial X
, then
h_{0}=1
.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
(X,\mathrm{d})
be as in Proposition
6.9
and assume that
x_{n}
is the transversal coordinate defined from the unit normal
\partial_{n}
to
\partial X
(in
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
). Then there are universal polynomials
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
with rational coefficients such that

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},2}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\alpha_{1}(n)}{n!\omega_{n}}C^{3}(x^{\prime},\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n})+\frac{\alpha_{2}(n)}{n!\omega_{n}}C^{3}(x^{\prime},g\otimes\partial_{n}).

Proof.

By definition, we have that

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},2}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{x_{n}}\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{2}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x_{n}}w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{2}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)-

\displaystyle-iw_{-,1}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)-iw_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,1}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)+

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x^{\prime}}w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\cdot\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1).

These expressions were computed in Section
C
of the appendix. If
x_{n}
is the transversal coordinate defined from the unit normal to
\partial X
, then
h_{0}=1
and
b=0
. The lemmas of Section
C
, for
h_{0}=1
and
b=0
, shows that
B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},2}(x^{\prime})
is in the linear span of
C^{3}(x^{\prime},\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n})
and
C^{3}(x^{\prime},g\otimes\partial_{n})
, and carefully inspecting the computations imply the existence of the universal polynomials
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a domain with smooth boundary equipped with the Euclidean distance. Then for a universal polynomial
\beta
, it holds that

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},2}=\frac{\beta(n)}{n!\omega_{n}}H,

where
H
denotes the mean curvature of the boundary.

Proof.

Fix a point
x_{0}\in\partial X
and choose coordinates as in Example
2.15
; in other words we write
\partial X
as the graph of a function
\varphi=\varphi(x^{\prime})
with
\nabla\varphi(x_{0})=0
. The computations in Example
2.15
show that
h_{0}(x^{\prime})=1+|\nabla\varphi(x^{\prime})|^{2}
is
x_{n}
-independent and that
b(x_{0})=\nabla\varphi(x_{0})=0
. Moreover,
C^{3}(x,v)
is a first order order polynomial in
v_{n}
, so
C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)=0
. A short computation using Equation (
13
) gives us that

C^{3}(x_{0},g\otimes\iota_{n}g)=-2g_{x_{0}}(\nabla^{2}\varphi(x_{0}))=-(n-1)H(x_{0}),

where
H
denotes the mean curvature. The result follows from Proposition
6.11
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
6.4
,
6.5
and
6.7
we arrive at the following theorem:

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function whose square is regular at the diagonal. Denote the Riemannian volume density on
X
defined from
g_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
by
\mathrm{d}x
and the induced Riemannian volume density on
\partial X
by
\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
. It holds that

(33)

\langle 1,Q_{X}^{-1}1\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}{\sim}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(R^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $R\to+\infty$},

where the coefficients
c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})
are given as

c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})=\int_{X}a_{k,0}(x,1)\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial X}B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}(x)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

where

(1)

a_{k,0}(\cdot,1)\in C^{\infty}(X)
is an invariant polynomial in the entries of the Taylor expansion (
9
) as described in Theorem
2.26
and can be computed inductively using Lemma
2.24
, with
a_{k,0}=0
if
k
is odd; and

(2)

B_{\mathrm{d}^{2},k}\in C^{\infty}(\partial X)
is an invariant polynomial in the entries of the Taylor coefficients of
\mathrm{d}^{2}
at the diagonal in
X
near
\partial X
as described in Proposition
6.9
and can be inductively computed using Lemma
5.19
.

In particular, we have that

(34)

\displaystyle c_{0}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{vol}(X)}{n!\omega_{n}},\quad c_{1}(X,\mathrm{d})=\frac{(n+1)\mathrm{vol}(\partial X)}{2n!\omega_{n}},

(35)

\displaystyle c_{2}(X,\mathrm{d})=

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{6\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{X}s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{(n-1)(n+1)^{2}}{8\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{\partial X}H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

where the scalar curvature
s_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is defined as in Theorem
6.1
and the mean curvature
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
is an explicit function in the linear span of
C^{3}(x^{\prime},\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n}\otimes\partial_{n})
and
C^{3}(x^{\prime},g\otimes\partial_{n})
that coincides with the usual mean curvature for domains in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Our notation
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}
in Theorem
6.13
is justified by Proposition
6.12
showing that
H_{\mathrm{d}^{2}}=H
is the mean curvature if
X
is a domain in Euclidean space.

Proof.

The expression in Equation (
33
) follows from Lemma
6.4
by adding together the computation of Lemma
6.5
with that in Lemma
6.7
.
The computation (
34
) follows from the fact that
a_{0,0}(x,1)=\frac{1}{n!\omega_{n}}
(see Theorem
2.26
) and Proposition
6.10
. The computation (
35
) is a consequence of Theorem
2.26
(computing the interior contribution) and Proposition
6.11
(computing the boundary contribution).
∎

Combining Theorem
4.5
with Theorem
6.13
we arrive at the following corollary.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional compact manifold with boundary and
\mathrm{d}
a distance function with property (MR) on
[R_{0},\infty)
, for some
R_{0}\geq 0
. It holds that

\langle 1,\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{-1}1\rangle_{L^{2}(X)}{\sim}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})R^{n+1-k}+O(R^{-\infty}),\quad\mbox{as $R\to+\infty$},

where the coefficients
c_{k}(X,\mathrm{d})
are as in Theorem
6.13
.

Corollary 6.15
.

If
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact domain with smooth boundary, then

\displaystyle c_{0}(X,\mathrm{d})=\frac{\mathrm{vol}(X)}{n!\omega_{n}},\quad c_{1}(X,\mathrm{d})=\frac{\mu\mathrm{vol}(\partial X)}{n!\omega_{n}},\quad c_{2}(X,\mathrm{d})=\frac{\mu^{2}(n-1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\int_{\partial X}H\mathrm{d}S.

The computation of Corollary
6.15
is compatible with the computations of
[
11
]
for
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
. We note that the precise proportionality constant in
c_{2}
follows from the computation from
[
11
]
for
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
since the pre-factor by Proposition
6.12
is determined as a universal polynomial in
n
.

Appendix A
Conormal distributions and parameter dependent calculus

Pseudodifferential operators with parameters will play an important role in our study of the operators
Q
and
\mathcal{Z}
, both to prove meromorphic extensions and to compute asymptotic expansions. We use an approach to parameter dependence described in terms of conormal distributions to which the operator
Q
is susceptible. More details on conormal distributions can be found in
[
25
, Chapter 18.2]
or
[
40
]
, or a summary thereof in the arXiv version of this paper
[
14
]
. We here review the necessary computational tools. We write
CI^{m}(Z,Y)
for the space of distributions on
Z
conormal to
Y
that admit a classical expansion near
Y
of order
m
; so that we have a symbol isomorphism
CI^{m}(Z,Y)/CI^{-\infty}(Z,Y)\cong CS^{m}(N^{*}Y)/CS^{-\infty}(N^{*}Y)
.

Take an
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and define
u_{\alpha,0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus\{0\})
by
u_{\alpha,0}(y,z):=|z|^{\alpha}
. If
\alpha\notin-m-\mathbb{N}
,
[
24
, Theorem 3.2.3]
guarantees that
u_{\alpha,0}
has a unique extension to a distribution
u_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
homogeneous of degree
\alpha
and depending holomorphically on
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-m-\mathbb{N})
. If
f=f(w)
is a function depending holomorphically on
w
in a punctured neighborhood of
\alpha
, we write
\mathrm{F.P.}_{w=\alpha}f
for the constant coefficient in the Laurent expansion of
f
around
w=\alpha
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, we use the notation
\mathrm{F.P.}|z|^{-\alpha}
to denote the distribution

\langle\mathrm{F.P.}|z|^{-\alpha},\varphi\rangle:=\mathrm{F.P.}_{w=\alpha}\langle u_{w},\varphi\rangle.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
\mathrm{F.P.}|z|^{\alpha}
is a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and for
\xi\neq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\mathrm{F.P.}|z|^{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\pi^{m/2}2^{\alpha+m}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha+m}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}|\xi|^{-m-\alpha},\quad&\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(-m-2\mathbb{N}),\\
\frac{\pi^{m/2}(-1)^{l}}{2^{2l}l!\Gamma\left(\frac{m}{2}+l\right)}|\xi|^{2l}(-\log|\xi|^{2}+\beta_{l,m}),\quad&\alpha=-m-2l,\;l\in\mathbb{N},\end{cases}

where

\beta_{l,m}:=2\log(2)+\frac{1}{2}\psi(m/2+l)-H_{l}-\gamma,

and
\psi(z):=\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(z)}{\Gamma(z)}
,
H_{0}=0
and
H_{l}:=\sum_{j=1}^{l}\frac{1}{j}
for
l>0
, and
\gamma
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

This computation can be found in
[
37
, Lemma 25.2]
par the value of
\beta_{l,m}
, which can be found from a Laurent expansion. See also
[
38
, Exemple 5, Chapitre VII.7]
and
[
14
]
.

For
g\in C^{\infty}(Z)
and a submanifold
Y\subseteq Z
with a prescribed tubular neighborhood
U\cong NY
, we define the transversal Hessian of
g
in
y\in Y
as the symmetric bilinear form on
(NY)_{y}
defined from the Hessian at the zero section of
g
restricted to
(NY)_{y}
along the tubular neighborhood.

Proposition A.2
.

Assume that
Y\subseteq Z
is an
k
-dimensional smooth submanifold of an
N
-dimensional smooth manifold. Let
\tilde{G}\in C^{\infty}(Z)
be a smooth function such that

•

\tilde{G}
and
\mathrm{d}\tilde{G}
vanishes on
Y
;

•

\tilde{G}(x)>0
for
x\notin Y
; and

•

for each
y\in Y
, the transversal Hessian
H_{\tilde{G}}
of
\tilde{G}
(defined as in Definition
2.1
) is a positive definite quadratic form on the transversal tangent bundle of
Y\subseteq Z
.

Then
\log\tilde{G}\in CI^{-N+k}(Z;Y)
and its principal symbol
\overline{\sigma}_{-N+k}\left(\log\tilde{G}\right)\in C^{\infty}(N^{*}Y\setminus Y)
is given by

\overline{\sigma}_{-N+k}\left(\log\tilde{G}\right)(y,\xi)=-2\pi(N-k-1)!\omega_{N-k-1}|g_{\tilde{G}}(\xi,\xi)|^{-(N-k)/2},\quad\xi\neq 0,

where
g_{\tilde{G}}
is the metric dual to the transversal Hessian
H_{\tilde{G}}
.

Proof.

The statement is local, so we can assume that there is an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
containing
0
such that
Z=U
and
Y=U\cap\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Since
\mathrm{d}g
vanishes on
TY
, we can consider its restriction to
Y
to be a section
\mathrm{d}g|_{Y}:Y\to N^{*}Y
. Under the assumption that the transversal Hessian of
g
is non-degenerate in all points of
Y
, we can assume that
U
is taken small enough to be able to choose coordinates
(\tilde{y},\tilde{z})
on
U
such that
g(\tilde{y},\tilde{z})=|\tilde{z}|^{2}
for
\tilde{z}\neq 0
. For notational simplicity, we assume that
g(y,z)=|z|^{2}
and that
Z=U=\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
Y=\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The proposition now follows from Proposition
A.1
.
∎

This work makes heavy use of parameter dependent pseudodifferential operators. This subject is well explained in
[
39
]
. We only require classical symbols with parameters. For clarity, for a conical set
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and
m\in\mathbb{C}
, a classical symbol with parameter of order
m
is a symbol with parameter
a
that admits a sequence
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions homogeneous of degree
m-j
(
a_{j}(x,t\xi,tR)=t^{m-j}a_{j}(x,\xi,R)
for
t>0
) such that
a\sim\sum_{j}a_{j}
away from
(\xi,R)=0
. We write
CS^{m}
for the space of classical symbols with parameter of order
m
and
\Psi^{m}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)
for the space of classical pseudodifferential operators with parameter
R\in\Gamma
of order
m
. An important feature of the parameter dependent calculus is that there is a Gårding equality, we omit its proof as it extends ad verbatim from the classical setting
[
25
]
.

Theorem A.3

(Gårding inequality)
.

Let
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma_{\alpha}(0)\cup-\Gamma_{\alpha}(0)
be a bisector with opening angle
<\alpha\in[0,\pi/2)
, and
A\in\Psi^{m}_{\rm cl}(M;\Gamma)
a formally self-adjoint operator with strictly positive principal symbol, i.e. for some
\varepsilon>0
,
\overline{\sigma}(A)(x,\xi,R)\geq\varepsilon
for all
|\xi|^{2}+|R|^{2}=1
and
x\in M
.

Then for large
R
, the quadratic form
f\mapsto\langle Af,f\rangle_{L^{2}}
is continuous, positive and coercive on
H^{\mathrm{Re}(m)/2}_{|R|}
. To be precise, there is an
R_{0}>0
and a
C>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}\|f\|_{H^{\mathrm{Re}(m)/2}_{|R|}}\leq\langle Af,f\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{\mathrm{Re}(m)}_{|R|}},\quad\forall f\in H^{s}(M),\;|R|>R_{0}.

One of the reasons for introducing conormal distributions above was that it will give us a direct way of verifying that the magnitude operator is an elliptic pseudodifferential operator with parameter. We let
Z=U\times\mathbb{R}\subseteq M\times M\times\mathbb{R}
denote an
\mathbb{R}
-invariant tubular neighborhood of the diagonal of
M
. Assume that
K\in CI^{m}(Z;M\times\{0\})
in exponential coordinates has a classical asymptotic expansion of the form
K\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\chi K_{j}
where
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
is a function with
\chi=1
near the diagonal and
K_{j}
is a smooth function on
TM\oplus\mathbb{R}\setminus(M\times\{0\})
such that if
m\notin\mathbb{Z}
,
K_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
-m-j-n-1
. If
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
K_{j}=u_{j}+p_{j}\log(|v|^{2}+\eta^{2})
where
u_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
-m-j-n-1
and
p_{j}
is a homogeneous polynomial in
(v,\eta)
of degree
-m-j-n-1
(in particular
p_{j}=0
if
j<-m-n-1
).

Definition A.4
.

We shall say that
K\in CI^{m}(Z;M\times\{0\})
has a
uniform asymptotic expansion
if for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C>0
and an
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
R\neq 0

\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}\partial_{\eta}^{k}\left(K-\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}\chi K_{j}\right)\right|\leq C(1+|v|+|\eta|)^{-N}.

For
x\in M
, we write
\exp_{x}:T_{x}M\to M
for the exponential map. Note that under our assumption on the injectivity radius,
\exp_{x}:B_{x}M\to M
is a diffeomorphism onto its range.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
M
be a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold and
Z
is as in the preceding paragraphs. Assume that
K\in CI^{m}(Z;M\times\{0\})
admits a uniform asymptotic expansion. Then there is a pseudodifferential operator with parameter
A\in\Psi^{m}(M;\mathbb{R})
such that for any
R
, the Schwartz kernel of
A(R)
is given by
\mathcal{F}_{\eta\to R}K
. In other words, for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,
A(R)f
is defined as the oscillatory integral

A(R)f(x):=\int_{B_{x}M\oplus\mathbb{R}}K(x,v,\eta)f(\exp_{x}(v))\mathrm{e}^{i\eta R}\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}\eta.

In particular, the full symbol of
A
in
CS^{m}(M;\mathbb{R})/S^{-\infty}(M;\mathbb{R})
coincides with the full symbol of
K
in
CS^{m}(N^{*}(\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\}\subseteq Z))/S^{-\infty}(N^{*}(\mathrm{Diag}_{M}\times\{0\}\subseteq Z))
.

Proof.

If the uniform asymptotic expansion of
K
only contains one term, i.e.
K=K_{0}
, the statement of the proposition holds by homogeneity properties of the Fourier transform. As such, the proposition is in fact a statement concerning the asymptotic completeness of the space of pseudodifferential operators with parameters. This is proven just as in the usual setting (see
[
25
, Proposition 18.1.3]
).
∎

Appendix B
Partial fraction decompositions of symbols

We note the following structural result from basic calculus:

Lemma B.1
.

For all
l,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<2l
, there exists homogeneous rational functions (with rational coefficients) of
(h_{+},h_{-})
denoted by
\beta_{l,m,0,\pm},\beta_{l,m,1,\pm},\ldots,\beta_{l,m,l-1,\pm}
, where each
\beta_{l,m,j,\pm}=b_{l,m,j,\pm}(h_{+},h_{-})
has homogeneous degree
m-j-l
in
(h_{+},h_{-})
, such that

\displaystyle\xi_{n}^{m}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-l}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l}=\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\beta_{l,m,j,+}(h_{+},h_{-})(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{j-l}+\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\beta_{l,m,j,-}(h_{+},h_{-})(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{j-l}.

More generally, by differentiating with respect to
h_{\pm}
we obtain:

Lemma B.2
.

For all
l,m\in\mathbb{N}
and two distinct complex numbers
h_{+},h_{-}\in\mathbb{C}
, consider the rational function:

K_{m,l}(\xi_{n}):=(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-m}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l}.

This rational function can be decomposed as

\displaystyle K_{m,l}(\xi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{l+j}}\binom{l+j-1}{j}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-m+j}+\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(h_{+}-h_{-})^{m+j}}\binom{m+j-1}{j}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l+j}\ .

These following formulas allow us to obtain explicit partial fraction decompositions for the terms relevant to the factorization of
\mathcal{Z}_{R}
.

Corollary B.3
.

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and two distinct complex numbers
h_{+},h_{-}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle\xi_{n}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-l}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l}=

\displaystyle K_{l-1,l}(\xi_{n})+h_{+}K_{l,l}(\xi_{n})\ ,

\displaystyle\xi_{n}^{2}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-l}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l}=

\displaystyle K_{l-1,l-1}(\xi_{n})+h_{-}K_{l-1,l}(\xi_{n})+h_{+}K_{l,l-1}(\xi_{n})+h_{+}h_{-}K_{l,l}(\xi_{n})\ ,

\displaystyle\xi_{n}^{3}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})^{-l}(\xi_{n}-h_{-})^{-l}=

\displaystyle K_{l-2,l-1}(\xi_{n})+(2h_{+}+h_{-})K_{l-1,l-1}(\xi_{n})+

\displaystyle+(h_{-}^{2}+h_{+}^{2}+h_{+}h_{-})K_{l-1,l}(\xi_{n})+h_{+}^{2}h_{-}K_{l,l}(\xi_{n})

Appendix C
Evaluation of some boundary symbols at zero

For the purpose of computations in Subsection
6.3
, we are interested in evaluating some symbols at
\xi=0
and
R=1
. We will use the notations from the Sections
5
and
6
freely. We tacitly assume that
n>1
to avoid limit cases.

Recall from Proposition
5.13
that

R^{2}+g(\xi,\xi)=h_{0}(\xi_{n}-h_{+})(\xi_{n}-h_{-}),

where
h_{\pm}=h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)\in S^{1}(T^{*}Y\times\mathbb{R},Y\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})
are of the form

h_{\pm}(x,\xi^{\prime},R)=-\frac{\xi^{\prime}(b(x))}{h_{0}(x)}\pm i\frac{\sqrt{R^{2}+g_{Y}(\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})-(\xi^{\prime}(b))^{2}}}{\sqrt{h_{0}(x)}}.

Here we use the splitting of the metric

g=\begin{pmatrix}h_{0}&b\\
b^{T}&g_{Y}\end{pmatrix},

For
x_{n}=0
, i.e. on
\partial X
, we write
x^{\prime}
instead of
(x^{\prime},0)
. We conclude the following lemma from elementary computations, which are included in
[
14
]
.
We use the notation
\mu=\frac{n+1}{2}
.

Lemma C.1
.

The following identities hold on
\partial X
:

\displaystyle w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{x_{n}}\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{2}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu(\mu-1)(\mu-2)}{2n!\omega_{n}}\partial_{x_{n}}h_{0}(x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{n}}w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}^{2}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mu^{2}(\mu-1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\partial_{x_{n}}h_{0}(x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\nabla_{x^{\prime}}w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\cdot\nabla_{\xi^{\prime}}\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mu^{2}(\mu-1)}{2\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\frac{b(x^{\prime})}{h_{0}(x^{\prime})}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{0}(x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle w_{-,1}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-99.58464pt=\frac{i\mu\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\bigg(\frac{3}{2}C^{3}(x^{\prime},g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+\frac{17(n+3)}{4h_{0}(x)}C^{3}(x^{\prime},\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)\bigg)-

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt-\frac{7i\mu^{3}}{4\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\bigg(\partial_{x_{n}}h_{0}(x^{\prime})+\frac{b(x^{\prime})}{h_{0}(x^{\prime})}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{0}(x^{\prime})\bigg),

\displaystyle w_{-,0}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)\partial_{\xi_{n}}w_{+,1}(x^{\prime},0,0,1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-99.58464pt=\frac{i\mathfrak{c}_{1,n}(n^{2}-1)}{(n!\omega_{n})^{2}}\bigg(-\frac{3(\mu-1)}{2}C^{3}(x^{\prime},g\otimes\iota_{n}g)+\frac{\mu(n+3)}{4h_{0}(x^{\prime})}C^{3}(x,\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g\otimes\iota_{n}g)\bigg)+

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\frac{i\mu^{2}(3\mu-5)}{4\cdot n!\omega_{n}}\bigg(\partial_{x_{n}}h_{0}(x^{\prime})+\frac{b(x^{\prime})}{h_{0}(x^{\prime})}\cdot\nabla_{x^{\prime}}h_{0}(x^{\prime})\bigg).
